Table 1
Sampling Bias, Representative Samples, and Reliable Statistical Inference
1. Q: Why can a reported average income of $25,111 for a graduating class be misleading even when it is calculated correctly from the responses received?
A: Show answer
A mathematically correct calculation can still describe the wrong group. If the figure is calculated only from alumni whose addresses can be found and who choose to answer a personal income questionnaire, the respondents may differ systematically from classmates who are missing or decline to respond. Alumni with successful careers may be easier to locate and more willing to report their earnings, while people with low incomes, unstable circumstances, or less connection to the university may be underrepresented. The result may accurately describe the respondents but not the entire graduating class. The number's precision does not establish that the sample represents the population. Before interpreting an average, examine who was eligible, who could be contacted, who responded, how values were reported, and whether the observed respondents differ from those not represented.
Study instruction:
- Separate the arithmetic accuracy of a statistic from the representativeness of the data used to calculate it.
Key point:
- A correct calculation from a biased sample can produce a misleading population conclusion.
- Always ask which people the reported average actually describes.
Source links:
- AAPOR Standards and Ethics
- Statistics Canada: Non-sampling Error
2. Q: What is sampling, and why is it used instead of measuring every member of a population?
A: Show answer
Sampling is the process of selecting a subset of units from a defined population to learn about that population. A complete count, or census, may be impractical when the population is large, dispersed, changing, expensive to contact, or difficult to measure. A properly designed sample can provide useful estimates with less time and cost. The logic is comparable to inspecting a handful of beans from a barrel to estimate the proportions of different colors: the handful is informative only if it is selected in a way that reflects the barrel. Sampling is not simply taking whichever observations are easiest to obtain. The population, sampling frame, selection procedure, response process, and measurement method must all be considered. Sampling reduces the number of observations required; it does not remove the need for careful design or quality control.
Study instruction:
- Define a target population for a practical study and explain why a sample may be more feasible than a census.
Key point:
- Sampling uses a subset to estimate characteristics of a larger defined group.
- The value of a sample depends on how it is selected and measured.
Source links:
- AAPOR Standards and Ethics
3. Q: What is the difference between a population, a sampling frame, a sample, and respondents?
A: Show answer
The population is the complete group about which the researcher wants to draw conclusions. The sampling frame is the operational list or mechanism from which units can be selected, such as an alumni register, address database, telephone frame, or list of registered organizations. The sample is the set of units selected or invited from that frame. Respondents are the sampled units that provide usable information. These groups may differ substantially. For example, a study of all graduates may use an alumni database that omits people with outdated contact details; the selected sample may include only reachable alumni; and the final respondent group may consist only of those willing to disclose income. A conclusion about all graduates therefore requires evidence that coverage, selection, and response have not created material distortions. Clearly naming each group prevents a report from treating respondents as though they automatically equal the target population.
Study instruction:
- Draw the sequence from target population to frame, selected sample, and respondents for a survey you know.
Key point:
- The population, frame, sample, and respondent group are not interchangeable.
- Every transition can introduce exclusions or differences that affect inference.
Source links:
- AAPOR Standards and Ethics
- AAPOR Disclosure Standards
4. Q: What is sampling bias, and how does it distort a statistical conclusion?
A: Show answer
Sampling bias occurs when the selection or participation process systematically favors some members or characteristics over others, so the observed sample differs from the target population in ways relevant to the estimate. If higher-income alumni are easier to locate and more likely to reply, their earnings may be overrepresented and the estimated class income may be too high. If a public-opinion poll recruits mostly people who are highly engaged online, it may overstate the views of people who are less connected or less active. Bias is systematic rather than merely random fluctuation. Increasing the number of observations from the same biased process can make the estimate more precise around the wrong value. The direction and size of bias depend on which groups are underrepresented or overrepresented and how their values differ on the subject being measured.
Study instruction:
- Identify the mechanism that favors one group and explain how that group's characteristics could affect the estimate.
Key point:
- Bias is a systematic distortion, not simply a small sample size.
- A larger sample does not automatically correct a biased selection process.
Source links:
- Statistics Canada: Non-sampling Error
- U.S. Census Bureau: Nonsampling Error
5. Q: Why is a sample only as reliable as the process used to select it?
A: Show answer
The selection process determines which members of the population have an opportunity to contribute information. If the process systematically excludes people with particular characteristics, the sample cannot reveal their experiences reliably unless the design or later adjustments address the omission. A sophisticated statistical calculation cannot reconstruct information that was never collected without additional assumptions or external data. In the Yale alumni example, people whose addresses were unknown and those who did not answer were not merely missing at random by definition; their circumstances could be related to income. The same issue arises when researchers survey people at a location that excludes non-visitors or recruit participants through a channel used disproportionately by one demographic group. Reliability therefore begins with defining the population and building a defensible sampling frame, not with selecting a formula after data collection.
Study instruction:
- Review a study's recruitment procedure and list the population members who could not realistically enter the sample.
Key point:
- Statistical processing cannot automatically repair information excluded by the design.
- Selection procedures must be evaluated before results are interpreted.
Source links:
- AAPOR Disclosure Standards
- Statistics Canada: Non-sampling Error
6. Q: What is a representative sample?
A: Show answer
A representative sample provides a sufficiently accurate reflection of the target population for the characteristics and estimates being studied. Representativeness is not established merely because the sample is large, geographically diverse, or resembles the population on one visible characteristic. It depends on coverage, selection probabilities, response patterns, measurement quality, and the relationship between sample composition and the variables of interest. A sample may match population age and sex distributions but still differ in income, political engagement, health status, or other relevant dimensions. It is therefore more useful to ask whether the sample supports a particular inference than to declare it universally representative. Researchers should describe the design, compare available sample and population benchmarks, investigate nonresponse, and state limitations that remain.
Study instruction:
- Choose two variables relevant to a research question and explain how you would assess whether respondents reflect the target population on them.
Key point:
- Representativeness is relative to a defined population and the estimates being made.
- Similarity on a few demographic variables does not prove the absence of bias.
Source links:
- AAPOR Standards and Ethics
- U.S. Census Bureau: Response Rates Definitions
7. Q: How can nonresponse create bias in an otherwise well-designed survey?
A: Show answer
Nonresponse occurs when selected eligible units do not provide the required information. It creates bias when respondents and nonrespondents differ in characteristics related to the survey estimates. In an alumni income survey, graduates who are unemployed or financially struggling may be less willing to answer, while highly successful graduates may be easier to locate or more inclined to participate. The resulting respondent group can therefore overstate average income. Nonresponse may result from refusal, inability to locate a person, absence, language barriers, lack of time, or insufficient answers. A high response rate does not guarantee that bias is absent, and a low response rate does not by itself prove that a particular estimate is biased; the key question is whether response propensity is associated with the measured outcome. Researchers should document response dispositions and assess differences using available auxiliary information.
Study instruction:
- For a hypothetical survey, identify likely reasons for nonresponse and the variables that may distinguish respondents from nonrespondents.
Key point:
- Nonresponse is especially concerning when response is related to the topic being measured.
- Examine differences, not only the overall response percentage.
Source links:
- U.S. Census Bureau: Response Rates Definitions
- BLS: CPS Response Rates
8. Q: Why can a very low questionnaire response rate make an income estimate particularly questionable?
A: Show answer
A low response rate means that the final estimate is based on a small fraction of the people initially selected. The estimate becomes questionable when the people who respond have systematically different incomes from those who do not. Income is sensitive information: respondents may exaggerate, understate, omit investment income, misunderstand the requested period, or decline because they consider the question intrusive. If a small group of unusually prosperous or unusually engaged people replies, their answers may not describe the wider group. A low response rate is therefore a warning that should trigger investigation, not an automatic mathematical verdict. Researchers should examine who was reached, refusal and non-contact patterns, follow-up results, response differences across subgroups, and whether weighting or other adjustments are justified by credible auxiliary data.
Study instruction:
- Design a nonresponse assessment for a sensitive income questionnaire, including contact attempts and comparison variables.
Key point:
- Low participation can conceal important differences between respondents and nonrespondents.
- The response rate must be interpreted alongside evidence about who did and did not respond.
Source links:
- BLS: CPS Response Rates
- U.S. Census Bureau: Response Rates Definitions
9. Q: What is coverage error, and how did it affect the Yale alumni example?
A: Show answer
Coverage error occurs when the sampling frame fails to include some members of the target population, includes ineligible units, duplicates units, or classifies units incorrectly. In the alumni example, graduates whose addresses were unknown could not be contacted through the available alumni records. If those missing graduates differed economically from the people who could be located, the frame undercovered a group relevant to the income estimate. The omission is consequential even before any questionnaire is sent. Coverage error can arise from outdated registers, incomplete address lists, exclusion of people without internet access, or failure to include informal workers in a business survey. Researchers should define the target population precisely, evaluate the frame's completeness, update contact information where feasible, and explain uncovered groups. A response adjustment cannot fully fix undercoverage if no reliable information exists about omitted members.
Study instruction:
- Identify likely omissions, duplicates, and outdated records in a sampling frame and propose a frame-improvement procedure.
Key point:
- A sample cannot represent people who had no realistic chance of entering the frame.
- Frame coverage should be checked before recruitment begins.
Source links:
- Statistics Canada: Non-sampling Error
- AAPOR Disclosure Standards
10. Q: How can self-selection or volunteer participation distort survey findings?
A: Show answer
Self-selection occurs when people decide for themselves whether to join a study rather than being selected through a design that gives population members known selection probabilities. Volunteers may have stronger opinions, greater interest, more available time, or more experience with the subject than people who do not volunteer. An online poll asking readers to click a link may attract people who are unusually enthusiastic or dissatisfied. The resulting percentage describes the people who chose to participate, not necessarily the broader public. Self-selection can also occur after a probability sample is drawn if participation is related to the topic. Researchers using volunteer or opt-in samples should disclose the recruitment method, avoid presenting unqualified population margins of error, and explain the modeling assumptions used if they attempt to generalize beyond participants.
Study instruction:
- Compare a voluntary website poll with a probability sample and identify the different assumptions required for population inference.
Key point:
- Volunteers can differ systematically from people who do not participate.
- Opt-in results require transparent limits and careful analytical assumptions.
Source links:
- AAPOR Standards and Ethics
- AAPOR Disclosure Standards
11. Q: What is response bias, and how can it affect self-reported information?
A: Show answer
Response bias occurs when answers systematically differ from the values or behaviors the survey intends to measure. Respondents may exaggerate income, minimize tax-related information, overstate socially approved behavior, misremember events, or interpret questions differently. In the chapter's magazine example, respondents reported reading prestigious publications more often than circulation evidence suggested, illustrating how answers can reflect image management rather than actual behavior. A survey about tooth-brushing may similarly elicit socially desirable answers. Response bias is distinct from sampling bias: the right people may be contacted, but their answers may still be inaccurate. Researchers can reduce it through neutral wording, confidentiality protections, appropriate recall periods, careful interviewer training, and, where suitable, validation against independent records or observable behavior.
Study instruction:
- Identify a sensitive or socially desirable behavior and propose two ways to improve the accuracy of self-reports.
Key point:
- Respondents may report what sounds acceptable rather than what actually happened.
- Accurate sampling does not guarantee accurate answers.
Source links:
- AAPOR: Best Practices for Survey Research
- Statistics Canada: Non-sampling Error
12. Q: What is social desirability bias, and why is it likely in questions about income, health, or personal behavior?
A: Show answer
Social desirability bias occurs when respondents give answers they believe will be viewed favorably or will avoid embarrassment, criticism, or disapproval. A person may overstate income to appear successful, claim to exercise regularly, underreport alcohol consumption, or give a politically acceptable response to an interviewer. The effect can be especially strong when the question is sensitive, the respondent doubts confidentiality, or the interviewer is perceived as an authority figure. The resulting data may measure the respondent's preferred self-presentation rather than the underlying behavior or characteristic. Researchers should explain confidentiality accurately, use neutral language, consider private self-administration, avoid unnecessary identifying details, and use administrative or behavioral validation when ethical and feasible. No method guarantees truthful reporting, so residual measurement limitations should be disclosed.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite a leading or judgmental question about a sensitive behavior in neutral, non-stigmatizing language.
Key point:
- Social pressure can systematically change what respondents report.
- Privacy, neutral wording, and appropriate validation can reduceâbut not eliminateâthe risk.
Source links:
- AAPOR: Best Practices for Survey Research
13. Q: Why should researchers question a statistic that reports an unusually precise average, such as an income figure stated to the nearest dollar?
A: Show answer
Numerical precision describes the format or resolution of a reported number; it does not establish measurement accuracy or representativeness. An average stated to the nearest dollar may have been produced by a spreadsheet or calculator, but the underlying income reports may be rounded, incomplete, inaccurate, or drawn from a biased group. The number of displayed digits can create an unwarranted impression of scientific certainty. Researchers should ask how the data were collected, whether income components were measured consistently, whether the reporting period was clear, whether nonrespondents differed, and how uncertainty was calculated. A precise estimate may be appropriate when the data and method support it, but presentation should not imply more certainty than the design justifies. Report uncertainty and limitations in language proportionate to the evidence.
Study instruction:
- Review a published statistic and distinguish numerical precision, measurement accuracy, sampling uncertainty, and possible bias.
Key point:
- More decimal places do not make an estimate more trustworthy.
- The method and data quality determine how much confidence a number deserves.
Source links:
- Statistics Canada: Non-sampling Error
- U.S. Census Bureau: Methodology and Nonsampling Error
14. Q: Why is increasing sample size not a sufficient solution to sampling bias?
A: Show answer
Increasing sample size generally reduces random sampling variability when the design and assumptions are appropriate, but it does not automatically remove systematic selection bias. If an online survey attracts mainly high-income respondents, collecting ten thousand additional responses through the same channel may produce a more stable estimate of that self-selected group while leaving the target population underrepresented. A larger sample can make a biased estimate appear more authoritative because its random error may be small. The remedy must address the source of bias: improve frame coverage, use defensible selection methods, pursue nonrespondents, adjust weights where justified, or narrow the population claim. Sample size and sample quality are separate design questions. Both matter, but they solve different problems.
Study instruction:
- Explain why doubling the number of respondents from a biased recruitment channel may not correct the estimate.
Key point:
- Sample size primarily affects random variability, not systematic bias.
- Correct the selection mechanism rather than relying on volume alone.
Source links:
- Statistics Canada: Non-sampling Error
- U.S. Census Bureau: Methodology and Nonsampling Error
15. Q: What is the difference between sampling error and non-sampling error?
A: Show answer
Sampling error is the random difference between a sample estimate and the corresponding population value that arises because only part of the population is observed. It can be quantified for probability samples using design-appropriate measures such as standard errors. Non-sampling error includes other problems, such as coverage omissions, nonresponse, inaccurate answers, ambiguous questions, interviewer effects, coding mistakes, and processing failures. These errors can occur in both sample surveys and censuses. Sampling error is not the same as bias: a sample can vary randomly around the population value, while systematic non-sampling errors can shift estimates in a consistent direction. A reported margin of error generally does not capture all non-sampling problems. Good reporting therefore explains both statistical uncertainty and the quality risks arising from collection and processing.
Study instruction:
- Classify five survey problems as sampling error or non-sampling error and explain whether each can occur in a census.
Key point:
- A margin of error does not measure every source of survey error.
- Non-sampling errors can affect censuses as well as sample surveys.
Source links:
- Statistics Canada: Non-sampling Error
- U.S. Census Bureau: Nonsampling Error
16. Q: What is a random sample, and what makes the selection procedure genuinely random?
A: Show answer
A random sample is selected using a chance-based mechanism in which each eligible population unit has a known, non-zero probability of selection. In a simple random sample, every unit has an equal probability of selection. A researcher might use a properly randomized list of all eligible alumni or select numbered records using a random-number generator. Merely choosing people who appear varied, interviewing passers-by, or asking colleagues to nominate participants is not equivalent to probability sampling. The sampling frame must be sufficiently complete, and the random procedure must be implemented as designed. In complex designs, probabilities may differ across units but still be known and incorporated into analysis. Random selection supports statistical inference; it does not guarantee that selected people will respond or provide accurate answers.
Study instruction:
- Design a simple random selection procedure from a complete register and state the population and unit of selection.
Key point:
- Random selection requires a chance-based method, not subjective judgment.
- Known selection probabilities support design-based inference.
Source links:
- AAPOR Standards and Ethics
- AAPOR Disclosure Standards
17. Q: Why does every member of the target population need a fair chance of selection in a simple random sample?
A: Show answer
The selection mechanism must give each eligible unit the same probability of entering a simple random sample so that the design does not favor particular members before data collection. If a list omits people without current addresses, the chance of selection is zero for those people, and the sample is not representative of the full target population even if the remaining names are drawn randomly. Equal probability within an incomplete frame does not solve frame undercoverage. Researchers should therefore distinguish fairness of selection within the frame from completeness of the frame itself. For designs with unequal probabilities, such as oversampling smaller subgroups, the probabilities should be known and reflected in weighting and variance estimation. The objective is not always identical selection probability; it is a defensible design that supports the intended inference.
Study instruction:
- Identify whether equal selection probabilities are appropriate for a given study or whether a stratified design with unequal probabilities is needed.
Key point:
- Random selection within a defective frame can still exclude important groups.
- Frame coverage and selection probability must be assessed separately.
Source links:
- AAPOR Disclosure Standards
18. Q: Why is a purely random sample often difficult or expensive to obtain in practice?
A: Show answer
A probability sample requires a usable frame, a method to select units, and a practical way to contact them. For large or dispersed populations, building and maintaining a complete list can be costly. Contact attempts may require multiple modes, language support, travel, follow-up, and secure handling of personal information. Some people may have no stable address or may be difficult to reach at standard times. The chapter notes that the theoretical appeal of random selection can be constrained by cost and operational feasibility. Researchers may therefore use stratified or other probability designs to improve efficiency, or carefully designed non-probability methods when probability sampling is not feasible. Any alternative must be described honestly, with limitations and assumptions made explicit rather than hidden behind a claim of randomness.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a cost and feasibility comparison for a national household survey using a complete address frame versus a convenience-based recruitment approach.
Key point:
- A statistically desirable design must also be operationally achievable.
- Cost constraints do not justify describing a non-random sample as random.
Source links:
- AAPOR Standards and Ethics
19. Q: What is stratified random sampling, and why might researchers use it?
A: Show answer
Stratified random sampling divides the target population into defined subgroups, or strata, and selects a random sample from each stratum. The strata may be based on characteristics such as geographic region, age group, organization size, or another variable known for the population. This approach can ensure that important subgroups are represented and may improve precision when the strata are internally more homogeneous on the outcome than the population as a whole. It can also support reliable subgroup estimates. However, it depends on accurate information about the population and clear rules for assigning units to strata. If the classifications are wrong or outdated, the design may introduce coverage or classification problems. Selection within each stratum must still follow a documented random procedure, and unequal selection probabilities may require weighting.
Study instruction:
- Create three plausible strata for a study and explain how units would be randomly selected within each one.
Key point:
- Stratification organizes the population into relevant groups before random selection.
- Accurate classifications and correct weighting are essential when selection rates differ.
Source links:
- AAPOR Standards and Ethics
20. Q: How can inaccurate information about subgroup proportions undermine a stratified sample?
A: Show answer
Stratified sampling often relies on information about the size or prevalence of each subgroup to set selection targets or weights. If the information is wrong, the sample allocation may not reflect the actual population. For example, if a researcher believes a region contains 20 percent of the population when it actually contains 30 percent, a proportionate allocation based on the incorrect figure will underrepresent that region. Misclassification can also occur when categories are ambiguous, such as deciding whether a person who farms part-time and works in a city should be classified as a farmer or an urban worker. The problem is not solved by calling the design stratified. Researchers should verify the source and date of population benchmarks, define mutually exclusive classification rules where possible, document uncertainty, and use suitable calibration or weighting only when the necessary data support it.
Study instruction:
- Review a proposed stratification scheme and identify which population counts and classification rules require validation.
Key point:
- Stratification is only as sound as the data used to define its groups.
- Incorrect subgroup proportions can distort allocation and population estimates.
Source links:
- AAPOR Disclosure Standards
- Statistics Canada: Non-sampling Error
21. Q: How can interviewer judgment introduce bias into a supposedly structured stratified sample?
A: Show answer
Interviewer judgment can influence which eligible people are approached, even when the study has quotas for age, region, or other characteristics. An interviewer may approach someone who appears friendly, cooperative, well dressed, or easy to interview rather than selecting the next eligible person according to a prescribed random procedure. Across many interviewers, these small discretionary choices can systematically favor people who are more visible, available, or socially approachable. The chapter's street-interview illustration shows how appearance and perceived willingness can influence whom an interviewer selects. To reduce this risk, researchers should provide clear within-stratum selection rules, train and supervise interviewers, monitor contact and disposition records, and conduct quality checks. Quotas alone do not create random selection within each subgroup.
Study instruction:
- Write an interviewer protocol that specifies how to select an eligible respondent when several people are present.
Key point:
- Quota completion does not prove that respondents were randomly selected.
- Interviewer discretion must be constrained and monitored.
Source links:
- AAPOR: Best Practices for Survey Research
22. Q: Why can interviewing people in a public place produce a biased sample even if the researcher approaches every tenth passer-by?
A: Show answer
Systematic selection of every tenth passer-by may be orderly, but it represents only the people who pass that location at the selected times. It does not automatically represent the whole city, country, or wider population. People who work nearby, commute through the area, shop there, or have particular schedules may be overrepresented, while people who stay at home, work elsewhere, have mobility limitations, or do not use that public space may be excluded. The method may be suitable if the target population is explicitly defined as people passing that location during specified periods. It is not sufficient for broad population claims unless the design accounts for the wider population and selection probabilities. Location, time, and access are part of the sampling frame and should be disclosed.
Study instruction:
- Define the exact population represented by a street-intercept survey and list groups likely to be missed by its location and timing.
Key point:
- A systematic interval does not compensate for an inappropriate sampling frame.
- The place and time of recruitment define who can be selected.
Source links:
- AAPOR Disclosure Standards
- Statistics Canada: Non-sampling Error
23. Q: How can the time of day and contact method affect who responds to a survey?
A: Show answer
Contact timing and mode affect reachability and willingness to participate. Daytime door-to-door interviews may miss people who are employed away from home. Evening interviews may miss people who work late, attend events, or are otherwise unavailable. Telephone, mail, web, and in-person approaches also differ in access and convenience across age, language, income, disability, and technology-use groups. If the survey relies on one time window or one mode, some groups may be systematically underrepresented. Researchers can reduce the risk through varied contact times, multiple modes, accessible language, reasonable repeat attempts, and monitoring of response patterns. They should also document the modes used and explain how recruitment differences were handled in analysis.
Study instruction:
- Design a contact schedule and mixed-mode follow-up plan for a population with varied work schedules and technology access.
Key point:
- Contact strategy shapes who can participate.
- Flexible and documented follow-up can improve coverage and reduce avoidable nonresponse.
Source links:
- AAPOR: Best Practices for Survey Research
- U.S. Census Bureau: Response Rates Definitions
24. Q: What does the 1936 Literary Digest election-polling failure demonstrate about sampling frames?
A: Show answer
The Literary Digest poll relied on a very large list of telephone subscribers and magazine subscribers to predict the presidential election. The list appeared credible because it had been used successfully in an earlier election, but in 1936 telephone ownership and magazine subscriptions were associated with economic status. The frame therefore overrepresented people who were relatively affluent and whose political preferences differed from those of the wider electorate. The poll predicted a result that did not match the actual election. The central lesson is that a large sample drawn from a systematically unrepresentative frame can produce a confidently wrong conclusion. A sampling frame must be evaluated against the target population for the specific time and purpose; historical performance does not guarantee current coverage or validity.
Study instruction:
- Explain why a list that worked in one election may become biased when population coverage and social conditions change.
Key point:
- A large sample cannot compensate for a frame that systematically excludes relevant groups.
- Past success does not establish present representativeness.
Source links:
- AAPOR Disclosure Standards
- Statistics Canada: Non-sampling Error
25. Q: What is interviewer effect, and how can the interviewer's identity influence answers?
A: Show answer
Interviewer effect occurs when respondent answers are influenced by characteristics, behavior, expectations, or perceived identity of the interviewer. The chapter describes a wartime study in which black respondents gave different answers to interviewers of different racial backgrounds. The differences may have reflected trust, perceived social pressure, fear of judgment, or a desire to provide an acceptable answer. The interviewer may also influence answers through tone, wording, body language, or the way questions are explained. Such effects can produce systematic measurement differences even when the questionnaire is unchanged. Researchers should standardize interviewer training, use neutral delivery, monitor interviewer-level response patterns, consider privacy and respondent comfort, and document relevant collection modes. Where interviewer identity could affect sensitive answers, self-administered methods may be worth evaluating.
Study instruction:
- Identify questions where interviewer identity or perceived authority could affect responses and recommend safeguards.
Key point:
- Data collection is a social interaction, not a neutral mechanical process.
- Standardization and privacy can reduce interviewer-related measurement effects.
Source links:
- AAPOR: Best Practices for Survey Research
- Statistics Canada: Non-sampling Error
26. Q: Why can a questionnaire provide only a limited sample of a person's attitudes and experiences?
A: Show answer
A questionnaire contains a selected set of questions, and each answer reflects only what a respondent understands, remembers, is willing to disclose, and can express in the response format. A person's attitudes and experiences are broader than any single set of prompts. Different wording, question order, recall periods, response scales, and interviewer explanations can produce different answers. A response should therefore not be treated as a complete or direct measurement of a person's inner beliefs. Researchers should define the construct they intend to measure, use tested questions, avoid double-barrelled or leading wording, provide appropriate response options, and interpret findings within the limits of the instrument. Where the topic is complex, multiple measures or qualitative follow-up may be needed.
Study instruction:
- Select a broad concept such as trust or well-being and identify several distinct dimensions a single survey question would fail to capture.
Key point:
- Survey answers are measurements produced by an instrument, not complete representations of a person.
- Question design and interpretation affect the evidence collected.
Source links:
- AAPOR: Best Practices for Survey Research
27. Q: What is the difference between unit nonresponse and item nonresponse?
A: Show answer
Unit nonresponse occurs when a selected eligible person, household, or organization does not provide the required interview or survey information. Item nonresponse occurs when a respondent participates but leaves one or more questions unanswered. The distinction matters because the causes and remedies differ. Unit nonresponse may require additional contact attempts, alternative modes, or adjustments to the respondent weights. Item nonresponse may require question redesign, clearer instructions, appropriate skip logic, or carefully justified imputation. For example, an alumnus may complete a survey but refuse to disclose income; that is item nonresponse for the income variable even though the person is a respondent for other questions. Reports should distinguish missing answers from substantive options such as ânone,â ânot applicable,â or âprefer not to answer.â
Study instruction:
- Classify missing information in a sample survey and determine whether the missingness is at the unit or item level.
Key point:
- Unit and item nonresponse occur at different stages and require different diagnostics.
- A blank response must not be confused with a substantive answer.
Source links:
- U.S. Census Bureau: Response Rates Definitions
- AAPOR Standards and Ethics
28. Q: How can weighting help address unequal selection probabilities or nonresponse, and what are its limits?
A: Show answer
Weighting assigns different influence to sampled observations so that estimates reflect known selection probabilities or align with reliable population benchmarks. A unit selected with a lower probability may receive a larger base weight than a unit selected with a higher probability. Nonresponse adjustments may increase the weights of respondents who resemble nonrespondents on observed characteristics, and calibration may align weighted totals with trusted population counts. However, weighting does not automatically eliminate bias. It depends on accurate auxiliary information, suitable adjustment groups or models, and the assumption that differences relevant to the estimate are sufficiently captured by the variables used. Extreme weights can also increase variance. Researchers should disclose how weights were calculated, which variables and benchmarks were used, and whether the reported precision accounts for the design and weighting.
Study instruction:
- Explain the purpose of base weights, nonresponse adjustments, and calibration weights, then identify one limitation of each.
Key point:
- Weighting can correct known imbalances under explicit assumptions.
- It cannot guarantee correction for unobserved differences or a severely incomplete frame.
Source links:
- AAPOR Standards and Ethics
- U.S. Census Bureau: Response Rates Definitions
29. Q: Why is a high response rate not by itself proof that a survey is unbiased?
A: Show answer
Response rate measures participation relative to the eligible sample under a defined calculation; it does not directly measure whether respondents and nonrespondents differ on the variables being estimated. A survey can achieve a high response rate but still contain coverage errors, inaccurate reporting, interviewer effects, or systematic measurement problems. Conversely, a lower response rate may produce limited bias for an estimate if response is not strongly related to that estimate, although the risk still needs assessment. The relationship between response propensity and the outcome matters more than the percentage alone. Researchers should report response rates using recognized definitions, analyze available characteristics of respondents and nonrespondents, investigate key estimates, and disclose uncertainty. A response rate is a diagnostic indicator, not a universal certificate of data quality.
Study instruction:
- Compare two hypothetical surveys with different response rates and explain why neither can be judged without information about nonrespondents and measurement quality.
Key point:
- Response rate and nonresponse bias are related but not identical concepts.
- Evaluate how response patterns affect the specific estimate of interest.
Source links:
- BLS: CPS Response Rates
- AAPOR Standards and Ethics
30. Q: How should a researcher assess whether missing or unreachable people could change the study's conclusion?
A: Show answer
The researcher should first describe the missing groups and why they were not observed. Next, compare respondents and nonrespondents using information available from the sampling frame or other appropriate records, such as age, location, employment category, organization size, or previous participation. Then examine whether these differences are associated with the outcome being estimated. Follow-up studies of a subsample of nonrespondents, alternative contact modes, administrative data, and sensitivity analyses can provide additional evidence. If nonrespondents cannot be characterized, the limitation should be stated rather than concealed. The researcher should test whether plausible values among missing units would materially change the conclusion. The analysis should distinguish observed evidence from assumptions and avoid treating a weighting adjustment as proof that all differences have been removed.
Study instruction:
- Develop a nonresponse-bias assessment plan that identifies comparison variables, follow-up methods, and sensitivity checks.
Key point:
- Assess missingness in relation to the outcome, not merely the number of missing cases.
- State what remains unknown when nonrespondents cannot be evaluated.
Source links:
- BLS: CPS Response Rates
- AAPOR Standards and Ethics
31. Q: What should readers ask when a poll claims that a stated percentage of the public supports or opposes an issue?
A: Show answer
Readers should ask: Which population is being described? How was the sampling frame constructed? Was the sample probability-based or non-probability-based? How were participants selected and contacted? What proportion responded, and how were nonresponses handled? What exact question wording and response options were used? When and in what mode was the survey conducted? Were results weighted, and on what variables? What is the sample size for each subgroup? What measures of precision are appropriate for this design, and what limitations remain? A percentage without these details may sound definitive while describing only a narrow group of participants. AAPOR's disclosure standards emphasize transparent reporting of sample source, recruitment, field dates, completed interviews, weighting, and data-quality procedures so users can evaluate the evidence rather than relying on a headline number.
Study instruction:
- Use a published poll and prepare a checklist of methodological questions that must be answered before accepting its headline percentage.
Key point:
- Always ask âwhich people?â before accepting a claim about âthe public.â
- Transparent methodology is essential for evaluating poll results.
Source links:
- AAPOR Disclosure Standards
- AAPOR: Best Practices for Survey Research
32. Q: How can a researcher reduce bias during the design and fieldwork stages of a survey?
A: Show answer
Bias reduction should be built into the entire survey lifecycle. Define the target population and unit of analysis precisely; choose a frame with adequate coverage; select units using a defensible method; and document selection probabilities. Use clear, neutral, single-concept questions that respondents can understand. Pilot-test the questionnaire, offer accessible modes and languages where appropriate, and train interviewers to follow consistent procedures. Monitor contact attempts, refusals, partial responses, interviewer patterns, and response distributions while data collection is underway. Follow up with nonrespondents using reasonable alternative times or modes, while respecting consent and privacy. Finally, validate coding and processing, assess missingness, apply justified adjustments, and disclose remaining limitations. No single safeguard eliminates all error; layered controls make weaknesses more visible and manageable.
Study instruction:
- Create a survey quality plan covering frame construction, selection, questionnaire testing, fieldwork monitoring, and analysis.
Key point:
- Bias prevention must cover design, collection, processing, and reporting.
- Continuous monitoring can identify problems before they contaminate the full dataset.
Source links:
- AAPOR: Best Practices for Survey Research
- AAPOR Standards and Ethics
33. Q: What would you do if a survey sample contains too few people from a group that is important to the research question?
A: Show answer
First determine whether the group was undercovered by the frame, selected at too low a rate, difficult to contact, or less likely to respond. These causes require different remedies. If the frame omits the group, improve or supplement the frame where possible. If the group is small but covered, a future design may oversample it using known selection probabilities so that subgroup analysis is sufficiently informative. If response is low, use appropriate follow-up, accessible modes, and careful nonresponse analysis. Weighting may be necessary to restore population proportions for overall estimates, but it can increase variance and should be applied with design-aware methods. The researcher should not simply collect convenience responses from the missing group and merge them without documenting the recruitment method and evaluating comparability.
Study instruction:
- Diagnose an underrepresented subgroup by separating frame coverage, selection probability, contactability, and response propensity.
Key point:
- Identify the cause of underrepresentation before choosing a corrective action.
- Oversampling and weighting require transparent design and analysis.
Source links:
- AAPOR Disclosure Standards
- U.S. Census Bureau: Response Rates Definitions
34. Q: What is the difference between random sampling and random assignment, and why should they not be confused?
A: Show answer
Random sampling is a method for selecting participants from a population and is used to support generalization from the sample to that population. Random assignment is a method for allocating already recruited participants to treatment or comparison groups in an experiment and is used to support causal comparisons within the study. A study may use random assignment without using a representative sample; its treatment effect may be internally credible for participants but not automatically generalizable to the wider population. Conversely, a probability sample may represent a population but not establish causation because participants were not randomly assigned to exposures. Both methods use chance, but they address different questions. Researchers should describe which procedure was used and avoid claiming population representativeness or causal validity from the wrong design feature.
Study instruction:
- Classify examples of experiments and surveys according to whether they use random sampling, random assignment, both, or neither.
Key point:
- Random sampling supports population inference; random assignment supports causal inference.
- One procedure does not substitute for the other.
Source links:
- AAPOR Standards and Ethics
35. Q: How should the results of a biased or non-probability sample be reported responsibly?
A: Show answer
The report should identify the sample as non-probability-based or otherwise describe the actual recruitment method, state the population from which participants were recruited, explain eligibility and contact procedures, disclose the number of completed responses, and describe weighting or modeling assumptions. It should not present a conventional probability-sample margin of error unless a defensible method and assumptions support such a measure. Findings should be framed as results for the observed participants or as model-based estimates with explicit limitations, not as unqualified statements about the entire population. AAPOR recommends transparency about the underlying model and the assumptions used to estimate precision for non-probability samples. Researchers should also acknowledge unmeasured error and avoid implying that a large opt-in sample is equivalent to a probability sample.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite an overconfident headline from an opt-in poll into a statement that accurately reflects its recruitment method and inference limits.
Key point:
- Describe how participants were recruited before making population claims.
- Do not report unsupported margins of error for non-probability samples.
Source links:
- AAPOR Standards and Ethics
- AAPOR Disclosure Standards
36. Q: What does the Connecticut cancer-survival example illustrate about historical data and incomplete follow-up?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes historical survival figures that appeared to show improvement over time, but earlier records had been reconstructed retrospectively and some patients could no longer be traced. When people whose outcomes are unknown are treated or implicitly assumed in a way that differs from reality, the estimated survival rate can be distorted. The example illustrates that a data series may look complete while its underlying follow-up is incomplete or inconsistent across periods. Researchers should examine when records were actually created, how earlier cases were identified, how losses to follow-up were handled, and whether missing outcomes differ systematically from observed outcomes. A trend should not be interpreted as evidence of improvement until changes in case ascertainment, definitions, follow-up, and data quality have been evaluated.
Study instruction:
- Assess a longitudinal outcome report for differences in case identification, follow-up completeness, and treatment of unknown outcomes across time periods.
Key point:
- Historical comparisons can be distorted when follow-up and record completeness differ.
- Unknown outcomes should be handled transparently rather than assumed away.
Source links:
- Statistics Canada: Non-sampling Error
- U.S. Census Bureau: Nonsampling Error
37. Q: How can a sample be biased even when the researcher cannot identify a specific source of bias?
A: Show answer
A lack of an obvious defect is not proof that a sample is unbiased. Some selection mechanisms are subtle: people may be more likely to answer because they have strong opinions, greater confidence, more time, a better relationship with the institution, or a stronger desire to please the interviewer. Frame omissions may be hidden in outdated records or poorly understood eligibility rules. A survey can also contain multiple small biases that reinforce one another. Researchers should therefore apply a structured skepticism: document design decisions, compare available population benchmarks, analyze response patterns, test alternative assumptions, and disclose uncertainties that cannot be measured. The objective is not to claim that every possible bias has been removed, but to identify plausible risks and show how they were evaluated and mitigated.
Study instruction:
- Conduct a bias audit of a survey and list plausible hidden mechanisms even where no single defect is immediately visible.
Key point:
- Absence of detected bias is not proof that no bias exists.
- Transparent limitations are part of responsible statistical reporting.
Source links:
- Statistics Canada: Non-sampling Error
- AAPOR Standards and Ethics
38. Q: What controls should an organization use when commissioning or reviewing a survey for management decisions?
A: Show answer
The commissioning organization should approve a clear research question, target population, sampling plan, questionnaire, fieldwork protocol, data-protection arrangements, and reporting requirements before collection begins. The contract or terms of reference should require disclosure of the sampling frame, probability or non-probability method, contact and recruitment modes, field dates, completed interviews, response or participation rates, weighting, quality checks, and limitations. During fieldwork, management should monitor progress, subgroup coverage, unusual response patterns, and deviations from the approved method. On receipt of the data, an independent reviewer should check coding, missing values, weighting, calculations, and whether conclusions match the evidence. Decisions with legal, financial, health, or public consequences may require specialist statistical review and documented approval.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a procurement and quality-assurance checklist for an external survey provider, including evidence required before final acceptance.
Key point:
- Survey quality should be specified, monitored, and independently reviewed.
- Management should not rely solely on a polished report or headline statistic.
Source links:
- AAPOR Disclosure Standards
- AAPOR: Best Practices for Survey Research
39. Q: How should a reader distinguish a statistic about respondents from a claim about the wider population?
A: Show answer
A respondent statistic summarizes the people who actually provided usable data. A population estimate attempts to describe a larger defined group and requires a defensible design, appropriate adjustments, and assumptions about coverage and response. The wording of a report should make this distinction explicit. âAmong 420 people who completed this online questionnaire, 68 percent supported the proposalâ is a statement about respondents. âSixty-eight percent of residents support the proposalâ is a population claim that requires evidence that the sample and analysis support generalization to residents. If the sample is voluntary or the frame is incomplete, the broader statement may be unjustified. Readers should check the methods before accepting a change in the group to which a percentage is attributed.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite three survey findings so each clearly identifies whether it describes respondents, a defined subgroup, or a target population estimate.
Key point:
- Never silently replace ârespondentsâ with âthe population.â
- The scope of the conclusion must match the scope of the evidence.
Source links:
- AAPOR Disclosure Standards
40. Q: What are the most important lessons from Chapter 1, âThe Sample with the Built-in Biasâ?
A: Show answer
The chapter's central lesson is that a statistic is only as trustworthy as the population definition, sampling frame, selection procedure, response process, and measurement method behind it. A precise average can mislead when unreachable people, nonrespondents, volunteers, or socially desirable answers create a sample that differs from the group the report claims to describe. Random sampling provides a strong basis for statistical inference when the frame and implementation are sound, while stratification can improve coverage and efficiency if subgroup information is accurate and selection within strata is properly controlled. Interviewer discretion, contact timing, location, question wording, and incomplete follow-up can introduce additional bias. Readers should ask âwhich people?â and researchers should disclose methods, examine nonresponse and coverage, distinguish sampling from non-sampling error, and state limitations. Large samples and complex calculations cannot transform systematically biased evidence into a reliable population conclusion.
Study instruction:
- Review the chapter's examples and prepare a short audit of a real poll covering population, frame, selection, response, measurement, and reporting limitations.
Key point:
- A statistic must be judged by the evidence-generation process, not by its precision or presentation.
- The key question is whether the sample and measurements support the population conclusion being made.
- Transparent methods and critical interpretation are essential to reliable statistical reasoning.
Source links:
- AAPOR Standards and Ethics
- Statistics Canada: Non-sampling Error
- U.S. Census Bureau: Response Rates Definitions
