Table 2
The Well-Chosen Average: Mean, Median, Mode, and Statistical Interpretation
1. Q: Why can the same neighborhood income data produce an average of $15,000 and a median of $3,500, and why can both figures be mathematically correct yet misleading?
A: Show answer
The two figures can describe the same households because they represent different measures of central tendency. The mean is calculated by adding all household incomes and dividing by the number of households. A small number of exceptionally wealthy households can raise this arithmetic average substantially. The median is the middle income after all households are arranged from lowest to highest; half of households fall below it and half above it. In the chapter's illustration, a few millionaire households lift the mean to $15,000 even though the median is only $3,500 and the most common income is $5,000. Neither calculation is necessarily arithmetically wrong. The misleading element is presenting an unqualified word such as average without identifying the measure, the population, and the distribution. A reader may wrongly interpret the mean as the income of a typical household.
Study instruction:
- Explain how a small number of very high incomes can raise a mean while leaving the median largely unchanged, and identify what additional information is needed to interpret both figures.
Key point:
- A mathematically correct statistic can still create a misleading impression when its meaning is not disclosed.
- Always identify whether an income average is a mean, median, or mode.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
2. Q: What does the term average mean, and why is it insufficient on its own when describing a dataset?
A: Show answer
In everyday language, average is often used as though it referred to one unambiguous calculation. Statistically, however, it may refer to different measures of central tendency, particularly the arithmetic mean, median, or mode. Each measure answers a different question. The mean describes the arithmetic balance point of numerical observations; the median identifies the middle position in an ordered dataset; and the mode identifies the most frequently occurring value or category. These measures can be close together in a roughly symmetrical distribution but differ substantially when data are skewed or contain extreme observations. A report that states only average income, average salary, or average property value leaves readers unable to determine what was calculated. Responsible reporting names the measure and explains the population and period covered.
Study instruction:
- Find three published statements using the word average and determine whether each identifies the statistical measure and the population being described.
Key point:
- Average is not a sufficiently precise statistical description unless the measure is identified.
- Mean, median, and mode represent different features of a distribution.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- Statistics Canada: Exploring Measures of Central Tendency
3. Q: How is the arithmetic mean calculated, and what does it represent?
A: Show answer
The arithmetic mean is calculated by adding all valid numerical observations and dividing the total by the number of observations. Its formula is (\bar{x}=\frac{\sum x_i}{n}) for a sample, where each (x_i) is an observed value and (n) is the number of observations. For example, if five employees earn 2,000, 2,200, 2,400, 2,600, and 2,800 currency units per month, total pay is 12,000 and the mean is 2,400. The mean uses every included observation and therefore reflects the total amount distributed across the group. It is useful for calculations involving totals, aggregate costs, and further statistical analysis. However, it can be pulled toward unusually high or low observations, so it does not necessarily describe what most individuals receive.
Study instruction:
- Calculate the mean for a small dataset by showing the sum, the number of observations, and the division used to obtain the result.
Key point:
- The mean equals the sum of observations divided by their count.
- Every included value affects the mean, including extreme values.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
4. Q: How is the median calculated, and what does it tell a reader about a group?
A: Show answer
The median is the middle observation after the data have been arranged in ascending or descending order. With an odd number of observations, it is the single central value. With an even number, it is conventionally calculated as the arithmetic mean of the two central observations. For example, the ordered values 2, 3, 5, 8, and 12 have a median of 5. For 2, 3, 5, 8, 12, and 20, the median is (5 + 8) ÷ 2 = 6.5. The median divides the ordered observations into two halves: approximately 50% are at or below it and 50% are at or above it, with the precise treatment of ties depending on the data. It is a positional measure rather than a measure of the total value of all observations.
Study instruction:
- Practise finding the median in both odd-sized and even-sized datasets, including datasets containing repeated values.
Key point:
- The median is determined by the middle position in ordered data.
- It is less sensitive to extreme values than the arithmetic mean.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- Statistics Canada: Exploring Measures of Central Tendency
5. Q: What is the mode, and when can it be more informative than the mean or median?
A: Show answer
The mode is the value or category that occurs most frequently in a dataset. It can be useful when the practical question concerns the most common salary band, product size, response category, or transaction amount. For example, if the annual salaries of a group of employees most frequently equal 5,000, that value is the mode even if the mean is raised by a highly paid executive. Unlike the mean and median, the mode can also be used with nominal categorical data, such as the most frequently selected service or product type. A dataset may have one mode, more than one mode, or no unique mode. The mode does not necessarily lie near the middle of the distribution, so it should not automatically be treated as a complete description of a typical observation.
Study instruction:
- Identify the most frequent value in a numerical dataset and the most frequent category in a categorical dataset, then explain what each mode communicates.
Key point:
- The mode identifies the most frequently occurring value or category.
- A mode can reveal the most common outcome without describing the distribution as a whole.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
6. Q: How do mean, median, and mode differ when applied to employee salaries?
A: Show answer
The mean salary describes total salary expenditure divided by the number of employees. The median salary identifies the central employee salary after employees are ordered by pay. The mode identifies the salary or salary band occurring most frequently. Suppose a small organization has many employees earning around 2,000–3,000 currency units and one executive earning 45,000. The executive's salary can increase the mean significantly, while the median may remain near the ordinary employee salary and the mode may identify the most common pay level. Each statistic answers a different management question: the mean is relevant to total compensation cost per employee, the median to the midpoint of employee pay, and the mode to the most common pay level. A credible compensation analysis should explain which measure is being used and may report more than one.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a salary dataset with one unusually high executive salary and calculate all three measures to observe their different interpretations.
Key point:
- Mean, median, and mode answer different questions about employee compensation.
- Reporting only the largest-looking average can conceal the distribution of ordinary employee pay.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
7. Q: Why can a few very wealthy households make the mean income much higher than the income of most households?
A: Show answer
The arithmetic mean is sensitive to the magnitude of every observation. A household with an exceptionally high income contributes much more to the total than an ordinary household. When the total is divided by the number of households, a small number of extreme incomes can raise the mean even though most households have not experienced any corresponding increase. This commonly occurs in income and wealth data because the upper tail can contain a relatively small number of very large values. The median is based on rank, so an extreme upper observation usually has little or no effect on the middle position unless it changes the ordering around the centre. The correct interpretation is not that the mean is invalid, but that the mean and median describe different aspects of an unequal distribution.
Study instruction:
- Compare a dataset before and after adding one very high income and calculate how much the mean and median change.
Key point:
- Extreme high values can pull the mean upward without changing the typical position of most observations.
- The median is often more informative about the midpoint of a highly unequal income distribution.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
8. Q: What is a positively skewed or right-skewed distribution, and how does it affect the mean?
A: Show answer
A positively skewed distribution has a longer or heavier tail toward larger values. Many observations may be concentrated at relatively low or moderate values, while a smaller number extend far to the right. The mean is pulled in the direction of this long upper tail because it incorporates the magnitude of every observation. In a typical unimodal right-skewed distribution, the mean is greater than the median, and the median is often greater than the mode, although this ordering is not a universal mathematical rule for every dataset. Household income, property prices, and some compensation distributions can exhibit right skew. A reader should inspect the distribution rather than assume that a reported mean is close to the value experienced by most members of the group.
Study instruction:
- Draw or inspect a right-skewed histogram and explain why a small number of high observations move the mean toward the upper tail.
Key point:
- A right tail can pull the mean above the median.
- The distribution's shape should influence the choice and interpretation of a central measure.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Shape
9. Q: What is a negatively skewed or left-skewed distribution, and how can it influence the mean?
A: Show answer
A negatively skewed distribution has a longer or heavier tail toward smaller values. Most observations may be concentrated at relatively high values, while a smaller number extend far toward the lower end. Those low observations can pull the mean below the median. For example, a group in which most test scores are high but a small number are very low may have a mean lower than the median. The direction of skewness describes the direction of the tail, not necessarily where most observations are concentrated. A left-skewed distribution should therefore be interpreted by examining the histogram, quantiles, and relevant context rather than relying on the mean alone.
Study instruction:
- Compare a left-skewed distribution with a right-skewed distribution and identify the direction in which the mean is pulled in each case.
Key point:
- A left tail can pull the mean below the median.
- Skewness is a property of distribution shape and must be interpreted with the data context.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Shape
10. Q: Why do the mean, median, and mode tend to be close together in a symmetrical, unimodal distribution?
A: Show answer
In an ideal symmetrical, unimodal distribution, observations are balanced around a central point and the highest concentration occurs around that same centre. The arithmetic balance point, the middle ranked value, and the most frequent value therefore coincide at the population level. A normal distribution is a familiar example. In real samples, the three estimates may differ slightly because of sampling variation, rounding, or the way a mode is estimated from grouped data. Symmetry alone does not guarantee a single mode, and not every symmetrical distribution has the same practical interpretation. The important point is that when the distribution is approximately symmetric and well behaved, the mean can be a reasonable measure of centre; when it is not, the choice requires more care.
Study instruction:
- Examine a histogram that is approximately bell-shaped and compare the mean, median, and mode, distinguishing theoretical equality from small sample differences.
Key point:
- In a normal distribution, the three measures of central tendency share the same centre.
- Distribution shape determines whether that convenient relationship is a reasonable approximation.
Source links:
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
11. Q: What is an outlier, and why should it be investigated before interpreting an average?
A: Show answer
An outlier is an observation that is unusually distant from the rest of the data relative to the pattern being examined. It may represent a genuine exceptional case, a data-entry error, a measurement problem, a different population, or a valid but rare event. Because the mean uses every numerical value, an extreme observation can have a substantial effect on it. The median is usually less affected because it depends on order rather than the magnitude of the most extreme value. Analysts should investigate the source, definition, unit, and validity of an outlier before deciding how to handle it. A valid extreme observation should not be removed simply because it makes a result inconvenient. Any exclusion, correction, or transformation must be justified and documented.
Study instruction:
- Identify an extreme value in a dataset and prepare a short investigation record stating whether it is valid, erroneous, or requires further clarification.
Key point:
- An outlier may be a genuine observation or a data-quality problem.
- Never remove an extreme value solely to produce a preferred average.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
12. Q: Why is the median generally less sensitive to extreme observations than the mean?
A: Show answer
The median depends on the relative order of observations, not on the distance between the most extreme values and the centre. If the highest income in a group increases from 100,000 to 1,000,000 while it remains the highest observation, the median may remain exactly the same. The mean, however, increases because the additional 900,000 is included in the total. This makes the median a robust descriptive measure for many skewed datasets. Robust does not mean immune to every data problem: a median can still change when observations near the middle are added, removed, or misclassified, and it does not describe the total amount. Its relative resistance to extremes makes it useful when the question concerns the midpoint rather than the aggregate.
Study instruction:
- Replace the largest value in a dataset with a much larger value and observe which central measure changes more.
Key point:
- The median is based on rank and is less affected by the magnitude of extreme values.
- The mean remains useful for totals but requires care when data contain extreme values.
Source links:
- Statistics Canada: Exploring Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
13. Q: What does it mean when nearly everybody in a group is below the average?
A: Show answer
It means the arithmetic mean has been raised by a relatively small number of very high observations. The mean is a balance point: values far above the rest can offset many values below it. It is therefore possible for the mean to exceed the income of most households or employees. This does not mean the arithmetic calculation is defective. It means that the mean is not necessarily a good description of the experience of a typical member of the group. In a highly right-skewed distribution, the median and the distribution of values may be more useful for understanding the majority. Reports should avoid implying that the mean is the amount most people receive.
Study instruction:
- Construct a dataset with many modest values and a few very large values, then count how many observations fall below the mean.
Key point:
- The mean is a balance point, not necessarily the value received by most people.
- A skewed distribution can place the mean above the majority of observations.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
14. Q: How can an employer use a mean salary figure to conceal differences between ordinary employees and senior owners or executives?
A: Show answer
A mean calculated across all employees and owners can be raised by a small number of high remuneration packages. If a report does not distinguish employees from owners, executives, or other groups, readers may assume that the mean describes ordinary employee pay. The chapter's manufacturing example demonstrates how classification and presentation choices can alter the apparent story: the owners' salaries and profit distributions can be presented separately or included in a broader average, changing the apparent average wage. The figures can be arithmetically correct while answering different questions. A useful compensation disclosure should define who is included, what counts as remuneration, the period measured, whether bonuses or profit distributions are included, and whether the statistic is a mean or median.
Study instruction:
- Recreate a compensation analysis that separately identifies ordinary employees, owners, and executives, then compare the results when groups are combined.
Key point:
- Group definitions and inclusion rules can materially change a reported salary average.
- Readers need to know whose pay and which forms of remuneration are included.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
15. Q: What lesson does the chapter's manufacturing-company example teach about changing the way compensation is grouped?
A: Show answer
The example shows that a presentation can make compensation appear more favorable by changing which people and amounts are combined in each calculation. Initially, 90 employees receive 198,000 in wages, or 2,200 each on average. Three owners receive 11,000 each in salary and share 45,000 of profit, giving each owner 26,000 when salary and profit are combined. The example then distributes 30,000 of profit as owner bonuses, includes the owners in a broader salary average, and reports the remaining profit separately. The resulting figures look more favorable even though the underlying economic arrangements have not become more equitable. The lesson is to examine the numerator, denominator, population, and classification before accepting a comparison.
Study instruction:
- Recalculate the example using the stated employee and owner totals, and document how each change in grouping affects the reported average.
Key point:
- A change in presentation can alter the apparent message without changing the underlying economic reality.
- Always reconcile reported averages to the underlying amounts and population counts.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
16. Q: Why is the median often useful when evaluating employee pay or household income?
A: Show answer
The median identifies the midpoint of the ordered income distribution and is less affected by a small number of unusually high incomes than the mean. It can therefore provide a more informative description of the income level around which the middle of a population is located. For example, a median salary indicates that half of the included employees earn at or below the median and half at or above it, subject to ties. It does not reveal the size of the total payroll, the range of salaries, or the degree of inequality. A complete compensation analysis may therefore report the median together with the mean, percentiles, distribution bands, and a clear definition of the employee population.
Study instruction:
- Interpret a median salary statement and list two questions it answers and two questions it cannot answer without additional statistics.
Key point:
- The median is often useful for describing the midpoint of skewed income data.
- It should be supplemented when the analysis concerns total cost or inequality.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
17. Q: When is the mode a useful measure for understanding wages or prices?
A: Show answer
The mode is useful when decision-makers want to identify the most frequently occurring wage, price, product size, or category. In the chapter's neighborhood illustration, the mode is 5,000 because that income occurs more frequently than any other income value. In an organization, the modal salary band may show the pay level at which the largest number of employees are concentrated. However, the mode does not show how many people are far above or below that value, and it may be unstable when the dataset contains many distinct continuous values. For grouped data, the modal class is the interval with the greatest frequency; it is not necessarily an exact individual value.
Study instruction:
- Create a frequency table for a salary or price dataset and identify the modal value or modal interval, noting any ties.
Key point:
- The mode identifies the most common observation or category.
- Frequency information is needed to understand how dominant the mode actually is.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
18. Q: Can a dataset have more than one mode or no meaningful mode at all?
A: Show answer
Yes. A dataset is bimodal when two values or peaks share the highest frequency, and multimodal when more than two peaks are present. Multiple modes may indicate distinct subgroups, such as two different salary structures or two customer segments. A dataset may also have no unique mode when every value occurs equally often or when continuous observations are all different. For continuous data, analysts often examine a histogram or a suitable density estimate to identify a modal region rather than insist on an exact repeated value. The existence of multiple modes is a reason to investigate the population structure rather than force a single average to represent potentially different groups.
Study instruction:
- Construct a bimodal dataset and explain what two peaks could mean about the groups represented in the data.
Key point:
- A distribution can have multiple modes or no unique mode.
- Multiple peaks may reveal meaningful subgroups that a single central measure would conceal.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
19. Q: Why should the population and inclusion criteria be stated when reporting an average?
A: Show answer
An average is meaningful only in relation to a defined set of observations. The phrase average family income is ambiguous unless the report explains what counts as a family, whether people living alone are included, which geographic area is covered, and which period is measured. Likewise, an average employee salary may include or exclude part-time workers, contractors, owners, temporary employees, and bonuses. Changing the population changes the observations and may change the result even if the calculation method stays the same. A clear definition allows readers to assess whether the statistic answers the question they care about and whether comparisons with other groups are valid.
Study instruction:
- Write an operational definition for a population in an income study, including household or employee eligibility, location, period, and treatment of missing records.
Key point:
- The population definition determines what an average actually describes.
- Inclusion and exclusion rules must be disclosed before comparisons are made.
Source links:
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
- Statistics Canada: Exploring Measures of Central Tendency
20. Q: How can changing the definition of a family alter a reported median household or family income?
A: Show answer
The median depends on which units are included and how those units are defined. If a statistic includes only related people living together, it may produce a different median from a statistic that also includes people living alone or uses a broader household definition. The number of units, their income values, and their ordering all change when the definition changes. Consequently, two reports may use the same median calculation but describe different populations. A reader should check whether the measure refers to individuals, families, households, tax units, or another defined unit, and whether income is measured before or after taxes or transfers. Without these details, a comparison may attribute a difference to economic conditions when it actually reflects different definitions.
Study instruction:
- Compare two official income tables and identify how their definitions of household, family, and income affect comparability.
Key point:
- A change in the unit of analysis can change the median even when the formula is unchanged.
- Population definitions are part of the statistic, not optional background information.
Source links:
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
- Statistics Canada: Exploring Measures of Central Tendency
21. Q: Why is it misleading to compare a mean from one period with a median from another period?
A: Show answer
The two figures do not measure the same feature of the distribution. A mean reflects the arithmetic total divided by the number of observations, while a median reflects the middle rank. If the distribution is skewed, these measures may be far apart. A report could therefore show an apparent increase or decrease that is caused partly by changing the statistic rather than by a genuine change in income. Valid time comparisons should use consistent definitions, populations, measurement periods, and statistical measures. If a change in method is unavoidable, the report should explain it and, where possible, recalculate earlier periods using a consistent method.
Study instruction:
- Review a time-series comparison and verify that the same central measure and population definition are used in every period.
Key point:
- Comparisons require consistent statistical definitions across periods.
- A switch from mean to median can create an apparent trend that is not a real change.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
22. Q: Why can a reported increase in average weekly earnings be misleading if the workforce composition changes?
A: Show answer
Average earnings can rise because individual wage rates increase, because more employees work full time rather than part time, because the mix of occupations changes, or because high-paid employees become a larger share of the group. These mechanisms have different economic meanings. If a workforce contains many part-time employees in one year and more full-time employees in the next, average weekly earnings may increase even if the hourly wage rates have not changed. A sound analysis should distinguish pay rates, hours worked, employment status, occupational composition, and the population included in each period. Depending on the question, hourly earnings, full-time-equivalent pay, or group-specific comparisons may be more appropriate than an unadjusted overall mean.
Study instruction:
- Design a comparison that separates changes in hourly pay from changes in hours worked and workforce composition.
Key point:
- A change in average earnings does not necessarily mean individual wage rates increased.
- Workforce composition and working hours can affect the reported mean.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
23. Q: How should an analyst compare average salaries between two organizations with different workforce structures?
A: Show answer
The analyst should first establish whether both organizations use the same population definition, time period, currency basis, and compensation components. Differences in the proportions of executives, managers, part-time staff, temporary staff, and occupational groups can make an overall mean incomparable. The analyst should then examine medians, salary bands, percentiles, and group-specific results, such as pay by grade or occupation. Where appropriate, the comparison can adjust for hours worked, job mix, or full-time-equivalent status. Any adjustment should be transparent and should not conceal legitimate differences. A single average is rarely sufficient to assess pay equity or workforce competitiveness when the underlying employee populations are materially different.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a comparison checklist covering population, job mix, working hours, remuneration components, currency, and the selected central measure.
Key point:
- Comparable averages require comparable populations and definitions.
- Group-level analysis can reveal differences hidden by an overall mean.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
24. Q: What is the relationship between central tendency and dispersion, and why is an average not enough to describe a dataset?
A: Show answer
Central tendency describes where the centre of a distribution lies, while dispersion describes how widely observations vary around that centre. Two groups can have the same mean or median but very different levels of inequality, consistency, or risk. For example, two teams may have the same average completion time, but one team may have nearly identical times while the other includes both very fast and very slow cases. A single average conceals that difference. Analysts should therefore consider measures such as range, interquartile range, variance, standard deviation, and percentiles, as appropriate to the data and purpose. The chapter points toward this broader analysis by showing that the choice of average is only one part of interpreting a distribution.
Study instruction:
- Create two datasets with the same mean but different spreads and explain why the same average does not imply the same underlying pattern.
Key point:
- Central tendency describes the centre; dispersion describes variability.
- A responsible statistical summary usually needs both location and spread.
Source links:
- Statistics Canada: Exploring Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
25. Q: How can a histogram help determine which average is most informative?
A: Show answer
A histogram displays how frequently observations fall within numerical intervals and reveals features such as symmetry, skewness, multiple peaks, gaps, and extreme values. If the histogram is approximately symmetrical and unimodal, the mean may be a reasonable measure of centre. If it is strongly skewed or contains influential extremes, the median may better describe the midpoint. If the main question concerns the most common outcome, the mode or modal interval may be more useful. A histogram also helps identify whether the data contain more than one subgroup, in which case a single central measure may be inadequate. The analyst should inspect the distribution before selecting a statistic rather than choosing the measure that produces the most attractive result.
Study instruction:
- Plot a salary dataset as a histogram and justify whether the mean, median, mode, or a combination is most informative.
Key point:
- Visual inspection reveals distribution features that a single average cannot show.
- The measure of centre should be selected after examining the data's shape.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Shape
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
26. Q: What is a weighted mean, and why must the weights be understood before using it?
A: Show answer
A weighted mean assigns different levels of influence to observations according to specified weights. It is calculated by multiplying each value by its weight, adding the weighted values, and dividing by the sum of the weights. A common example is an overall average assembled from groups of different sizes: a group with 500 employees should generally contribute more observations to an employee-level mean than a group with 10 employees. Weights may also represent survey design, exposure, or other justified factors. The critical question is what the weights represent and whether they match the intended population or estimand. Incorrect or unexplained weights can distort the result just as an inappropriate unweighted average can.
Study instruction:
- Calculate an overall mean from two groups of different sizes and compare the weighted result with the simple average of the two group means.
Key point:
- A weighted mean reflects the relative contribution assigned to each observation or group.
- The weighting method must match the question and be disclosed.
Source links:
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
- Statistics Canada: Exploring Measures of Central Tendency
27. Q: Why can the simple average of group averages produce an incorrect overall average?
A: Show answer
The simple average of group means gives each group equal weight, regardless of how many observations each group contains. This is appropriate only when equal group weighting is intended or the groups have equal sizes. Suppose one branch has 10 employees with a mean salary of 2,000 and another has 100 employees with a mean salary of 3,000. The simple average of the two branch means is 2,500, but the employee-level mean is (10 × 2,000 + 100 × 3,000) ÷ 110, or approximately 2,909.09. The second branch contributes far more employees and therefore more salary observations. Analysts must establish whether the objective is to compare a typical branch or calculate the average across all employees.
Study instruction:
- Recalculate an overall average from groups of unequal size using both an unweighted and a properly weighted method, then explain which answers the stated question.
Key point:
- Averaging group means without accounting for group size can misrepresent the overall population mean.
- Weighting depends on whether the unit of analysis is a group or an individual observation.
Source links:
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
- Statistics Canada: Exploring Measures of Central Tendency
28. Q: What is a trimmed mean, and when might it be considered?
A: Show answer
A trimmed mean is calculated after removing a specified proportion of the lowest and highest observations, then calculating the arithmetic mean of the remaining values. For example, a 10% trimmed mean removes the lowest 10% and highest 10% of observations according to a defined procedure. It can reduce the influence of extreme tails while retaining more numerical information than the median. However, trimming changes the statistic and may exclude valid observations. The analyst must specify the trimming proportion, method, sample size, and reason for using it. A trimmed mean should not be selected simply because it produces a preferred result. It is a supplementary robust measure whose suitability depends on the distribution and analytical objective.
Study instruction:
- Calculate an ordinary mean and a trimmed mean for a dataset with extreme values and document exactly which observations were excluded.
Key point:
- A trimmed mean reduces the influence of selected tail observations.
- The trimming rule must be justified and reported transparently.
Source links:
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
29. Q: What is a Winsorized mean, and how does it differ from a trimmed mean?
A: Show answer
A Winsorized mean limits the influence of extreme observations by replacing values beyond selected percentile cutoffs with the cutoff values rather than removing them. A trimmed mean excludes the selected tail observations from the calculation; a Winsorized mean retains the number of observations but caps their values at specified boundaries. Both methods are designed to reduce sensitivity to extreme tails, but they produce different results and rely on choices about the cutoffs. Neither method automatically makes a dataset representative or corrects errors in the underlying population definition. Analysts should disclose the procedure and may report the ordinary mean and median alongside the robust measure so readers can understand how much the result depends on the treatment of extreme values.
Study instruction:
- Apply the same percentile cutoffs to a dataset using trimming and Winsorization, then compare the resulting means and explain the difference.
Key point:
- Trimming removes selected observations; Winsorization caps their values.
- Both require a transparent, defensible rule for handling the tails.
Source links:
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
30. Q: Why should an analyst not automatically delete a high salary or income merely because it is an outlier?
A: Show answer
An extreme value may be a genuine part of the population, such as an executive salary, business owner's income, or exceptional property transaction. Removing it without a defensible reason changes the population being described and can understate the total or the upper end of the distribution. The correct response is to investigate whether the observation is valid, correctly recorded, measured on the same basis, and relevant to the intended population. If it is a data error, correction should be supported by evidence. If it is valid but influential, the analyst can explain its effect and report robust measures or subgroup results alongside the mean. Exclusion criteria should be established consistently and disclosed.
Study instruction:
- Review a dataset with one unusually high value and prepare a documented decision explaining whether it should be retained, corrected, or excluded.
Key point:
- An unusual value is not automatically an erroneous value.
- Statistical convenience is not a valid reason for deleting genuine observations.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
31. Q: How can an analyst determine whether the mean or median is more appropriate for a particular decision?
A: Show answer
The choice should begin with the question being answered. If the objective is to calculate total expenditure per employee, the mean may be useful because it is directly related to the aggregate payroll. If the objective is to describe the midpoint of employee experience in a skewed salary distribution, the median may be more informative. If the question concerns the most frequently occurring category or price, the mode may be relevant. The analyst should also inspect distribution shape, extreme observations, data type, group structure, and the consequences of misinterpretation. In many important reports, showing more than one measure is better than forcing a single statistic to serve several purposes.
Study instruction:
- For a property, salary, and service-time dataset, state the decision objective and justify the most suitable central measure for each.
Key point:
- Select the statistic that answers the decision question, not the one that looks most favorable.
- Reporting several measures can provide a more balanced view of skewed data.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
32. Q: How should a property buyer interpret a neighborhood's reported average income before making a purchase decision?
A: Show answer
The buyer should ask whether the figure is a mean, median, or mode and which households are included. A few wealthy residents can raise the mean without indicating that most residents have comparable incomes. The buyer should also consider the income distribution, household definitions, geographic boundaries, time period, and source of the data. The average alone does not establish property value, affordability, neighborhood stability, or the financial circumstances of a typical resident. For a decision involving affordability or local demand, the median and relevant income bands may be more informative, while other evidence such as comparable property sales, housing costs, and local economic conditions is also necessary.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate a real-estate advertisement that cites neighborhood income and list the statistical and market information needed before relying on the claim.
Key point:
- A high mean income does not prove that most households are wealthy.
- Property decisions require a defined population and evidence beyond a single average.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
33. Q: How can an advertiser use an unspecified average to make a product, service, or audience appear more attractive?
A: Show answer
An advertiser may select the measure that produces the most favorable figure and use the generic word average without disclosing whether it is a mean, median, or mode. For example, a mean income for a readership may be elevated by a small number of high-income subscribers, making the audience appear wealthier to potential advertisers. The figure may be correctly calculated but still encourage an unsupported inference about the typical reader. A critical reader should ask who was surveyed, who responded, what was measured, which average was used, when the data were collected, and whether the method is independently described. The same discipline applies to claims about average savings, returns, customer spending, or performance.
Study instruction:
- Select a marketing claim that uses the word average and identify the missing information needed to evaluate its meaning and relevance.
Key point:
- A favorable average can be selected to shape an audience's impression.
- Statistical transparency requires disclosure of the measure and underlying population.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
34. Q: What questions should a reader ask when a company announces its average employee pay?
A: Show answer
The reader should ask: Is the figure a mean, median, or mode? Which workers are included? Are owners and executives included? Does remuneration include bonuses, benefits, commissions, or profit distributions? Are part-time and full-time employees treated consistently? Is the figure annual, weekly, hourly, or based on a full-time equivalent? What period is covered, and is the comparison with earlier periods based on the same definitions? How many employees are represented, and is the distribution highly skewed? A report that provides the median, mean, pay bands, and workforce definitions is more informative than one unexplained figure. These questions help distinguish an informative compensation disclosure from a presentation that is technically correct but incomplete.
Study instruction:
- Use this checklist to review a published remuneration statement and identify which claims can and cannot be supported by the information disclosed.
Key point:
- The meaning of average pay depends on the people, amounts, and period included.
- A transparent report explains its calculation and gives enough context for interpretation.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
35. Q: Why is it important to distinguish a statistical estimate from the actual population value?
A: Show answer
A statistic calculated from a sample is an estimate of a population characteristic, not necessarily the exact value for every member of the population. The uncertainty depends on the sampling design, sample size, variability, nonresponse, measurement quality, and other sources of error. A report should explain whether a number comes from a census, administrative records, or a sample survey. When a sampling estimate is used, readers may need information about its standard error, confidence interval, margin of error, and design limitations. A precise-looking number does not guarantee exact knowledge of the population. The chapter's discussion of income figures highlights why readers should ask how the data were collected and how reliable the estimate is.
Study instruction:
- Find a published income estimate and identify whether it is a sample estimate or a population count, then locate its uncertainty information.
Key point:
- A sample statistic is an estimate and should not automatically be treated as the exact population value.
- Precision and uncertainty must be interpreted in light of the data collection method.
Source links:
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
- Statistics Canada: Exploring Measures of Central Tendency
36. Q: What is a margin of error, and why does it matter when interpreting a reported average or median?
A: Show answer
A margin of error expresses a range of sampling uncertainty around an estimate under specified assumptions and a stated confidence level. It helps readers understand that a sample-based estimate is not known with unlimited precision. The margin depends on factors such as sample size, variability, sampling design, and the confidence level used. It does not automatically account for every possible source of error, such as nonresponse bias, inaccurate answers, coverage problems, or incorrect definitions. A narrow margin of error cannot rescue a poorly defined or systematically biased sample. Readers should therefore interpret uncertainty information together with the sampling method and data-quality disclosures rather than treating it as a guarantee of accuracy.
Study instruction:
- Review a survey result with a reported margin of error and identify which uncertainty it addresses and which data-quality problems may remain outside it.
Key point:
- A margin of error describes sampling uncertainty under stated assumptions.
- It does not eliminate non-sampling errors or guarantee representativeness.
Source links:
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
- Statistics Canada: Exploring Measures of Central Tendency
37. Q: How can a small number of extreme values affect an average even when the sample is large?
A: Show answer
The effect of an extreme value on the mean depends on its magnitude relative to the other observations and on the total number of observations. A larger sample may dilute the influence of one extreme value, but it does not make the mean immune to an exceptionally large observation or a heavy-tailed distribution. If the data-generating process regularly produces extreme values, a large sample may still have a mean that is far from the median and from the value experienced by most observations. Analysts should examine the tail, report appropriate measures of spread, and consider whether the mean is the right summary. Sample size alone is not a substitute for understanding the distribution.
Study instruction:
- Calculate the mean and median for the same dataset at two sample sizes and examine how adding an extreme value affects each measure.
Key point:
- A large sample does not automatically remove the influence of extreme observations.
- Distribution shape and tail behavior remain important when choosing a measure of centre.
Source links:
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
38. Q: What is the danger of using an average without explaining the source and quality of the underlying data?
A: Show answer
A central measure can be calculated perfectly from data that are incomplete, outdated, inconsistently defined, incorrectly recorded, or collected from an unrepresentative group. The arithmetic does not reveal whether the observations cover the intended population or whether values were measured consistently. For income data, respondents may report gross income, net income, salary only, or total income from multiple sources. If the definition is inconsistent, the resulting mean or median may not be comparable. A credible report should explain the source, collection method, reference period, definitions, exclusions, missing data treatment, and known limitations. Statistical interpretation begins with the quality and meaning of the observations, not merely with the formula.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a data-quality review for an income dataset covering definitions, completeness, currency, reference period, missing values, and validation of extreme records.
Key point:
- Accurate arithmetic cannot compensate for poor or inconsistent source data.
- Data provenance and measurement definitions are essential to trustworthy averages.
Source links:
- Statistics Canada: Exploring Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
39. Q: How should a manager investigate a large difference between the mean and median in an organization's salary report?
A: Show answer
The manager should first confirm that both measures were calculated from the same employee population, period, currency, and remuneration definition. Next, inspect the salary distribution, identify high and low values, and determine whether the difference is driven by a small number of executives, owners, special payments, or data errors. Review the treatment of bonuses, part-time staff, vacant positions, and one-off payments. If the values are valid, the difference may indicate a genuinely skewed pay structure rather than a calculation defect. The manager should then communicate the mean and median with appropriate context and consider pay bands or percentiles to explain the distribution. Any corrections to the dataset should be supported by source evidence.
Study instruction:
- Build an investigation sequence for a salary report with a mean far above the median and identify the evidence required at each step.
Key point:
- A large mean–median gap is a signal to investigate distribution and definitions.
- Do not assume the difference is an error or manipulate the data to eliminate it.

- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency

40. Q: How can a finance or audit reviewer detect the selective presentation of averages in a management report?
A: Show answer
The reviewer should trace each reported average to the underlying data and recalculate it independently. The review should establish the measure used, the population and period, the numerator and denominator, the treatment of owners and executives, the inclusion of bonuses or other payments, and any exclusions. Compare the reported measure with the median, mode, distribution bands, and relevant prior periods. Investigate whether the report changes definitions between periods or combines groups in a way that creates an attractive result. Check that the narrative does not claim that most employees receive the mean or that a mean increase proves individual pay rates increased. Document any limitations and obtain explanations for unusual choices.
Study instruction:
- Perform a mini-audit of a compensation dashboard by reconciling totals, counts, group definitions, and reported central measures to source records.
Key point:
- Independent recalculation and transparent population definitions help detect misleading statistical presentation.
- The narrative must not claim more than the underlying statistic supports.
Source links:
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
41. Q: What is the difference between a statistically correct average and an honest statistical presentation?
A: Show answer
A statistically correct average follows the arithmetic definition of the selected measure. An honest presentation also makes clear what the measure represents, how the data were obtained, who was included, and what limitations affect interpretation. A technically correct mean can be used deceptively if the audience is encouraged to interpret it as the typical person's income when the distribution is highly skewed. Conversely, a median can also mislead if the report omits important information about total amounts or the upper and lower tails. Ethical reporting requires selecting measures that suit the question, disclosing relevant definitions, and avoiding language that implies unsupported conclusions. Transparency is not achieved merely by ensuring that the calculation is mathematically valid.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite a technically accurate but potentially misleading statistical statement so that it identifies the measure, population, period, and limitation.
Key point:
- Mathematical correctness and responsible interpretation are separate requirements.
- The presentation must not encourage conclusions that the data do not support.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
42. Q: How should a report present mean, median, and mode together without confusing readers?
A: Show answer
The report should define each measure in plain language and explain why each is relevant to the question. A compact table can show the mean, median, mode or modal class, sample size, period, population definition, and a suitable measure of spread. A histogram or frequency distribution can help readers see whether the data are symmetrical, skewed, or multimodal. If the mean is much higher than the median, the report should explain that high values may be pulling the mean upward rather than implying that most observations are near the mean. The report should avoid calling all three measures simply the average without qualification. The goal is to show complementary information rather than select one statistic as universally superior.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a one-page statistical summary that reports central tendency, spread, population, period, and a brief interpretation of the distribution.
Key point:
- Reporting multiple central measures can clarify different features of the same dataset.
- Labels and explanations should make each statistic's meaning explicit.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
43. Q: What are the limitations of using the median as the only measure of income distribution?
A: Show answer
The median identifies the midpoint but does not reveal how far incomes are spread above or below it, how unequal the distribution is, or how much total income is received by the group. Two populations can have the same median while one has relatively similar incomes and the other has very large differences between the lowest and highest earners. The median also does not describe the most common value or the aggregate payroll. For analysis of living standards, compensation policy, or inequality, it may be necessary to add percentiles, income bands, the mean, measures of dispersion, and subgroup comparisons. A median is often useful, but it is not a complete substitute for examining the full distribution.
Study instruction:
- Create two income distributions with the same median but different upper and lower tails and explain what additional statistics reveal the differences.
Key point:
- The median describes the midpoint, not the entire distribution.
- Additional measures are required to understand spread, inequality, and total amounts.
Source links:
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
44. Q: What are the limitations of using the mean as the only measure of income distribution?
A: Show answer
The mean does not show whether incomes are concentrated around the average or spread widely across the population. It can be strongly affected by extreme observations and may be far above the income of most households in a right-skewed distribution. It also does not identify the most common income or the midpoint. A mean is valuable when the question concerns total income divided by the number of people or households, but it should not be used as a substitute for a distributional analysis. Reporting the median, percentiles, ranges, and group breakdowns can help readers understand how the total is distributed.
Study instruction:
- Identify a management decision for which the mean is appropriate and another for which the mean alone would be insufficient.
Key point:
- The mean is useful for aggregate analysis but can misrepresent the experience of a typical member in skewed data.
- Distributional context is necessary for interpreting income averages responsibly.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
45. Q: What common mistakes should be avoided when calculating or interpreting mean, median, and mode?
A: Show answer
Common mistakes include using the word average without naming the measure; calculating a median before sorting the data; forgetting to average the two middle observations when the count is even; treating the mode as necessarily unique; assuming that the mean describes most people; deleting valid outliers without justification; averaging group means without appropriate weights; comparing statistics based on different populations; and ignoring changes in definitions between periods. Another mistake is selecting whichever measure produces the most favorable narrative rather than the measure that answers the analytical question. Good practice includes checking the data, documenting the method, inspecting the distribution, and explaining limitations.
Study instruction:
- Review a set of statistical calculations and identify at least five potential errors in the method, population definition, or interpretation.
Key point:
- Correct calculation requires correct method, data, and interpretation.
- A statistic should be selected for analytical relevance rather than persuasive convenience.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
46. Q: How should an analyst handle a situation in which the mean and median tell apparently different stories?
A: Show answer
The analyst should not treat the difference as a contradiction that must be eliminated. The mean describes the arithmetic balance point, while the median describes the middle position. A large gap may be evidence of skewness, extreme values, or a mixture of different subgroups. The analyst should verify the data and definitions, inspect the distribution, examine the tails, and determine which question each statistic answers. If the objective is to understand the typical midpoint, the median may be central; if it is to calculate average cost per person or total resources divided by a count, the mean may be necessary. Both may be reported with an explanation of why they differ.
Study instruction:
- Write an interpretation of a dataset in which the mean is substantially higher than the median, avoiding the assumption that either measure is automatically wrong.
Key point:
- Different central measures can provide complementary and valid information.
- A gap between mean and median should prompt distribution analysis, not selective reporting.
Source links:
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
47. Q: How can an organization explain a mean–median difference to non-specialist decision-makers?
A: Show answer
The explanation should use a simple example and connect the measures to the decision. For instance, explain that the mean is the total amount divided equally across all people, while the median is the middle person when everyone is arranged from lowest to highest. A small number of very high salaries can raise the mean even though the middle salary does not change much. A chart showing the distribution can make this distinction easier to understand. Avoid technical language without explanation, and do not describe the median as the salary everyone earns or the mean as the salary most people receive. The communication should state which measure is being used and why it is appropriate for the decision.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a short explanation of mean and median using a five-person salary example and present it to someone unfamiliar with statistics.
Key point:
- Plain-language explanations should distinguish arithmetic balance from the middle observation.
- Visual examples help prevent the word average from being misunderstood.
Source links:
- Statistics Canada: Exploring Measures of Central Tendency
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
48. Q: What practical decision process can be used before publishing an average?
A: Show answer
First, define the question and the population to be described. Second, verify the source data, measurement units, reference period, missing records, and inclusion rules. Third, inspect the distribution using a table or graph and identify skewness, multiple peaks, and extreme values. Fourth, select the mean, median, mode, or more than one measure according to the decision objective and data type. Fifth, calculate the statistic independently and reconcile it to the underlying values and count. Sixth, consider whether a measure of spread, percentiles, or subgroup analysis is needed. Finally, disclose the measure, population, period, data source, and relevant limitations in the report. This process reduces the risk that a valid calculation will be interpreted incorrectly.
Study instruction:
- Apply the seven-step process to a real dataset before preparing a management presentation or public statement.
Key point:
- Define, validate, inspect, select, calculate, contextualize, and disclose.
- The quality of a statistical statement depends on the full analytical process.
Source links:
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
49. Q: How can an organization prevent misleading averages from entering dashboards and performance reports?
A: Show answer
The organization should standardize the definitions and calculation methods used in recurring reports. Data dictionaries should specify the population, unit of analysis, period, remuneration or transaction components, treatment of missing values, and selected measure. Report templates should label mean, median, and mode explicitly rather than using a generic average field. Automated checks can reconcile totals and counts, flag unusual differences between mean and median, and identify changes in population definitions. Reviewers should verify that comparisons use consistent methods and that narrative conclusions are supported by the data. Governance should also require documentation of exclusions, adjustments, and any use of weighted or robust measures. Automation can support these controls but cannot replace analytical judgment.
Study instruction:
- Design a dashboard control checklist that standardizes metric definitions and flags unexplained changes in population or calculation methods.
Key point:
- Standardized definitions and review controls reduce inconsistent statistical reporting.
- Automated alerts should prompt investigation rather than automatically change valid observations.
Source links:
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
- Statistics Canada: Exploring Measures of Central Tendency
50. Q: What are the most important lessons from Chapter 2, “The Well-Chosen Average”?
A: Show answer
The chapter demonstrates that the word average can conceal important differences between the mean, median, and mode. A mean can be raised by a small number of very high incomes, while the median continues to describe the middle observation and the mode identifies the most common value. In skewed distributions, these measures may be far apart, so an unexplained average can create a misleading impression even when the arithmetic is correct. The chapter's salary examples also show that changing the population, grouping, or classification of remuneration can change the apparent story. Readers should ask what is being averaged, who is included, which measure is used, and how reliable and comparable the data are. A responsible analyst chooses the measure that fits the question, examines the distribution, and explains the limitations rather than selecting a statistic merely because it looks favorable.
Study instruction:
- Review the chapter and prepare a worked example showing how the mean, median, and mode can tell different stories about one income dataset, including the population definition and distribution shape.
Key point:
- Never accept an unspecified average without identifying the measure and the data behind it.
- Mean, median, and mode are complementary tools, not interchangeable labels.
- Statistical honesty requires transparent definitions, consistent comparisons, and interpretation that matches the evidence.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Measures of Central Tendency
- NIST/SEMATECH: Measures of Location
- Statistics Canada: Exploring Measures of Central Tendency
