Table 3
Statistical Significance, Sampling, and the Figures That Are Not There
1. Q: Why can missing statistical details make an apparently convincing claim misleading?
A: Show answer
A numerical claim can be technically accurate yet give a false impression when important context is omitted. A headline such as “23% fewer cavities” sounds persuasive, but its meaning depends on the number of people tested, how they were selected, how many trials were conducted, what comparison was used, how outcomes were measured, and whether the result could reasonably have arisen by chance. A small group may produce a large percentage change from only a few observations. A reported average may conceal wide variation, while a chart without scales or units may create an impression of magnitude without communicating it. The missing information is not decorative detail: it is what allows a reader to judge whether the claim is reliable, relevant, and applicable beyond the reported cases.
Study instruction:
- Take a numerical headline and list the sample, comparison, denominator, time period, uncertainty, and distribution information needed before accepting it.
Key point:
- A statistic cannot be evaluated responsibly without its context and uncertainty.
- Precision of wording or presentation is not proof of strength of evidence.
Source links:
-
2. Q: Why is an inadequate sample a central weakness in a statistical claim?
A: Show answer
A sample that is too small is often highly sensitive to random variation. One or two unusual observations can materially change its percentage, average, or apparent difference. In the toothpaste example, a dozen users could show a large apparent reduction in cavities even if the product has no real advantage. The result may describe those particular people during that particular period, but it provides weak evidence about the wider population. Small samples also make it difficult to estimate variability and detect modest effects. The problem is not that every small sample is automatically useless; a small, carefully designed qualitative or pilot study may answer a limited question. The problem arises when a small sample is used to support a broad, precise, or causal claim that its design cannot justify.
Study instruction:
- Recalculate how a change of one or two observations affects a percentage when the denominator is 12, 100, and 1,000.
Key point:
- Small samples are vulnerable to large chance fluctuations.
- The strength of a conclusion must match the scope and limitations of the sample.
Source links:
-
3. Q: Is a large sample always sufficient to make a statistical conclusion reliable?
A: Show answer
No. Sample size reduces some forms of random error, but it does not automatically correct selection bias, measurement error, nonresponse, confounding, or an unsuitable comparison. A survey of one million people recruited only from a specialist online forum may be less representative of the general population than a much smaller probability sample drawn carefully from the target population. Likewise, a very large dataset can estimate the wrong quantity with great precision if the outcome is poorly defined or the data are systematically incomplete. Reliability therefore depends on both quantity and design. Readers should ask how participants entered the sample, who was excluded, whether measurements were consistent, and whether the sample reflects the population to which the conclusion is being applied.
Study instruction:
- Compare a large convenience sample with a smaller probability sample and identify which sources of error each design can and cannot address.
Key point:
- Large numbers can reduce sampling variability but cannot repair systematic bias.
- Representativeness and measurement quality matter alongside sample size.
Source links:
-
4. Q: How can chance alone produce an apparently impressive improvement in a small experiment?
A: Show answer
When a study has few observations, ordinary random variation can produce unusually high or low results. If a small group records its cavity count before and after changing toothpaste, the later count may be lower simply because of chance, changes in diet, different observation conditions, or natural fluctuation. If investigators repeat the exercise with many small groups but publicize only the group showing a striking improvement, the headline can appear compelling even though the product has no genuine effect. The reported result is not necessarily fabricated; the distortion comes from selecting and emphasizing an extreme outcome while hiding the unsuccessful attempts. A reader therefore needs to know the full testing process, not only the most attractive result.
Study instruction:
- Explain how repeated small trials and selective publication can create a persuasive result even when the tested intervention has no effect.
Key point:
- Random variation can create extreme results in small samples.
- Reporting only the most favorable trial conceals the full evidence.
Source links:
-
5. Q: What does the coin-toss example demonstrate about random variation?
A: Show answer
A fair coin has a theoretical probability of one-half for heads on each independent toss, but a short sequence need not contain exactly half heads. Eight heads in ten tosses is unusual but possible; it does not establish that the coin has an 80% chance of landing heads. With more independent tosses, the observed proportion will generally tend to be closer to one-half, although it need not equal one-half exactly. The example separates the probability model from the result of a particular sample. A sample proportion is an estimate subject to uncertainty, not a guaranteed reproduction of the underlying probability. The same reasoning applies to small product tests, medical studies, surveys, and other experiments.
Study instruction:
- Simulate short and long sequences of coin tosses and compare the variation in observed proportions across repeated samples.
Key point:
- A short-run result can differ substantially from the underlying probability.
- One unusual sample does not establish a new general rule.
Source links:
-
6. Q: What is the law of large numbers, and what does it not guarantee?
A: Show answer
The law of large numbers describes how, under appropriate conditions, the average or proportion calculated from repeated observations tends to approach its expected value as the number of observations grows. It explains why a large number of independent coin tosses usually produces a heads proportion closer to one-half than a very short sequence. It does not say that every additional observation must move the running average closer to the expected value, that the result will become exactly equal to it, or that a biased process becomes fair through repetition. The observations must also satisfy relevant assumptions, such as a stable probability model and suitable independence conditions. The principle is about long-run behavior, not a promise that short sequences will balance neatly.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish convergence of a sample proportion over many trials from the incorrect belief that every short sequence must contain equal numbers of outcomes.
Key point:
- Larger samples generally stabilize estimates under suitable assumptions.
- The law of large numbers does not eliminate bias or guarantee exact equality.
Source links:
-
7. Q: Why can the number of observations needed for a useful study not be determined by a universal rule?
A: Show answer
The required sample size depends on what the study is trying to detect and how variable or rare the outcome is. Relevant factors include the size of the effect that matters, the natural variability of the outcome, the desired precision, the chosen significance level, statistical power, the study design, the number of groups, and expected nonresponse or loss to follow-up. A study of a common outcome may need a different sample from a study of a rare event. A study seeking to detect a very small improvement generally needs more information than one seeking to detect a large effect. Sample size should therefore be planned around the research question and analysis, rather than selected because a number looks impressive or because a previous study used it.
Study instruction:
- Identify the outcome, target effect, expected variability, confidence or precision goal, and likely attrition before proposing a sample size.
Key point:
- “Enough” depends on the question, outcome frequency, variability, and required precision.
- Sample size should be justified before results are examined.
Source links:
-
8. Q: Why did the polio-vaccine example illustrate a study that was large in appearance but statistically uninformative?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes an experiment involving 450 vaccinated children and 680 unvaccinated controls during a period when paralytic polio was uncommon. No recognizable cases appeared in either group. Although the total number of children sounded substantial, the expected number of cases at the underlying incidence was only about two. With so few expected events, the study could not reliably distinguish a protective effect from the absence of disease by chance. The example shows that a sample must be large enough relative to the frequency of the outcome, not merely large in absolute terms. It also demonstrates why researchers must estimate event rates and plan for adequate statistical information before beginning a study.
Study instruction:
- Explain why a study of a rare outcome may need far more participants or longer observation than a study of a common outcome.
Key point:
- A large participant count can still yield too few outcome events for a reliable conclusion.
- Expected event frequency is essential to study planning.
Source links:
-
9. Q: What is the base rate of an event, and why should readers examine it?
A: Show answer
A base rate is the underlying frequency or probability of an event in the relevant population before considering a particular test result or intervention. If an outcome is rare, a study may observe no cases even when the intervention has no effect. If a condition occurs in only a small proportion of people, a screening test can also produce many false positives unless its accuracy is sufficiently high and interpreted with the base rate in mind. Ignoring the base rate can make a result seem more informative than it is. Readers should ask how common the outcome normally is, whether the study population has a different risk profile, and how many events would be expected without the intervention.
Study instruction:
- For a rare-event claim, calculate the expected number of events from the population size and baseline rate before interpreting the reported result.
Key point:
- The underlying frequency of an outcome changes how much evidence a sample can provide.
- Rare outcomes require particular care in study design and interpretation.
Source links:
-
10. Q: Why is a suitable control or comparison group important in an experiment?
A: Show answer
A comparison group helps researchers judge what would have happened without the intervention or under an alternative condition. Observing that a group improved after using a product does not establish that the product caused the improvement. The change could reflect natural recovery, seasonal effects, changes in behavior, measurement differences, or regression toward typical values after an unusually poor baseline. A well-designed control group helps separate the intervention's effect from these other influences. Random assignment, when feasible and ethical, can reduce systematic differences between groups. The comparison must also be measured over the same period and under comparable conditions. Without a credible counterfactual, a before-and-after difference can be wrongly attributed to the product or treatment.
Study instruction:
- For an intervention claim, identify the treatment group, comparison group, allocation method, baseline measures, and alternative explanations for change.
Key point:
- Improvement after an intervention does not by itself prove that the intervention caused it.
- A credible comparison is central to causal interpretation.
Source links:
-
11. Q: What is a statistical significance test intended to help a reader assess?
A: Show answer
A significance test evaluates how compatible observed data are with a specified null model, using a test statistic and assumptions about how the data were generated. It helps quantify whether an observed difference would be surprising if the null hypothesis and model assumptions were true. The chapter calls the missing significance information a little figure that helps readers judge whether an apparent result may be due to chance. In modern interpretation, a test does not prove that an effect is real, establish that the alternative explanation is true, or measure the practical value of a result. Its meaning depends on the model, design, data quality, and analysis plan. Statistical significance is one component of evidence, not a substitute for sound reasoning.
Study instruction:
- State the null hypothesis and explain what a significance test evaluates before interpreting a reported statistical result.
Key point:
- Significance tests assess data compatibility with a model under specified assumptions.
- A significant result is not proof of causation or practical importance.
Source links:
-
12. Q: What is a p-value, and what does it not mean?
A: Show answer
A p-value is the probability, calculated under a specified null hypothesis and statistical model, of obtaining a test statistic at least as extreme as the one observed. It is not the probability that the null hypothesis is true, the probability that the result happened “by chance” in an everyday sense, or the probability that a claimed treatment works. A small p-value indicates that the observed data would be relatively unusual under the null model, assuming the design and analysis assumptions hold. It does not reveal the size of the effect, the quality of the measurements, the likelihood of replication, or the practical value of the difference. Readers should examine the effect estimate, uncertainty interval, design, and full analysis alongside the p-value.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite three common p-value misinterpretations into accurate statements conditional on the null hypothesis and model assumptions.
Key point:
- A p-value is calculated assuming a null model; it is not the probability that the hypothesis is true.
- Effect size and study quality must be assessed separately.
Source links:
-
13. Q: How does a significance level differ from an observed p-value?
A: Show answer
The significance level, often denoted alpha, is a threshold chosen before examining the study results to define the tolerated long-run rate of false positive decisions under the null model. The p-value is calculated from the observed data. A common decision rule compares the p-value with alpha: if the p-value is at or below the preselected threshold, the result is called statistically significant under that procedure. The two quantities are not interchangeable. Choosing a threshold after seeing the data, trying many analyses until one passes it, or failing to disclose the choices made can invalidate the apparent evidential strength. A transparent report should state the planned threshold and disclose whether the analysis was exploratory or confirmatory.
Study instruction:
- Practice identifying which value was selected before analysis and which value was calculated from the observed data.
Key point:
- Alpha is a preselected decision threshold; the p-value is data-dependent.
- Post hoc threshold selection weakens the credibility of the decision process.
Source links:
-
14. Q: What does a 5% significance level mean, and why is it not a 95% guarantee that a claim is true?
A: Show answer
A 5% significance level sets a decision threshold of 0.05 for a specified testing procedure. Under repeated use of a correctly calibrated procedure when the null hypothesis is true and its assumptions hold, the long-run probability of a false positive rejection is controlled at about 5%. It does not mean that a particular conclusion has a 95% probability of being correct. That probability would require a different framework and additional assumptions about prior plausibility and alternative hypotheses. The chapter's simplified explanation uses the idea of 19 chances out of 20, but readers should avoid translating that shorthand into certainty about a single study. The threshold is a convention, not a universal guarantee of evidence quality.
Study instruction:
- Explain the long-run interpretation of a 5% threshold and identify at least two things it does not guarantee about an individual study.
Key point:
- A 5% significance level is a property of a testing procedure under its assumptions.
- It is not a 95% probability that the research claim is true.
Source links:
-
15. Q: Why might a researcher use a 1% significance level instead of a 5% level?
A: Show answer
A lower significance threshold makes the test more demanding before rejecting the null hypothesis and can reduce the long-run false positive rate for that test when its assumptions hold. It may be appropriate when false positive conclusions would be especially costly, such as in some high-consequence scientific or regulatory settings. However, lowering alpha without increasing the sample or improving measurement can also make it harder to detect a genuine effect, increasing the risk of a false negative. The threshold should be justified by the study's purpose and the relative costs of different errors. A 1% result is not automatically more important or more reliable in every sense than a 5% result; design, power, effect size, and transparency still matter.
Study instruction:
- Compare the consequences of false positives and false negatives in a concrete decision and explain how that trade-off affects the chosen threshold.
Key point:
- A stricter threshold can reduce false positive decisions but may reduce sensitivity.
- Threshold selection should reflect the decision context and be planned in advance.
Source links:
-
16. Q: Why should statistical significance be distinguished from practical significance?
A: Show answer
Statistical significance concerns how unusual the data are under a statistical model; practical significance concerns whether the size of the effect matters in the real decision context. A very large study can detect a tiny difference that has negligible health, financial, or operational value. Conversely, a potentially important effect may fail to reach a conventional significance threshold because the study is small or imprecise. Decision-makers should examine the magnitude of the effect, uncertainty, costs, benefits, risks, and consequences of acting or not acting. In the toothpaste example, a percentage headline is not enough: readers need the actual cavity counts, baseline rate, follow-up period, and whether the reduction is meaningful to users.
Study instruction:
- For a reported significant result, identify the absolute effect, baseline outcome, uncertainty, and practical consequence before deciding whether it matters.
Key point:
- A statistically detectable difference may be too small to matter in practice.
- Practical importance cannot be inferred from a p-value alone.
Source links:
-
17. Q: How do confidence intervals add information beyond a single significance decision?
A: Show answer
A confidence interval gives a range of parameter values compatible with the data under a specified statistical procedure. It communicates precision and uncertainty more directly than a binary label such as significant or not significant. A narrow interval generally indicates a more precise estimate than a wide interval, although precision does not guarantee absence of bias. In a frequentist interpretation, a 95% confidence procedure is designed so that, over repeated comparable samples under its assumptions, 95% of intervals constructed by that procedure would contain the true parameter. It is not correct to say that a particular fixed parameter has a 95% probability of lying in the observed interval under that interpretation. Readers should inspect the interval's width, endpoints, and substantive meaning.
Study instruction:
- Interpret a confidence interval by describing its estimate, lower and upper bounds, precision, and practical implications without assigning a probability to the fixed parameter.
Key point:
- Intervals communicate uncertainty and plausible effect sizes more richly than a yes-or-no test label.
- A narrow interval can still be biased if the design is flawed.
Source links:
-
18. Q: Why is the range or spread of observations important when an average is reported?
A: Show answer
An average compresses many observations into one summary and can conceal how different the individual values are. Two populations may have the same mean but very different ranges, standard deviations, shapes, and proportions in important subgroups. If a report gives only an average, readers cannot tell whether most observations cluster near it or whether the mean is influenced by a small number of extreme values. The chapter's housing and temperature examples show how a single average can hide materially different conditions. A responsible report should provide a suitable measure of spread, distribution, or subgroup variation alongside the average, selected according to the data type and decision being made.
Study instruction:
- Find two datasets with the same mean but different distributions and explain why the same average leads to different decisions.
Key point:
- An average does not describe the full distribution.
- Spread and subgroup variation are essential to interpretation.
Source links:
-
19. Q: What is the difference between the mean, median, and range, and why should a report identify its summary measure?
A: Show answer
The mean is the arithmetic total divided by the number of observations. The median is the middle value after observations are ordered, or the midpoint of the two central values when the count is even. The range is the difference between the maximum and minimum values. These measures answer different questions. The mean uses every value but can be pulled by extreme observations. The median is less sensitive to extremes and may better describe a skewed distribution. The range communicates the distance between endpoints but ignores how observations are distributed between them. A report should state which average or spread measure is being used and provide additional distribution information when the decision requires it.
Study instruction:
- Calculate the mean, median, and range for a skewed dataset and explain which measure best represents a typical observation.
Key point:
- Different summary measures reveal different features of the data.
- Never interpret an unspecified average as though its meaning were obvious.
Source links:
-
20. Q: How did the average family size of 3.6 illustrate the danger of designing for an abstract average?
A: Show answer
A family size of 3.6 is a mathematical average, not a household that exists as a literal design category. If builders translate it automatically into a standard two-bedroom house, they may overlook one-person and two-person households as well as families with more than four members. The chapter cites a distribution in which only 45% of households were three- or four-person families, while 35% had one or two people and 20% had more than four. The exact historical percentages should not be treated as current demographic facts, but the analytical lesson remains: a mean can conceal the mix of real groups. Planning should use the distribution and the needs of different segments, not merely the central average.
Study instruction:
- Convert an average into a frequency distribution and identify which population segments would be underserved by a one-size-fits-all design.
Key point:
- A population average is not necessarily representative of the majority or any actual individual.
- Design decisions should consider the distribution and distinct user needs.
Source links:
-
21. Q: Why can an average be misleading when planning products, housing, services, or public policy?
A: Show answer
Planning around an average can obscure the people at both ends of the distribution and the different needs of subgroups. A service designed for the “average” user may be unsuitable for children, older adults, people with disabilities, large households, small households, or people with uncommon but important requirements. The average may also conceal geographic, income, age, or usage differences. A better process begins by defining the target population, examining its distribution, segmenting users where meaningful, and evaluating the costs of failing to serve each group. Averages remain useful for some planning calculations, but they should be accompanied by ranges, percentiles, subgroup estimates, and explicit service objectives.
Study instruction:
- Choose a service or product and identify at least three user segments whose needs could be hidden by a single average.
Key point:
- Average-based planning can systematically overlook minority groups.
- Distribution-aware design improves fit and exposes trade-offs.
Source links:
-
22. Q: Why is an average calculated across a group often unsuitable for predicting one individual's outcome?
A: Show answer
A group average describes a central feature of a population, not the precise trajectory of a particular person. Individuals may differ in genetics, environment, behavior, starting conditions, and timing. The chapter's child-height example explains that children follow different growth patterns: some grow slowly before accelerating, some grow quickly and then level off, and others grow more steadily. A chart based on average growth across many children may describe the group adequately but perform poorly as a prediction for one child. Individual prediction requires information relevant to that individual and a model validated for that purpose. Readers should distinguish group-level description from individual-level forecasting and avoid assuming that an average trajectory applies to every member.
Study instruction:
- Identify whether a reported statistic describes a group or predicts an individual, and list the additional information needed for an individual prediction.
Key point:
- Group averages do not automatically predict individual outcomes.
- Individual variation must be considered in personal decisions.
Source links:
-
23. Q: What is the difference between a developmental norm and a prediction about an individual child?
A: Show answer
A developmental norm summarizes observed patterns in a reference population, such as the ages at which children commonly reach a milestone. It is descriptive and depends on how the reference group was selected and how the milestone was defined. It does not establish the exact age at which every child should achieve the milestone or predict an individual's future development with certainty. Children vary in their development, and a single timing difference may not indicate a problem. A norm should therefore be interpreted using an appropriate range, repeated observations, clinical context, and professional assessment when concerns exist. The chapter's warning is against converting a population statistic into an individual judgment without considering variation or context.
Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between a population reference point, a percentile, a developmental range, and an individual clinical assessment.
Key point:
- A norm describes a population pattern; it is not an individual deadline.
- Developmental concerns require context and appropriate professional evaluation.
Source links:
-
24. Q: Why should readers distinguish the statistical meaning of normal from the moral meaning of desirable?
A: Show answer
In statistics, normal may refer to what is common, typical, or within a defined reference distribution. It does not automatically mean morally good, healthy in every context, legally acceptable, or desirable. The chapter's discussion of developmental norms and Kinsey's report illustrates how readers may mistake a descriptive statement about frequency for an endorsement. The reverse error is also possible: something uncommon is not necessarily bad or defective. Researchers should define what they mean by normal and avoid implying value judgments from frequency data alone. When a decision concerns health, ethics, or policy, descriptive evidence must be considered alongside relevant clinical standards, ethical principles, and explicit normative criteria.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite a statement containing the word normal to clarify whether it describes frequency, a reference range, a clinical judgment, or a value judgment.
Key point:
- Common does not mean good, and uncommon does not mean defective.
- Descriptive statistics cannot settle ethical or normative questions by themselves.
Source links:
-
25. Q: How can missing reference ranges cause unnecessary concern or false reassurance?
A: Show answer
A single milestone, score, or measurement may be presented as though it were a strict boundary between normal and abnormal. Without the distribution or reference range, readers may interpret a small difference as evidence of a serious problem. Others may feel falsely reassured because their result matches the average even though important symptoms or contextual factors remain. Reference ranges should be interpreted in relation to the population, measurement method, age or other relevant characteristics, and purpose of the assessment. In clinical settings, laboratory reference intervals are not the same as diagnostic thresholds, and a result inside a reference interval does not automatically exclude disease. Professional interpretation is needed when health decisions are involved.
Study instruction:
- Examine a reported norm and identify the reference population, range, measurement method, and limits on individual interpretation.
Key point:
- A central value alone cannot show the boundaries or diversity of a population.
- Reference ranges support context but do not replace professional judgment.
Source links:
-
26. Q: What is the danger of reporting a mean without identifying the distribution behind it?
A: Show answer
A mean can conceal skewness, clusters, outliers, and important subgroups. For example, an average income may be high because a small number of people earn very large amounts while most earn much less. An average waiting time may hide a group of customers who wait far longer than others. Averages can also conceal multimodal distributions in which two or more distinct groups exist. When the distribution matters, a report should show suitable visualizations, quantiles, subgroup summaries, and measures of spread. The appropriate description depends on the question: a mean may be useful for total resource planning, while a median and upper percentiles may better describe a typical or worst-case experience.
Study instruction:
- Inspect a skewed or clustered dataset and choose summary measures that reveal both the typical value and important tail behavior.
Key point:
- The same mean can arise from very different distributions.
- Select summary measures according to the decision and shape of the data.
Source links:
-
27. Q: Why must a statistical claim define exactly what is being measured?
A: Show answer
A claim is only interpretable when its key terms have operational definitions. Words such as improvement, success, access, availability, hardness, energy, or effectiveness may have several meanings. If a company says a treatment triples hardness, readers need to know the material tested, the hardness scale, the baseline, the test conditions, and whether the comparison is with the same material before treatment or with a different product. Without those details, the claim may be technically impressive but impossible to evaluate. Operational definitions turn broad language into observable measures and make comparisons reproducible. They also expose whether the reported metric actually represents the outcome that customers or decision-makers care about.
Study instruction:
- Convert a vague performance claim into a measurable definition specifying the unit, method, baseline, conditions, and target population.
Key point:
- Undefined terms make a numerical claim difficult to test or compare.
- A clear operational definition links the wording to an observable measure.
Source links:
-
28. Q: How should a reader evaluate a claim that a product or process is several times better than a competitor?
A: Show answer
First identify the exact outcome being compared and the scale on which it is measured. Then establish the baseline, comparator, test conditions, sample size, variability, and whether the comparison uses equivalent products and procedures. A statement that something is “three times harder” could mean three times a particular laboratory reading under one test condition, not three times as durable in everyday use. Relative comparisons also need absolute values: a threefold increase from a very small baseline may have little practical effect. Readers should look for independent replication, transparent methods, uncertainty estimates, and limitations. If the claim does not specify what is being compared, the safest conclusion is that its meaning is incomplete.
Study instruction:
- Audit a comparative product claim and list the information required to reproduce the test and interpret the practical consequence.
Key point:
- Multipliers are meaningless without a defined scale and comparator.
- Relative change should be interpreted with absolute values and test conditions.
Source links:
-
29. Q: Why is the word available potentially misleading in a claim about access to a service?
A: Show answer
Availability is not the same as actual access, affordability, use, or reliable service. A utility company might say that power is available to a large proportion of farms, but that could mean only that a distribution line passes nearby. It does not prove that each farm has a connection, can afford service, receives reliable supply, or actually uses electricity. Similar distinctions arise in education, healthcare, broadband, public transport, and humanitarian assistance. A responsible indicator should define the relevant stage of access, such as geographic coverage, eligibility, connection, affordability, uptake, or continuity. Without that definition, a favorable headline may overstate the practical benefit experienced by the intended population.
Study instruction:
- For a service-coverage claim, separate geographic availability, eligibility, connection, affordability, actual use, and service reliability into distinct measures.
Key point:
- Service availability does not prove actual access or utilization.
- Indicators must measure the outcome that the audience believes is being claimed.
Source links:
-
30. Q: What information should accompany a graph so that readers can interpret it accurately?
A: Show answer
A useful graph should identify the variables, units, scale, time period, population or sample, data source, and relevant definitions. Axes should be labeled clearly, and the baseline and scale should not exaggerate or conceal the magnitude of differences. The chart should make clear whether values are counts, percentages, rates, index numbers, or estimates. Where uncertainty or variability matters, the graph should include appropriate intervals or distribution information. A title alone is not enough. A visually striking line can communicate a false sense of growth or improvement if the reader cannot determine what the numbers represent. The underlying data and methods should be accessible enough for the claim to be checked.
Study instruction:
- Inspect a graph and verify its title, axes, units, scale, baseline, timeframe, source, and any uncertainty information before interpreting the trend.
Key point:
- A graph must show what is measured and how values are scaled.
- Visual impact cannot replace labels, units, and traceable data.
Source links:
-
31. Q: Why is a chart without numerical values or a meaningful scale difficult to evaluate?
A: Show answer
A line that rises steeply may suggest rapid growth, but without values, units, and a defined scale the reader cannot determine whether the increase is large, small, or even measurable in a useful way. The chapter describes advertisements that used rising lines without numbers, allowing the same image to imply dramatic expansion or almost no growth. A graph should not rely on shape alone to communicate magnitude. Readers need to know the values represented, the scale intervals, the period covered, and whether the axis begins at zero or uses another justified baseline. If these details are absent, the visual should be treated as illustration rather than quantitative evidence.
Study instruction:
- Reconstruct two different numerical datasets that could produce the same unlabelled rising line and explain why the graphic alone proves neither trend magnitude.
Key point:
- An unscaled line cannot establish the size of a change.
- Numerical labels and axis definitions are necessary for quantitative interpretation.
Source links:
-
32. Q: How can a graph create a misleading impression even when its plotted points are accurate?
A: Show answer
A graph can distort interpretation through a truncated or inconsistent axis, an inappropriate aspect ratio, selective time periods, missing categories, or a scale that magnifies small changes. The plotted values may be correct while the visual emphasis is disproportionate. A truncated vertical axis can make a modest difference appear dramatic; a very wide axis can make an important change look negligible. Omitting the baseline or changing the time window can also alter the story. Readers should compare the graphic with the underlying values and ask whether the design is appropriate for the message. Ethical chart design should make important differences visible without exaggerating them or hiding context.
Study instruction:
- Compare a chart with a zero baseline and a truncated baseline, then explain how the same data can appear different visually.
Key point:
- Accurate numbers can still be presented in a misleading visual form.
- Axis design and selection of the displayed period affect perception.
Source links:
-
33. Q: Why should a reported trend include the actual values and the period being compared?
A: Show answer
A trend claim requires a defined sequence of observations over time. Without the starting and ending values, dates, measurement frequency, and relevant context, readers cannot judge the direction, size, persistence, or volatility of the change. A company may report rising sales while omitting that the increase occurred only after a one-time acquisition or a change in accounting definitions. A short period may capture a temporary fluctuation rather than a durable trend. A long period may conceal recent deterioration. Readers should also consider inflation, seasonality, changes in population, and other factors that affect comparability. A trend should be supported by a transparent time series, not merely a suggestive line or selective pair of endpoints.
Study instruction:
- For a trend statement, identify the dates, observations, units, baseline, comparison method, and any changes in measurement or population over time.
Key point:
- A trend needs a defined time period and comparable observations.
- Selective endpoints can conceal volatility or changes in context.
Source links:
-
34. Q: How can the same average temperature conceal very different living conditions?
A: Show answer
Two locations can have the same annual mean temperature while experiencing very different seasonal extremes. The chapter compares an island with a relatively moderate range and a desert with much colder and hotter extremes, despite a similar average. A person choosing a place to live or camp needs to know the range and seasonal pattern, not just the mean. The same principle applies to average rainfall, waiting time, income, workload, and service reliability. Averages are useful for broad comparison, but decisions that depend on exposure, risk, or comfort require information about variability, extremes, and timing. Historical figures in the chapter are illustrative and should not be used as current climate data.
Study instruction:
- Compare two datasets with the same mean and different minimums, maximums, and seasonal patterns; explain which decision each distribution supports.
Key point:
- Similar averages can conceal very different ranges and risks.
- Extreme values and timing matter when planning for exposure or resilience.
Source links:
-
35. Q: What is the difference between sample size and sample representativeness?
A: Show answer
Sample size is the number of observations included, while representativeness concerns how well the sample reflects the target population for the characteristics relevant to the research question. A large sample can be unrepresentative if some groups are systematically excluded or overrepresented. A smaller probability sample can sometimes provide better population estimates if it is carefully selected and weighted appropriately, although its uncertainty may be greater. Readers should inspect the sampling frame, recruitment method, response rate, inclusion criteria, and differences between respondents and nonrespondents. A report should not use a large count as a substitute for explaining how the sample was obtained and to whom the findings can reasonably be generalized.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate a survey's sampling frame and recruitment process separately from its reported sample count.
Key point:
- Size addresses quantity; representativeness addresses who is observed.
- Both are needed for defensible population inference.
Source links:
-
36. Q: How can selection bias undermine an otherwise large and carefully calculated study?
A: Show answer
Selection bias occurs when inclusion in the sample is related to characteristics that also affect the outcome or the question being studied. For example, asking only highly satisfied customers to review a product will likely overstate satisfaction, even if thousands respond. A voluntary survey may attract people with unusually strong opinions. If certain groups are less likely to participate or are lost during follow-up, the final sample may differ systematically from the population of interest. Increasing the number of participants does not remove this systematic distortion. Researchers should use an appropriate sampling frame, monitor response patterns, compare respondents with nonrespondents where possible, and disclose limitations. Statistical weighting can help in some cases but cannot automatically correct unknown or unmeasured selection mechanisms.
Study instruction:
- Identify who is likely to be missing from a voluntary survey and explain how their absence could change the estimate.
Key point:
- Selection bias is a systematic problem, not simply a small-sample problem.
- More observations do not guarantee a representative sample.
Source links:
-
37. Q: What is statistical power, and why should it be considered before collecting data?
A: Show answer
Statistical power is the probability that a specified test will reject the null hypothesis when a particular alternative effect is true, under the assumed model and design. A study with low power may fail to detect an effect that matters, producing an inconclusive result. Power depends on sample size, effect magnitude, variability, significance threshold, design, and measurement quality. Planning for power helps researchers decide how many observations are needed to detect a meaningful effect with a reasonable probability. It also clarifies that a nonsignificant result is not necessarily evidence that no effect exists. The estimate may simply be too imprecise. Reports should therefore include effect estimates and uncertainty, not only whether a threshold was crossed.
Study instruction:
- Explain how a smaller target effect, greater variability, or stricter significance threshold changes the sample size needed for a planned level of power.
Key point:
- Power concerns the chance of detecting a specified effect under stated assumptions.
- A nonsignificant result does not automatically demonstrate no effect.
Source links:
-
38. Q: How can repeated testing and selective reporting produce false confidence in a result?
A: Show answer
If researchers test many outcomes, subgroups, time periods, or analytical approaches, some results may cross a significance threshold by chance even when no genuine effect exists. The risk increases when only the most favorable analysis is reported and the number of attempts is hidden. This is related to multiple testing and selective reporting. Repeatedly trying small groups until one shows a large improvement, as in the chapter's toothpaste example, creates a distorted picture of the evidence. Better practice includes pre-specifying primary outcomes and analysis plans, disclosing all relevant tests, adjusting for multiple comparisons where appropriate, and clearly labeling exploratory findings. Independent replication helps determine whether an apparently striking result is robust.
Study instruction:
- Describe how testing many outcomes changes the chance of at least one false positive and identify transparency measures that reduce selective reporting.
Key point:
- The number of analyses attempted matters when interpreting a reported result.
- Full disclosure and replication reduce the risk of overinterpreting chance findings.
Source links:
-
39. Q: What is publication or reporting bias, and how does it affect the evidence available to readers?
A: Show answer
Publication or reporting bias occurs when the probability that a study or outcome is made public depends on the direction, size, or statistical significance of its findings. Positive or dramatic results may be more likely to be publicized, while null, negative, or inconclusive results remain unseen. Readers then encounter a selected subset of all evidence and may overestimate the effectiveness of an intervention. The chapter's example of unsuccessful toothpaste trials being set aside illustrates the basic mechanism. A complete assessment should seek trial registrations, protocols, results from all relevant studies, and systematic reviews that consider unpublished or selectively reported evidence. The absence of a published failure does not prove that failures did not occur.
Study instruction:
- Explain why a literature search limited to positive published studies may exaggerate an intervention's apparent effectiveness.
Key point:
- The visible evidence may be a selected subset of the evidence generated.
- Null and unsuccessful results are important to an unbiased assessment.
Source links:
-
40. Q: How should a reader evaluate a percentage improvement claim such as “23% fewer cavities”?
A: Show answer
Begin by identifying the baseline number of cavities and the number after the intervention, then determine whether the 23% figure is a relative reduction or an absolute percentage-point change. Ask how many people were included, how long they were observed, how participants were assigned, whether the comparison group was similar, and how cavities were diagnosed. Check whether the result was one of many trials and whether all outcomes were disclosed. A 23% relative reduction can represent a very small absolute change when the baseline risk is low. Readers should also examine uncertainty and whether the effect is practically important. Without counts, denominators, design details, and full reporting, the headline cannot support a sound purchasing or health decision.
Study instruction:
- Convert a relative percentage claim into absolute counts and an absolute risk difference using a clearly stated hypothetical baseline.
Key point:
- Percentages require denominators and baseline values.
- Relative improvement can sound large while the absolute benefit is small or uncertain.
Source links:
-
41. Q: What should an independent reviewer request before accepting a small product test as evidence of effectiveness?
A: Show answer
The reviewer should request the full protocol, sample selection method, participant count, baseline measurements, comparator, outcome definition, duration, measurement procedures, complete results, and analysis plan. The reviewer should ask whether the product was tested under conditions representative of normal use, whether participants and assessors knew which product was used, and whether other factors changed during the trial. All attempts and outcomes should be disclosed, including tests that did not produce favorable results. Where appropriate, the reviewer should seek independent replication and a qualified statistical assessment. Certification of the report's arithmetic or financial presentation does not by itself establish that the experimental design was scientifically adequate or that the conclusion is justified.
Study instruction:
- Create a review checklist that separates verification of reported numbers from evaluation of the study design and causal claim.
Key point:
- Auditing calculations is not the same as validating research design.
- Independent review needs the protocol, full data, and transparent analysis history.
Source links:
-
42. Q: How should decision-makers respond when an early medical or public-health study reports an impressive result but has too few outcome events?
A: Show answer
They should distinguish a promising observation from sufficient evidence for widespread adoption. The decision process should examine the number of expected and observed events, study design, comparison group, effect estimate, uncertainty, potential harms, and availability of corroborating research. For a rare outcome, a small study may be incapable of detecting meaningful differences even if it includes hundreds of participants. Decisions should also consider the ethical cost of delaying a potentially useful intervention against the risk of exposing people to an ineffective or harmful treatment. Where uncertainty remains, appropriate expert review, additional trials, monitoring, and transparent communication are essential. The chapter's historical examples warn against allowing public excitement or urgent headlines to substitute for adequate evidence.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate a hypothetical early treatment study by calculating expected events and identifying what further evidence would be needed before a broad recommendation.
Key point:
- Promising early results are not equivalent to established effectiveness.
- Urgency does not remove the need for adequate evidence and safety review.
Source links:
-
43. Q: Why should a historical statistic in a book be checked before it is used in a current decision?
A: Show answer
Statistical examples are tied to a particular time, population, measurement method, and source. Demographics, disease incidence, technology, prices, climate patterns, and social behavior can change substantially. The chapter's housing percentages, polio example, electricity coverage claim, and temperature comparisons illustrate reasoning about missing context; they should not be treated as current measurements. Before using an historical figure in policy, business, health, or operational planning, verify its date, original source, definitions, and relevance to the present population. The enduring lesson may remain valid even when the original number is outdated. Separating the analytical principle from the historical data prevents an illustrative example from becoming an unsupported contemporary claim.
Study instruction:
- Mark every historical number in a source and identify the original date, population, measurement definition, and evidence needed to update it.
Key point:
- A sound statistical lesson does not make an old statistic current.
- Revalidate historical figures before applying them to present decisions.
Source links:
-
44. Q: How can journalists and analysts prevent the loss of important statistical context when communicating research?
A: Show answer
They should report the original measure, denominator, sample size, population, time period, comparison, uncertainty, and important limitations rather than repeating a dramatic headline without checking its meaning. Technical language should be translated accurately, not replaced with vague claims that sound more certain. Reporters should ask researchers what the result does not show, whether the analysis was planned, how many outcomes were tested, and whether the finding has been replicated. Analysts should distinguish an observed association from a causal effect and a population average from an individual prediction. Where space is limited, the most decision-relevant qualifications should remain visible. Good communication simplifies wording without removing the information needed to interpret the evidence.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite a sensational statistical headline into a concise report that includes the key denominator, comparison, uncertainty, and limitation.
Key point:
- Clear communication preserves the qualifications that change the meaning of a statistic.
- Simplification must not turn uncertainty into certainty.
Source links:
-
45. Q: What practical checklist can be used to detect the missing figures behind a statistical claim?
A: Show answer
Ask: What exactly is measured, and how is it defined? What is the sample size and how were observations selected? What is the target population? What is the denominator and baseline? What comparison group or counterfactual is used? How many outcomes, groups, or trials were examined? Were all results reported? What is the effect size in absolute as well as relative terms? What uncertainty interval or significance information is available? What is the range and shape of the distribution? Are averages being applied to individuals? Are graph axes, units, dates, and scales visible? What important terms remain ambiguous? Is the source independent and the result replicated? Finally, what decision is the statistic being used to justify, and does the evidence support that decision?
Study instruction:
- Apply this checklist to one advertisement, news article, dashboard, or management report and record every missing item before accepting its conclusion.
Key point:
- Ask about sample, baseline, comparison, uncertainty, distribution, definitions, and visual scale.
- The right questions often reveal more than the headline itself.
