Table 6
The One-Dimensional Picture: Pictographs, Proportionality, and Visual Distortion
1. Q: What is a pictograph, and how does it differ from an ordinary bar chart?
A: Show answer
A pictograph uses pictures or symbols to represent quantities, such as a person icon for a million people, a moneybag for a sum of money, or an animal for livestock. An ordinary bar chart generally represents values through aligned lengths on a numerical scale. Pictographs can attract attention and make a subject familiar, but they introduce extra visual dimensions that readers may interpret as quantity. If an icon is enlarged in height and width to represent a larger value, its area increases faster than its height. If it is also made thicker, its volume grows faster still. The creator must therefore define exactly which visual attribute represents the data.
Study instruction:
- Find a pictograph in a report or advertisement. Identify whether quantity is represented by count, length, area, or volume.
Key point:
- A pictograph is a visual encoding, not merely an illustration.
- The represented visual dimension must match the data dimension.
Source links:
- GOV.UK: A Bite-Sized Guide to Visualising Data
2. Q: Why can a pictograph be more misleading than a simple bar chart?
A: Show answer
A pictograph often changes the size of a familiar object in several dimensions at once. A bar chart normally communicates magnitude through one dimension—length—so a doubling in value can be shown by doubling bar length. When a moneybag, person, cow, or furnace is drawn twice as tall, it may also become twice as wide and twice as deep. The viewer then perceives a much larger area or volume than the numerical ratio warrants. The chapter's moneybag example turns a two-to-one value comparison into an apparent eight-to-one volume comparison. Decorative appeal can therefore magnify a numerical difference.
Study instruction:
- Compare a bar chart and an enlarged-icon chart representing the same values. Explain how the perceived difference changes.
Key point:
- Pictorial appeal can introduce unintended dimensional distortion.
- Use a simple bar or line when it communicates the values more faithfully.
Source links:
- Google: Visualization Traps
3. Q: What is the difference between one-dimensional, two-dimensional, and three-dimensional visual encoding?
A: Show answer
One-dimensional encoding uses a single measurable attribute, such as length or position. Two-dimensional encoding uses area, such as the surface occupied by a circle or icon. Three-dimensional encoding uses volume, such as the apparent bulk of a solid object. These dimensions do not increase at the same rate. If every linear dimension is doubled, area becomes four times as large and volume becomes eight times as large. A chart that uses a two- or three-dimensional object to represent a one-dimensional quantity can therefore exaggerate differences even when its height or length appears proportionate.
Study instruction:
- For a symbol enlarged by a factor of two in every dimension, calculate the resulting area and volume ratios.
Key point:
- Length doubles to 2:1, area to 4:1, and volume to 8:1.
- Match the visual attribute to the dimensional nature of the data.
Source links:
- GOV.UK: A Bite-Sized Guide to Visualising Data
- Google: Visualization Traps
4. Q: Why does doubling an icon's height and width create a fourfold area?
A: Show answer
Area is calculated from two linear dimensions. If a rectangular or similar icon is twice as high and twice as wide, its area is multiplied by two in each dimension: 2 × 2 = 4. A pictograph that intends to show a value twice as large but doubles both height and width therefore gives the larger symbol four times the page area. Readers may interpret the overall occupied area as the quantity, especially when the graphic is viewed quickly. To encode a twofold quantity through area, the area—not each linear dimension—must double; the dimensions should increase by the square root of two, assuming similar shapes.
Study instruction:
- Draw two squares whose areas represent values of 30 and 60. Calculate the correct side-length ratio and compare it with a square whose side length is doubled.
Key point:
- Area changes with the square of a linear scale factor.
- Do not make both height and width proportional to a one-dimensional value.
Source links:
- GOV.UK: A Bite-Sized Guide to Visualising Data
- CDC: Infographics Standards Guide
5. Q: Why does doubling all dimensions of a three-dimensional object create eight times the volume?
A: Show answer
The volume of a similar solid depends on three linear dimensions. Doubling height, width, and depth multiplies volume by 2 × 2 × 2, producing eight times the volume. The chapter uses moneybags to show why an object twice as tall can appear to represent eight times the quantity when its width and thickness also double. This is not a small design detail: the viewer may remember the bulk of the objects rather than the numerical labels. If volume is intended to encode a value, the volume itself must be proportional to the value, which requires a cube-root relationship for each linear dimension.
Study instruction:
- Calculate the volume ratio for two similar cubes with side lengths of 3 cm and 6 cm. Explain what the ratio implies for pictorial charts.
Key point:
- A twofold increase in each dimension produces an eightfold volume.
- Object size should not be scaled linearly in every dimension when it represents a single quantity.
Source links:
- GOV.UK: A Bite-Sized Guide to Visualising Data
6. Q: How can a pictograph represent quantities honestly?
A: Show answer
An honest pictograph makes the relationship between each symbol and the represented value explicit and preserves proportionality in the visual attribute readers are expected to compare. If one icon represents 10 units and another represents 20, the second should not be made twice as tall, twice as wide, and twice as thick unless the intended visual measure is appropriately adjusted. A safer approach is to repeat identical symbols in equal increments, use a consistent single dimension, or place exact values beside the images. The legend should explain the unit represented by one symbol and how partial symbols are handled.
Study instruction:
- Redesign a pictograph so every symbol has the same size and a clear key explains the quantity represented by each symbol.
Key point:
- Use identical symbols for count-based pictographs or correctly scaled dimensions.
- State the unit represented by each symbol and show exact values where useful.
Source links:
- World Health Organization: Data Visualization Handbook
7. Q: What is the difference between scaling an icon by height and scaling it by area?
A: Show answer
Scaling by height makes a single linear dimension correspond to the value. If height doubles while width remains fixed, the icon's area doubles. If both height and width double, area quadruples. Scaling by area instead requires the total visible area to change in direct proportion to the value; the individual dimensions must therefore change more slowly. Readers often infer quantity from the whole footprint of a picture rather than from its height alone. A creator should not assume the audience will ignore width or depth merely because the legend refers to height.
Study instruction:
- For an icon with a fixed width, compare the area change when height doubles with the area change when both dimensions double.
Key point:
- Height is one dimension; area depends on two dimensions.
- The visual property the audience perceives must be the property scaled to the data.
Source links:
- GOV.UK: A Bite-Sized Guide to Visualising Data
- Google: Visualization Traps
8. Q: Why is a one-dimensional quantity misleading when represented by a two-dimensional picture?
A: Show answer
A one-dimensional quantity such as a count or length changes linearly. If a picture's height and width both increase in proportion to that count, its area changes quadratically. For values of 1, 2, and 3, doubling and tripling both dimensions creates areas of 4 and 9 rather than 2 and 3. The image therefore exaggerates the progression. The GOV.UK visualisation guide explains this mismatch and recommends a simple bar or line chart when it communicates the quantity more directly. The key issue is not whether the picture looks attractive but whether its visible area gives a fair representation.
Study instruction:
- Create a three-value pictograph for 1, 2, and 3. Compare symbols scaled by area with symbols whose height and width both follow the values.
Key point:
- One-dimensional data should not be encoded through unadjusted two-dimensional growth.
- Use length or position when those attributes express the quantity more accurately.
Source links:
- GOV.UK: A Bite-Sized Guide to Visualising Data
- Google: Visualization Traps
9. Q: How can repeating equal-sized icons avoid the distortion caused by enlarging a single icon?
A: Show answer
Repeating equal-sized icons represents quantity through the number of symbols rather than the size of one symbol. If one person icon represents 100 people, two identical icons represent 200, and three represent 300. The visual comparison is then based on count, not on the area or volume of a changing object. The method is transparent when the legend clearly states the value per icon and the graphic does not use partial symbols ambiguously. For large quantities, however, repeating hundreds of icons can become cluttered; a bar chart or a labelled numerical summary may be clearer.
Study instruction:
- Design a pictograph using equal-sized symbols for three categories. Add a legend and verify that the number of symbols matches the totals.
Key point:
- Equal-sized repeated symbols encode quantity through count.
- A clear legend prevents confusion about the value represented by each icon.
Source links:
- World Health Organization: Data Visualization Handbook
- GOV.UK: A Bite-Sized Guide to Visualising Data
10. Q: What is the danger of using a three-dimensional object to represent a two-dimensional or one-dimensional value?
A: Show answer
A three-dimensional object invites viewers to judge its bulk, not just its height or width. If a designer scales each dimension in direct proportion to a value, volume changes as the cube of the scale factor. Even if the intended encoding is area, the depth of a three-dimensional illustration can add another cue that readers interpret as magnitude. The chapter's enlarged furnace and moneybag examples demonstrate how apparent volume can become far greater than the numerical ratio. Unless the underlying data genuinely represent volume, a flat chart is usually easier to interpret honestly.
Study instruction:
- Replace a set of enlarged 3D objects with a two-dimensional bar chart. Compare which version makes the numerical ratios easier to judge.
Key point:
- 3D objects add visual dimensions that can magnify perceived differences.
- Avoid perspective and depth effects when the data do not require them.
Source links:
- GOV.UK: A Bite-Sized Guide to Visualising Data
- Google: Visualization Traps
11. Q: What is the difference between a proportional object illustration and a decorative illustration?
A: Show answer
A proportional object illustration encodes a numerical value through a defined visual attribute such as count, length, area, or volume. Its size must therefore correspond to the data according to a consistent mathematical rule. A decorative illustration merely accompanies the text and should not imply a quantitative comparison. Problems arise when an image is intended to be both decorative and quantitative but the reader cannot tell which visual feature represents the value. A large animal, moneybag, or furnace may be interpreted as a larger amount even if the creator intended only to add visual interest. The chart should make its encoding explicit or separate decoration from data.
Study instruction:
- Review an infographic and classify each image as decorative or quantitative. For every quantitative image, state the visual attribute and scaling rule.
Key point:
- Decorative imagery should not imply unsupported quantitative meaning.
- Quantitative symbols require a defined and proportionate visual encoding.
Source links:
- Google: Visualization Traps
- World Health Organization: Data Visualization Handbook
12. Q: How did the chapter's $30 and $60 moneybag example illustrate visual exaggeration?
A: Show answer
The chapter compares two weekly wage figures, $30 and $60. A conventional bar chart makes the second value twice the first in length, matching the two-to-one numerical ratio. An honest pictograph can also use two identical moneybags for the $60 amount and one for $30. But if the $60 moneybag is drawn twice as tall, it is also twice as wide and, in a three-dimensional drawing, twice as thick. Its area becomes four times as large and its volume eight times as large. The labels still say two to one, but the picture suggests a far larger disparity.
Study instruction:
- Reproduce the $30/$60 example as bars, repeated equal icons, and one enlarged 3D icon. Explain the different visual ratios.
Key point:
- A twofold numerical difference can look fourfold by area or eightfold by volume.
- A proportional bar or repeated-symbol display avoids the moneybag distortion.
Source links:
- GOV.UK: A Bite-Sized Guide to Visualising Data
13. Q: Why can the viewer remember the apparent size of a picture rather than its numerical label?
A: Show answer
People often scan a graphic quickly and rely on visual size, shape, and prominence before reading small labels or legends. A large pictorial symbol can therefore leave a stronger impression than the printed value. The chapter emphasizes that the visual impression often dominates, especially when the image is designed to attract attention. A label may technically disclose the correct ratio, but it does not necessarily neutralize the impression created by an oversized object. Designers should not rely on a legend to excuse a distorted graphic; the image itself should be proportionate and easy to interpret.
Study instruction:
- Show a chart briefly to a colleague, then ask what ratio they remember. Compare the answer with the labels and actual values.
Key point:
- Viewers may process overall size before reading numerical details.
- Do not expect labels alone to correct a misleading visual impression.
Source links:
- Google: Visualization Traps
14. Q: What is the problem with changing both the width and length of bars in a bar chart?
A: Show answer
A standard bar chart communicates a single quantity through bar length while keeping bar width constant. If both width and length change, the area of each bar changes in two dimensions and can exaggerate the difference. For example, doubling both length and width creates four times the area even though the intended value has only doubled. This weakens the chart's visual consistency and makes comparison dependent on a property that was not meant to encode the data. Bar width should generally remain constant when one numerical measure is being compared.
Study instruction:
- Inspect a bar chart for variable bar widths. Rebuild it with equal widths and a common baseline, then compare the visual message.
Key point:
- A one-variable bar chart should use one visual dimension for magnitude.
- Changing width as well as length introduces an unintended area encoding.
Source links:
- GOV.UK: A Bite-Sized Guide to Visualising Data
15. Q: Why are truncated bar charts misleading in the same way as truncated line graphs?
A: Show answer
A truncated bar chart removes part of the value scale, so the visible bar lengths represent only the portion above the selected baseline. This makes differences appear larger than they are relative to the full values. The chapter notes that a truncated bar chart deserves the same suspicion as a truncated line graph. In a pictorial chart, the problem can be compounded by enlarging symbols or adding depth. Readers should check the baseline, the complete scale, and the numerical values before comparing apparent sizes.
Study instruction:
- Find a bar chart with a non-zero baseline. Calculate the displayed bar lengths and compare them with the actual values from zero.
Key point:
- Truncation exaggerates the visual difference between values.
- A bar chart should ordinarily begin at zero to preserve proportional length.
Source links:
- GOV.UK: A Bite-Sized Guide to Visualising Data
- Google: Visualization Traps
16. Q: How can the wrong pictorial scale distort a comparison between industrial capacities?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes blast-furnace illustrations comparing steelmaking capacity across decades. The actual increase was from 10 million tons to 14.25 million tons, or about 42.5 percent, but the later furnace was drawn substantially taller and wider. The resulting image suggested a much larger difference than the data supported. Additional distortion came from a black bar representing molten iron that was made disproportionately longer. This example shows that even when the general direction of a comparison is true, inconsistent scaling of different visual features can inflate the apparent magnitude.
Study instruction:
- Use the actual capacities to calculate the percentage increase. Then list every dimension of the furnace drawing that could misrepresent it.
Key point:
- A true increase can be visually exaggerated by inconsistent object scaling.
- Every visual feature that appears quantitative must follow a defined scale.
Source links:
- UNECE: Making Data Meaningful, Part 2
17. Q: What is the risk of using the same scale factor for height, width, and thickness?
A: Show answer
Applying the same scale factor to all three dimensions causes volume to change by the cube of that factor. If a value rises by 50 percent and the object's dimensions are each multiplied by 1.5, the volume becomes 1.5³, or 3.375 times the original. The picture therefore suggests more than a threefold increase rather than a 50-percent increase. This is why three-dimensional pictorial graphs can turn modest numerical growth into a sensational visual statement. A creator should use a visual attribute whose scaling law matches the quantity or choose a simpler chart.
Study instruction:
- For a value increase of 50 percent, calculate the apparent volume change if every object dimension is scaled by 1.5.
Key point:
- Volume scales with the cube of the linear factor.
- Do not scale every object dimension linearly to represent a single quantity.
Source links:
- GOV.UK: A Bite-Sized Guide to Visualising Data
- Google: Visualization Traps
18. Q: How can a pictograph accidentally imply that the objects themselves have changed physically?
A: Show answer
When an infographic shows a larger cow, person, or furnace to represent a larger count or capacity, the viewer may interpret the image literally as a change in the physical size of the object. The chapter's cow example warns that a chart comparing the number of milk cows across years could leave readers thinking modern cows are larger than earlier cows. This confusion occurs because the graphic uses the object itself as the symbol for the quantity. Repeated identical icons or a bar chart avoid suggesting that the physical characteristics of the subject have changed.
Study instruction:
- Imagine a chart showing livestock counts with larger animal drawings. Ask what a viewer could infer about animal size rather than population.
Key point:
- Changing symbol size can imply a change in the object, not just its count.
- Use equal-sized icons or abstract bars when comparing population totals.
Source links:
- World Health Organization: Data Visualization Handbook
19. Q: What is the difference between showing a change in population and showing a change in the size of individuals?
A: Show answer
Population change concerns the number of individuals in a group, while individual-size change concerns the physical dimensions or mass of each individual. A pictograph that enlarges a human or animal symbol to represent a larger population may accidentally suggest that each individual has grown. These are different variables and should not be conflated. If both population and average individual size are relevant, they should be measured and displayed separately, with labels that identify each measure. A chart should not let the size of a symbol silently stand for two distinct concepts.
Study instruction:
- For a livestock report, design separate indicators for animal count and average animal weight. Explain why one enlarged animal icon cannot communicate both safely.
Key point:
- Counts and individual physical size are separate measures.
- Use distinct labels and visual encodings for distinct variables.
Source links:
- World Health Organization: Data Visualization Handbook
- Statistics Canada: Data Visualization—Best Practices
20. Q: How should an analyst choose a visual encoding for quantitative information?
A: Show answer
The analyst should begin by identifying the data type and the comparison the audience needs to make. Position and aligned length are generally easier to compare accurately than area or volume. If a simple bar or line chart answers the question, a pictorial design may add complexity without adding information. If icons are useful for accessibility or engagement, use identical symbols to encode counts or scale a clearly defined area proportionately. The legend, labels, units, and source should make the mapping explicit. The design should help the intended audience understand the values, not merely attract attention.
Study instruction:
- For a dataset of counts, compare position, length, area, and icon-count encodings. Select the one that makes the ratio easiest to judge.
Key point:
- Choose an encoding that matches the data and task.
- Position and length are usually easier to compare than area or volume.
Source links:
- Google: Visualization Traps
- Statistics Canada: Data Visualization—Best Practices
21. Q: Why are position and length often preferable to area and volume for comparing quantities?
A: Show answer
Readers can generally compare aligned positions and lengths more precisely than they can estimate the area or volume of differently sized shapes. A bar chart places values against a common baseline, allowing the audience to judge relative lengths. A pictograph that uses area or volume requires the viewer to estimate a two- or three-dimensional size, which is harder and more vulnerable to distortion. Government visualization guidance therefore recommends using simple charts when they communicate the quantity more clearly. Area or volume should be used only when the data genuinely represent those dimensions and the scale is properly constructed.
Study instruction:
- Rank several chart designs by how easily a reader can estimate a two-to-one ratio. Explain why aligned bars are often clearer than scaled icons.
Key point:
- Aligned position and length support clearer magnitude comparisons.
- Area and volume are harder to estimate and easier to distort.
Source links:
- Statistics Canada: Data Visualization—Best Practices
- GOV.UK: A Bite-Sized Guide to Visualising Data
22. Q: What should a legend explain in a pictorial graph?
A: Show answer
A legend should state what each symbol represents, whether symbols are repeated in equal sizes or scaled, and how partial symbols or rounded quantities are treated. It should also identify the population, unit, period, and source where relevant. If the size of an icon represents area or volume, the legend should explain the scale and the visual attribute used. A legend cannot fully correct a picture whose proportions are misleading, but it can remove ambiguity about the intended mapping. Exact values should be displayed when the graphic is used for important comparisons or decisions.
Study instruction:
- Review a pictograph legend. Confirm that it defines symbol value, unit, period, source, and any rounding or partial-symbol convention.
Key point:
- A legend must explain the data-to-symbol mapping.
- Labels do not excuse disproportionate visual scaling.
Source links:
- World Health Organization: Data Visualization Handbook
- GOV.UK: A Bite-Sized Guide to Visualising Data
23. Q: How can a designer preserve visual appeal without distorting the data?
A: Show answer
A designer can use attractive colors, clear typography, consistent symbols, and meaningful illustrations while keeping quantitative encoding simple. Repeated equal-sized icons can provide visual interest without changing area or volume. A flat two-dimensional symbol can be placed beside an accurately scaled bar or exact value. Decorative images should be visually separated from the data marks so readers do not treat them as additional measurements. The chapter's criticism is not that pictures are inherently wrong; it is that pictures become deceptive when their apparent size does not match the quantity.
Study instruction:
- Redesign a visually appealing infographic using equal-sized decorative icons and a separate accurate bar chart for the actual values.
Key point:
- Visual appeal and quantitative accuracy are compatible.
- Separate decoration from the marks that encode values.
Source links:
- Google: Visualization Traps
- CDC: Infographics Standards Guide
24. Q: What is the 'lie' created when a value ratio of 2:1 is shown as a 4:1 area ratio?
A: Show answer
The underlying values may be 30 and 60, so the correct numerical ratio is 1:2. If the larger symbol is drawn twice as tall and twice as wide, its area is four times the smaller symbol's area. The image therefore communicates a 1:4 area ratio even though the values are 1:2. The distortion arises because the designer scaled two dimensions linearly instead of scaling total area in proportion to the value. The audience may remember the much larger footprint and overestimate the difference.
Study instruction:
- Use graph paper to draw two symbols representing 30 and 60. Measure their areas and compare the result with the numerical ratio.
Key point:
- A twofold value increase should not become a fourfold area increase.
- Scale area directly to values if area is the intended encoding.
Source links:
- GOV.UK: A Bite-Sized Guide to Visualising Data
25. Q: What is the 'lie' created when a value ratio of 2:1 is shown as an 8:1 volume ratio?
A: Show answer
If a three-dimensional object representing 60 is twice as tall, twice as wide, and twice as thick as an object representing 30, its volume is eight times greater. The numbers indicate a twofold difference, but the picture suggests an eightfold difference. This is the chapter's central warning about pictorial graphs: the audience sees the bulk of the object and may interpret it as the magnitude of the data. A creator must not scale all dimensions directly to a one-dimensional quantity. Use repeated symbols, proportional volume scaling, or a simpler bar chart.
Study instruction:
- Explain the moneybag example using the formula for the volume of similar solids. Identify how the visual ratio differs from the numerical ratio.
Key point:
- Doubling each of three dimensions multiplies volume by eight.
- A 3D symbol can dramatically exaggerate a simple numerical comparison.
Source links:
- GOV.UK: A Bite-Sized Guide to Visualising Data
26. Q: How should a reviewer assess whether a pictorial chart is proportionate?
A: Show answer
The reviewer should identify the quantity represented and the visual attribute that is supposed to encode it. Then compare the ratio of the data with the ratio of the visual property: count, length, area, or volume. If the image changes more than one dimension, calculate the combined effect. Check whether symbols are identical, whether their sizes are consistent, and whether perspective or depth changes apparent prominence. Finally, compare the pictograph with a simple bar chart or table. If the message changes substantially, the pictorial version may be exaggerating the difference.
Study instruction:
- Create a pictograph review worksheet with columns for data ratio, visual dimension, measured visual ratio, and resulting interpretation.
Key point:
- Check the data ratio against the encoded visual ratio.
- A simple comparison chart can expose pictorial distortion.
Source links:
- CDC: Infographics Standards Guide
- Google: Visualization Traps
27. Q: What are common warning signs of a misleading pictorial graph?
A: Show answer
Warning signs include icons whose height, width, and depth all change together; variable-width bars; enlarged illustrations without a scaling rule; perspective effects; missing legends; absent exact values; inconsistent symbols across categories; and decorative objects that appear to represent quantities. A chart may also use an exaggerated visual scale while the title describes only a modest increase. These features do not prove intentional deception, but they justify checking the underlying data and measuring the symbols. The reviewer should ask what the viewer is likely to perceive, not merely what the designer intended.
Study instruction:
- Inspect an infographic and mark each symbol whose area or volume changes. Calculate whether the visual ratio matches the data ratio.
Key point:
- Unexplained size changes and perspective are warning signs.
- Review the perceived visual ratio, not only the stated labels.
Source links:
- GOV.UK: A Bite-Sized Guide to Visualising Data
- Google: Visualization Traps
28. Q: How can pictorial charts be used responsibly in public communication?
A: Show answer
Pictorial charts can make information approachable when the symbols are familiar and the mapping is simple. They should use equal-sized icons for counts or a mathematically correct area or volume scale when those dimensions are intentionally used. The source, unit, period, and population should be stated, and the graphic should avoid perspective effects that change apparent prominence. The World Health Organization's visual communication guidance emphasizes proportional length and volume, comparable criteria, consistent rounding, and disclosure of data sources. These practices help preserve both accessibility and accuracy.
Study instruction:
- Design a public-facing graphic for a non-specialist audience. Test whether readers understand the symbol key and the numerical ratios correctly.
Key point:
- Accessible presentation still requires accurate proportions and context.
- Test how the audience interprets the graphic rather than relying on designer intent.
Source links:
- World Health Organization: Data Visualization Handbook
- CDC: Infographics Standards Guide
29. Q: How can an organization control the use of pictographs in formal reports?
A: Show answer
An organization can establish a chart style guide that specifies acceptable encodings, requires a legend and source, and prohibits unreviewed three-dimensional effects or proportionally enlarged icons. Preparers should retain the underlying data and calculation steps. Reviewers should verify that the symbol scale matches the data and that no visual feature unintentionally represents a second variable. High-impact donor, financial, or public reports should receive independent review before release. Where a pictograph is less clear than a bar chart, the standard should favor the simpler chart rather than decorative novelty.
Study instruction:
- Draft a pictograph approval checklist covering data source, visual encoding, symbol scale, labels, accessibility, and independent review.
Key point:
- Visual integrity should be part of report quality control.
- Retain data and review evidence for published graphics.
Source links:
- CDC: Infographics Standards Guide
- World Health Organization: Data Visualization Handbook
30. Q: What should a reader do when a pictorial graph appears to show an overwhelming difference?
A: Show answer
The reader should ignore the initial impression long enough to inspect the legend, exact values, and visual dimensions. Determine whether the symbol's height, width, area, or volume is being changed and calculate the corresponding ratio. Compare the result with the numerical ratio and, if possible, redraw the data as a bar chart. Also check whether the measure is defined, whether the comparison is like-for-like, and whether the source is disclosed. If the picture appears to show a much larger difference than the numbers, the graphic is likely exaggerating the result.
Study instruction:
- Take a pictorial graph from a publication. Convert its values into a simple table or bar chart and compare the apparent ratios.
Key point:
- Do not trust the apparent size of a pictorial symbol without checking its scale.
- Reconstruct the comparison using the underlying numbers.
Source links:
- GOV.UK: A Bite-Sized Guide to Visualising Data
31. Q: How can a chart confuse a change in total capacity with a change in the physical size of equipment?
A: Show answer
An illustration of a larger furnace may be intended to show increased production capacity, but viewers may infer that the furnace itself has grown in every dimension or that its capacity has increased by the same visual volume. These are not equivalent claims. Capacity is a measured operational quantity; the physical dimensions of the equipment are a separate attribute. If the chart uses a furnace drawing, its visual size must be tied to a clear and proportionate encoding of capacity. Otherwise, use bars with exact values and explain any operational context separately.
Study instruction:
- For an industrial comparison, create separate measures for capacity and equipment dimensions. Explain why one scaled illustration cannot safely represent both.
Key point:
- Operational capacity and physical dimensions are distinct variables.
- Do not let an illustration imply an unmeasured physical change.
Source links:
- UNECE: Making Data Meaningful, Part 2
32. Q: Why should a chart avoid implying more information than the data contain?
A: Show answer
A chart should encode only attributes supported by the data. If a dataset contains a count, the graphic should not imply changes in individual size, thickness, weight, or volume unless those variables were measured. Adding visual dimensions creates a false impression of additional evidence. The chapter's cow example shows how a population increase could be misread as a change in animal size. A responsible chart keeps the visual message aligned with the measured variable and uses separate measures when several dimensions matter.
Study instruction:
- List the variables actually measured in a dataset. Compare them with every visual attribute shown in the infographic and remove unsupported implications.
Key point:
- A graphic must not imply unmeasured dimensions.
- One visual attribute should not silently stand for several different variables.
Source links:
- World Health Organization: Data Visualization Handbook
- Google: Visualization Traps
33. Q: What is the relationship between a chart's visual encoding and the reader's mental estimate?
A: Show answer
Visual encoding determines which feature the reader uses to compare values: position, length, area, color, count, or volume. Readers do not always estimate these features with equal accuracy. Aligned lengths and positions are usually easier to compare than the area or bulk of irregular objects. A pictorial graphic may therefore be technically labelled yet still produce a poor mental estimate if its symbols are difficult to compare. The designer should choose a visual form that supports the task and test whether the audience can recover the intended ratios.
Study instruction:
- Ask several readers to estimate ratios from a bar chart and a pictograph. Compare their estimates with the actual ratios.
Key point:
- The chosen visual encoding shapes the reader's estimate.
- Prefer visual properties that people can compare reliably.
Source links:
- Statistics Canada: Data Visualization—Best Practices
- Google: Visualization Traps
34. Q: What are the main lessons of Chapter 6, 'The One-Dimensional Picture'?
A: Show answer
The chapter warns that pictographs can exaggerate values when the size of a picture is increased in more than one dimension. Doubling an object's height and width produces four times the area; doubling height, width, and thickness produces eight times the volume. The $30/$60 moneybag illustration therefore turns a two-to-one numerical comparison into an apparent eight-to-one visual comparison. Similar distortions can occur with furnaces, people, cows, and other symbols. The practical lesson is to match the visual encoding to the data: use aligned lengths, equal-sized repeated symbols, accurate area or volume scaling, clear legends, and exact values. Attractive graphics must remain proportionate and transparent.
Study instruction:
- Recreate the chapter's moneybag example and the furnace example. For each, calculate the numerical ratio, area ratio, and volume ratio, then propose an honest alternative.
Key point:
- Pictorial appeal can turn a modest difference into a dramatic visual exaggeration.
- Use proportionate encodings, clear legends, and numerical checks before trusting a graphic.
Source links:
- GOV.UK: A Bite-Sized Guide to Visualising Data
- World Health Organization: Data Visualization Handbook

Table 7
Semiattached Figures: Relevance, Comparisons, and Statistical Context
1. Q: What is a semiattached figure, and why can it mislead readers?
A: Show answer
A semiattached figure is a number that is connected to the subject being discussed but does not actually establish the claim the presenter wants readers to accept. The figure may be accurate in isolation, yet the relationship between the measured fact and the conclusion is weak, incomplete, or irrelevant. The chapter's examples include laboratory germ counts used to imply a cold remedy works, physician brand preferences used to imply product superiority, and accident totals used to imply individual risk. The central problem is not always fabrication. It is the gap between what was measured and what the audience is encouraged to believe.
Study instruction:
- For each numerical claim in an advertisement or report, write down exactly what was measured and the broader conclusion the presenter wants the reader to draw.
Key point:
- A true number can support a misleading conclusion when it is only loosely connected to the claim.
- Test the relationship between evidence and conclusion, not just the arithmetic.
Source links:
- W. W. Norton: How to Lie with Statistics
- Statistics Canada: Questions to Ask About Surveys
2. Q: How can a statistic be accurate but irrelevant to the question it appears to answer?
A: Show answer
A statistic is relevant only when its measurement, population, comparison, and time period correspond to the claim being made. A laboratory report may accurately count organisms killed in a test tube but say little about whether a product cures an illness in people. A count of road deaths may accurately describe the number of fatalities but not compare the risk faced by individual travelers. A survey may accurately record complaints but fail to measure whether respondents oppose an organization. In each case, the number is not necessarily false; it answers a different question. Readers should identify the exact question a statistic answers before treating it as evidence for another one.
Study instruction:
- Select three claims and classify each supporting number as directly relevant, partially relevant, or irrelevant to the conclusion, explaining the missing connection.
Key point:
- Accuracy of a measurement does not guarantee relevance to the advertised or reported claim.
- The measured variable must match the question being answered.
Source links:
- Federal Trade Commission: Health Products Compliance Guidance
- CDC: Describing Epidemiologic Data
3. Q: Why does killing germs in a test tube not prove that a product cures a cold in a person?
A: Show answer
A test-tube experiment examines an effect under controlled laboratory conditions, not the full biological and practical conditions of human use. The concentration may be different, the product may be diluted, the active ingredient may behave differently in the body, and the organism tested may not be responsible for the illness. Even a genuine laboratory effect does not establish that the product reaches the relevant site in a person, changes the disease process, or improves symptoms. The chapter uses this example to show how a narrow experimental result can be promoted as though it demonstrated a much broader clinical benefit. The appropriate evidence must match the product, dose, route, population, and health outcome claimed.
Study instruction:
- List the differences between a laboratory test and real-world use of a health product, then identify which differences could change the result.
Key point:
- In-vitro activity is not equivalent to demonstrated clinical benefit.
- Evidence must be relevant to the actual product and the actual claim.
Source links:
- Federal Trade Commission: Health Products Compliance Guidance
4. Q: What is the difference between an in-vitro result and an in-vivo or clinical result?
A: Show answer
An in-vitro result is obtained outside a living organism, commonly in a test tube, culture dish, or other laboratory system. An in-vivo result is observed in a living organism, while a clinical result concerns outcomes in people under defined study conditions. Each level can answer useful questions, but the conclusions are not interchangeable. A laboratory result may help identify a possible mechanism or justify further research, yet it does not automatically show that the same effect occurs in humans or improves a meaningful health outcome. Researchers and advertisers must explain what the study actually tested and avoid presenting preliminary laboratory evidence as proof of a clinical result.
Study instruction:
- Sort several hypothetical findings into laboratory, animal, observational human, and controlled clinical evidence, and state what each level can and cannot establish.
Key point:
- Evidence from one research setting cannot automatically be generalized to another.
- The strength of a claim must match the type of evidence available.
Source links:
- Federal Trade Commission: Health Products Compliance Guidance
5. Q: Why does the concentration or dilution of a product matter when interpreting a laboratory test?
A: Show answer
A product may show an effect at a concentration that is not achieved during normal use. If the test uses half an ounce of concentrated material but the instructions require dilution before use, the tested exposure may be very different from the exposure experienced by a consumer. Concentration can affect both effectiveness and safety, so a laboratory result obtained at one dose cannot automatically support a claim about another dose. Researchers should compare the formulation, concentration, route of administration, duration, and conditions of use between the study and the marketed product. If these differ substantially, the evidence may not support the advertised conclusion.
Study instruction:
- Compare the dose and formulation used in a study with the instructions for the marketed product and identify any gap that would weaken the claim.
Key point:
- A result at one concentration does not prove the same result at another.
- Study conditions must match the way the product is actually used.
Source links:
- Federal Trade Commission: Health Products Compliance Guidance
6. Q: Why must the organism tested in a laboratory experiment be relevant to the disease being discussed?
A: Show answer
A test can show that a substance affects a particular organism without proving that the organism causes the illness named in the advertisement. If the product kills a bacterium that is unrelated to the common cold, the result does not demonstrate that it treats a cold. The chapter emphasizes that a reader should ask what germ was tested and whether that organism is connected to the condition. More generally, a valid experimental result must be linked to a plausible disease mechanism and an outcome that matters to patients. The presence of scientific terminology does not make an irrelevant test clinically meaningful.
Study instruction:
- For a health claim, identify the condition, the biological target tested, and the evidence linking that target to the claimed outcome.
Key point:
- The tested mechanism must be relevant to the disease and the claimed benefit.
- Scientific-looking evidence can still answer the wrong question.
Source links:
- Federal Trade Commission: Health Products Compliance Guidance
7. Q: How can an impressive laboratory name or report create borrowed credibility for a weak claim?
A: Show answer
A reputable laboratory, a formal report, or detailed technical language can make a claim appear authoritative before readers examine what the experiment actually demonstrates. The chapter describes using an impressive laboratory name, reproducing a report, and showing a doctor-like figure in an advertisement. These features may signal scientific legitimacy, but they do not repair a mismatch between the test and the claimed result. Readers should inspect the research question, methods, test material, concentration, organism, comparison group, and measured outcome. Authority is relevant to the competence of the investigators, but it is not a substitute for evidence that supports the specific conclusion.
Study instruction:
- Examine an advertisement that highlights a laboratory or expert and separate its credibility signals from the actual evidence presented.
Key point:
- Institutional prestige does not make an irrelevant test relevant.
- Evaluate the method and inference, not just the name attached to the report.
Source links:
- Federal Trade Commission: Health Products Compliance Guidance
- W. W. Norton: How to Lie with Statistics
8. Q: Why is a photograph of a doctor in a white coat not proof that a product is effective?
A: Show answer
A photograph can imply professional approval or scientific validation even when the pictured person has no relevant expertise, has not evaluated the product, or is simply a model. Visual presentation can create an impression that the written evidence does not support. The chapter warns that an advertisement may use a doctor-like figure alongside a laboratory report to make a weak claim appear stronger. The reader should distinguish a decorative image, a paid endorsement, an expert opinion, and independent clinical evidence. Even a genuine endorsement does not replace evidence that the product works for the intended users under ordinary conditions.
Study instruction:
- Identify the implied claims created by an expert image or testimonial and determine what independent evidence would be needed to substantiate them.
Key point:
- Visual authority cues can imply more proof than the advertiser possesses.
- Endorsement and efficacy evidence are different things.
Source links:
- Federal Trade Commission: Health Products Compliance Guidance
9. Q: Why does the statement that 27 percent of eminent physicians use a cigarette brand fail to prove that the brand is safer or better?
A: Show answer
The statistic describes a reported preference or behavior among a group of physicians. It does not establish that physicians possess special evidence about the relative safety of cigarette brands, that the product reduces health risks, or that the brand is superior to alternatives. The figure may also omit how the physicians were selected, how many were asked, how they answered, and what the other 73 percent used. A professional title can make the number sound like an expert recommendation even when the measured fact is only brand use. The conclusion therefore goes beyond the information contained in the percentage.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite the statement as a narrow descriptive claim and list the additional evidence needed to support a claim about product safety or superiority.
Key point:
- Expert use or preference does not establish product effectiveness or safety.
- A percentage must be interpreted according to what respondents actually reported.
Source links:
- Federal Trade Commission: Health Products Compliance Guidance
- Statistics Canada: Questions to Ask About Surveys
10. Q: What should a reader ask when an advertisement says a product extracts 26 percent more juice?
A: Show answer
The first question is “26 percent more than what?” A percentage improvement is meaningful only when the baseline or comparator is identified. In the chapter's juicer example, the comparison was eventually found to be an old-fashioned hand reamer, not the leading competing products. The claim therefore did not show that the advertised machine was the best on the market or even that it performed better than the alternatives a buyer was likely to consider. A useful comparison should identify the comparator, test conditions, number of trials, measurement method, and practical relevance to the purchasing decision.
Study instruction:
- Take a product-performance claim and identify its baseline, comparison products, test conditions, and the consumer decision it is supposed to inform.
Key point:
- A percentage increase is incomplete without a clearly named baseline.
- The comparator determines what the improvement actually means.
Source links:
- Federal Trade Commission: Health Products Compliance Guidance
11. Q: Why can a precise percentage improvement be meaningless when the comparison is poorly chosen?
A: Show answer
A precise percentage can create an impression of scientific measurement while concealing that the comparison is weak or irrelevant. If a juicer produces 26 percent more juice than a hand reamer, the number may be accurate under the test conditions, but it does not tell a buyer how the juicer compares with other electric models. Precision describes the calculation, not the importance of the comparator. The reader should ask whether the comparison represents a realistic alternative, whether the test used equivalent conditions, and whether the measured difference matters for the intended decision. A carefully calculated statistic can still have little practical value.
Study instruction:
- Design a fair comparison for two competing products and specify the baseline, sample of trials, standardized conditions, and outcome measure.
Key point:
- Precision cannot rescue an irrelevant comparison.
- The test must compare alternatives that matter to the audience.
Source links:
- Federal Trade Commission: Health Products Compliance Guidance
- Statistics Canada: Questions to Ask About Surveys
12. Q: How can a laboratory test be technically correct but still fail to support a consumer purchasing decision?
A: Show answer
A laboratory test may measure one narrow performance feature under controlled conditions while the consumer needs to know about overall usefulness, reliability, cost, safety, durability, or performance in ordinary use. The chapter's juicer example measures juice extracted against a hand reamer, but the buyer wants to compare the product with other available machines. A single favorable metric may therefore be accurate yet incomplete. A sound purchasing comparison should select outcomes that reflect the actual decision and disclose limitations that could change the result. Readers should not assume that a product is best simply because it wins one carefully chosen test.
Study instruction:
- Build a consumer comparison checklist that includes the performance metric, practical alternatives, test conditions, safety, durability, and price.
Key point:
- A narrow test does not establish overall product superiority.
- Choose comparison measures that match the real decision.
Source links:
- Federal Trade Commission: Health Products Compliance Guidance
13. Q: Why is the phrase “more than any other brand” not enough to prove that a product is the best choice?
A: Show answer
“More than any other brand” may describe a ranking on one measure, such as the share of respondents who use a product, but it does not establish that the product is safest, most effective, most durable, or best value. The ranking may also depend on the population surveyed, the question asked, the period covered, and the number of competing brands included. If the claim concerns health or performance, preference data are especially weak substitutes for direct evidence of the claimed outcome. Readers should ask what dimension was ranked and whether that dimension is relevant to the conclusion being implied.
Study instruction:
- Identify the precise attribute behind a “number one” claim and list two other attributes that would be needed before calling the product best overall.
Key point:
- A ranking on one measure is not a ranking on every measure.
- “Most used” and “best performing” are different claims.
Source links:
- Federal Trade Commission: Health Products Compliance Guidance
- Statistics Canada: Questions to Ask About Surveys
14. Q: What is the role of a comparator in evaluating a percentage claim?
A: Show answer
The comparator defines the reference point against which the measured difference is calculated. A product may be compared with an older model, a low-performing competitor, a placebo, a standard treatment, or no treatment at all. Each comparison answers a different question. A 26 percent improvement over an obsolete device cannot be treated as a 26 percent advantage over current products. The comparator should be relevant to the claim, chosen before the results are known, and tested under equivalent conditions. Without this information, readers cannot tell whether the reported improvement is practically meaningful or merely favorable to the presenter.
Study instruction:
- For a claim about improved performance, identify at least two possible comparators and explain how each changes the meaning of the reported percentage.
Key point:
- The reference point determines the meaning of the comparison.
- Comparators should be realistic, disclosed, and measured fairly.
Source links:
- Federal Trade Commission: Health Products Compliance Guidance
15. Q: How can a statistic about the number of deaths create a misleading impression about personal safety?
A: Show answer
A death count records how many fatalities occurred, but it does not show how many people were exposed to the risk or how much activity took place. A transportation mode used by many more people or over many more miles may have a larger number of deaths even if its risk per journey or per mile is lower. The chapter's driving and railway examples show why a total count should not be treated as an individual probability. To compare risk, the numerator must be related to a relevant measure of exposure, such as passenger miles, trips, vehicle miles, or hours of operation. The chosen denominator must match the question being asked.
Study instruction:
- Convert a raw fatality comparison into an exposure-adjusted comparison and state what type of risk the denominator measures.
Key point:
- Counts describe totals; rates relate outcomes to exposure.
- Personal risk cannot be inferred from totals alone.
Source links:
- Bureau of Transportation Statistics: Transportation Fatality Rates
- CDC: Describing Epidemiologic Data
16. Q: Why do transportation fatality totals need an exposure denominator before they are used to compare safety?
A: Show answer
The number of fatalities is influenced by how many people travel, how often they travel, and how far they go. If one mode carries far more passengers or operates far more miles, it may accumulate more deaths even when the risk associated with each unit of travel is lower. The Bureau of Transportation Statistics explains that exposure rates divide adverse outcomes by an activity measure such as trips, miles, or hours of operation. This provides a more informative basis for comparing safety than raw totals, although the selected exposure measure must still fit the type of travel and the risk being examined.
Study instruction:
- Choose an appropriate exposure denominator for road, rail, and aviation comparisons and explain why the same denominator may not suit every mode.
Key point:
- Exposure measures put event counts into context.
- The denominator must correspond to the activity that creates the risk.
Source links:
- Bureau of Transportation Statistics: Transportation Fatality Rates
17. Q: What does a fatality rate per passenger mile reveal that a raw passenger death count cannot?
A: Show answer
A rate per passenger mile relates the number of deaths to the distance traveled by passengers. It helps compare the amount of risk associated with a given volume of passenger travel, rather than simply counting how many people died. The chapter recommends passenger miles when comparing transportation modes because the total number of fatalities alone is affected by how much each mode is used. A rate still does not answer every safety question: risk per trip, per hour, or per passenger mile may differ, and exposure estimates must be reliable. The reader should choose the denominator that best matches the decision being made.
Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between risk per trip, per passenger mile, and per hour, and identify a decision for which each measure might be relevant.
Key point:
- Rates account for exposure, but different denominators answer different risk questions.
- A rate is only meaningful when its unit is understood.
Source links:
- Bureau of Transportation Statistics: Transportation Fatality Rates
18. Q: Why does “four times more fatalities at 7 P.M. than at 7 A.M.” not prove that an individual driver is four times more likely to die at night?
A: Show answer
The statement compares the number of deaths at two times, not the number of deaths relative to the amount of driving at those times. If far more people are on the road at 7 P.M., the total number of fatalities can be higher even when the risk per driver, per trip, or per mile is not four times higher. To assess individual risk, the analyst needs an exposure denominator such as vehicle miles traveled during each time period and a consistent definition of the fatality outcome. The chapter's example shows how a count can be presented as though it were a personal probability when the necessary denominator is missing.
Study instruction:
- Identify the additional traffic-volume data needed to test whether evening driving is riskier than morning driving on a per-mile basis.
Key point:
- A difference in counts is not automatically a difference in individual risk.
- Exposure must be measured for both time periods.
Source links:
- Bureau of Transportation Statistics: Transportation Fatality Rates
- CDC: Describing Epidemiologic Data
19. Q: How can more accidents in clear weather occur even if foggy weather is more dangerous per mile?
A: Show answer
Clear weather occurs more often and may account for much more total driving, so more crashes can happen during clear conditions simply because more people are exposed. Fog may produce fewer crashes in total while creating a higher risk for each mile driven in fog. The correct comparison requires both the number of crashes and the amount of driving under each weather condition. This example illustrates why the size of the numerator cannot be interpreted without the denominator. A reader should distinguish the frequency of exposure from the risk associated with each exposure unit.
Study instruction:
- Create a hypothetical comparison of clear-weather and fog-weather crashes using both crash counts and vehicle miles traveled, then calculate which condition has the higher rate.
Key point:
- More events can occur in a common condition even when the per-exposure risk is lower.
- The denominator changes the interpretation of the count.
Source links:
- Bureau of Transportation Statistics: Transportation Fatality Rates
- CDC: Describing Epidemiologic Data
20. Q: Why can a rise in the number of airplane deaths over time fail to show that flying has become more dangerous?
A: Show answer
The number of people flying and the total amount of air travel may have increased greatly over the same period. A larger number of fatalities could therefore reflect greater exposure rather than a higher risk per flight, per departure, or per passenger mile. A meaningful comparison needs a consistent denominator and comparable definitions across the years being examined. The chapter uses this example to show that historical totals cannot be compared fairly without considering changes in the amount of activity. Readers should ask whether the population, exposure, and reporting methods remained sufficiently consistent for the trend to support the conclusion.
Study instruction:
- Identify at least two exposure measures that could be used to compare aviation safety over time and explain what each one captures.
Key point:
- More total activity can produce more total events without increasing the underlying rate.
- Trends need a stable and relevant denominator.
Source links:
- Bureau of Transportation Statistics: Transportation Fatality Rates
21. Q: Why did the chapter's figure of 4,712 railroad deaths not mean that 4,712 train passengers were killed?
A: Show answer
The total included different categories of people, not only passengers riding trains. The chapter explains that many victims were people in automobiles struck at crossings, while others were trespassers riding outside the passenger service; only a small portion were train passengers. The raw total therefore answered a broader question about deaths chargeable to railroads, not the narrower question of passenger safety. A reader must inspect the case definition and categories included in a count before interpreting it. The same principle applies whenever a headline total combines users, workers, bystanders, and people affected indirectly by an activity.
Study instruction:
- Break a broad incident total into relevant categories and determine which categories belong in a measure of passenger risk.
Key point:
- The label attached to a total may conceal different populations and event types.
- Check who and what the numerator includes.
Source links:
- W. W. Norton: How to Lie with Statistics
- CDC: Describing Epidemiologic Data
22. Q: Why is the number of passenger miles a more relevant denominator than total deaths when comparing passenger travel?
A: Show answer
Passenger miles combine the number of passengers with the distance they travel, giving a measure of the amount of passenger exposure. Dividing fatalities by passenger miles helps distinguish a mode that carries many people over long distances from one with fewer passenger miles. The chapter recommends this type of comparison because total deaths alone can reflect differences in how much travel occurs. However, passenger miles are not a universal denominator for every transportation question. A journey-based comparison may require trips, while an operational comparison may use hours or departures. The analyst should choose the exposure unit that corresponds to the risk being assessed.
Study instruction:
- Explain why passenger miles may be appropriate for long-distance passenger travel but not necessarily for every transport safety question.
Key point:
- Passenger miles measure distance-weighted passenger exposure.
- No single denominator answers every risk question.
Source links:
- Bureau of Transportation Statistics: Transportation Fatality Rates
23. Q: What makes a denominator appropriate when calculating a rate?
A: Show answer
The denominator should represent the population, activity, or time at risk that could have produced the events counted in the numerator. If the numerator counts new disease cases, the denominator should correspond to the population at risk during the relevant period. If the numerator counts transportation fatalities, the denominator may be passenger miles, vehicle miles, trips, or operating hours depending on the question. The numerator and denominator should refer to compatible populations, definitions, and time periods. A mismatched denominator can make a mathematically correct rate answer a different question from the one implied by the report.
Study instruction:
- For a health, safety, or business statistic, identify the numerator, denominator, reference period, and population represented by each component.
Key point:
- A rate is meaningful only when numerator and denominator match conceptually.
- The denominator should reflect the population or exposure capable of producing the event.
Source links:
- CDC: Describing Epidemiologic Data
- Bureau of Transportation Statistics: Transportation Fatality Rates
24. Q: What is denominator neglect, and how does it create misleading comparisons?
A: Show answer
Denominator neglect occurs when readers focus on the number of events without considering the size of the population or amount of exposure from which those events arose. A city with twice as many residents may have more cases of an illness without having a higher rate. A transportation mode carrying many more passengers may have more deaths without having a higher risk per passenger mile. The chapter's examples show that totals are often presented because they are easy to understand and can be emotionally striking. The reader should ask how many people, trips, miles, or units were exposed before drawing a conclusion about comparative risk or performance.
Study instruction:
- Find a news headline based on a count and identify the denominator needed to determine whether the underlying rate actually changed.
Key point:
- Counts without denominators can confuse volume with risk.
- Always ask “out of how many?” and “over what exposure?”
Source links:
- CDC: Describing Epidemiologic Data
- Bureau of Transportation Statistics: Transportation Fatality Rates
25. Q: How can a complaint survey be misrepresented as proof that 78 percent of employees oppose a union?
A: Show answer
A survey may record that many employees have at least one complaint about a union, but those complaints may be small, varied, or unrelated to whether employees support the union overall. Re-labeling the sum of complaints as “opposition” changes the meaning of the measurement. An employee might object to one procedure while still supporting the organization. The chapter shows how a collection of minor grievances can be presented as though it measured a broad political or organizational position. The key issue is construct validity: does the question and coding method actually measure the attitude named in the headline?
Study instruction:
- Draft separate survey questions for specific complaints and overall support, then explain why their results should not be combined into one measure.
Key point:
- Complaints about parts of an organization do not necessarily equal opposition to the organization.
- Do not rename a measured variable to make it support a stronger claim.
Source links:
- Statistics Canada: Questions to Ask About Surveys
- W. W. Norton: How to Lie with Statistics
26. Q: What is construct validity, and why does it matter when interpreting a survey percentage?
A: Show answer
Construct validity concerns whether a measure actually captures the concept it is intended to represent. A question about whether employees have any complaint does not necessarily measure whether they oppose a union. A question about whether black people have equal job opportunities may reveal a respondent's attitudes about race rather than the actual employment conditions faced by black workers. The survey may collect answers accurately, but the conclusion is invalid if the question measures a different construct. Researchers should define the concept, develop questions that correspond to it, test how respondents interpret the wording, and avoid making broader claims than the instrument can support.
Study instruction:
- Choose a broad concept such as trust, satisfaction, discrimination, or support and design two questions that measure distinct aspects of it.
Key point:
- The question must measure the concept named in the conclusion.
- Accurate responses to the wrong question still produce a misleading claim.
Source links:
- Statistics Canada: Questions to Ask About Surveys
- U.S. Census Bureau: Nonsampling Error Standard
27. Q: Why did the chapter's poll about equal job opportunities fail to measure actual employment conditions?
A: Show answer
The poll asked people whether black people had as good a chance as white people to get jobs, but the answers were strongly associated with the respondents' own racial attitudes. People who were more prejudiced were more likely to say that opportunities were equal, while many respondents sympathetic to black people said they were not. The poll therefore revealed something about attitudes and perceptions, not a direct measurement of hiring outcomes or employment discrimination. A survey of beliefs cannot be treated as an audit of actual job opportunities unless the question and data collection method measure those opportunities directly.
Study instruction:
- Identify what an opinion question can reveal about respondents and what additional labor-market evidence would be needed to assess actual employment conditions.
Key point:
- Opinions about a condition are not the same as measurements of the condition itself.
- Distinguish perceptions, attitudes, and objective outcomes.
Source links:
- Statistics Canada: Questions to Ask About Surveys
- U.S. Census Bureau: Nonsampling Error Standard
28. Q: How can a trend in survey answers move in the opposite direction from the real-world condition being studied?
A: Show answer
A trend in responses can change because the composition or attitudes of respondents change, even when the underlying condition does not improve. In the chapter's poll example, people with stronger prejudice were more likely to answer that black people had equal job opportunities. If prejudice increased during the polling period, the share of “yes” answers could rise and be presented as evidence of improving equality, even though the underlying employment situation might be worsening. The survey trend therefore reflects a changing relationship between attitudes and answers, not necessarily a changing social condition. Analysts must investigate what drives the response pattern before interpreting it as a real-world trend.
Study instruction:
- Construct a hypothetical trend in which respondent attitudes change while the measured social condition stays constant, and explain how the poll result could be misread.
Key point:
- A change in answers is not automatically a change in the underlying condition.
- Interpret trends through the mechanism that generated the responses.
Source links:
- Statistics Canada: Questions to Ask About Surveys
- AAPOR: Sampling Methods for Political Polling
29. Q: Why should a survey's question wording be examined before its percentage is accepted?
A: Show answer
Wording determines what respondents are being asked to judge and can influence how they interpret the subject. A question may combine several issues, use a vague term, invite a socially desirable answer, or measure a minor complaint rather than an overall attitude. The resulting percentage may be calculated correctly but mislabeled in the report. Researchers should examine the exact wording, response options, question order, population asked, and mode of administration. Readers should also ask whether respondents were asked about personal experience, general belief, observed behavior, or a hypothetical situation, because those are different kinds of evidence.
Study instruction:
- Review a survey question and identify ambiguous terms, assumptions, leading wording, and any mismatch between the response options and the conclusion.
Key point:
- The wording is part of the measurement, not a minor editorial detail.
- A percentage cannot be interpreted correctly without the question that produced it.
Source links:
- Statistics Canada: Questions to Ask About Surveys
- U.S. Census Bureau: Nonsampling Error Standard
30. Q: How can a poll measure attitudes accurately while still being useless for proving an objective claim?
A: Show answer
A poll may reliably record what respondents believe, fear, prefer, or perceive. That does not mean it measures the external condition that shaped those beliefs. For example, a question about whether job opportunities are equal can measure perceptions or attitudes, but it cannot by itself establish hiring rates, pay differences, promotion patterns, or employer behavior. A poll about a union can reveal specific complaints without proving overall opposition. The researcher must state whether the target is opinion, behavior, experience, or an external outcome, then choose evidence appropriate to that target. A valid attitude measure can still be irrelevant to an objective claim.
Study instruction:
- For one public-policy claim, separate the evidence needed to measure public opinion from the evidence needed to measure actual conditions.
Key point:
- Reliable opinion data are not substitutes for direct outcome data.
- State clearly whether the survey measures beliefs or external facts.
Source links:
- Statistics Canada: Questions to Ask About Surveys
- AAPOR: Sampling Methods for Political Polling
31. Q: Why can different financial ratios make the same company's profit appear small or large?
A: Show answer
A profit figure can be divided by sales, assets, equity, investment, or another base, and each ratio answers a different question. Profit as a percentage of sales measures how much of each sales dollar remains as profit. Return on investment relates profit to the capital committed to generate it. A company can have a small margin on each sale but turn its capital over rapidly, or a larger margin while using much more capital. The chapter shows that selecting one denominator rather than another can change the impression created by the same underlying business results. Readers should identify the numerator, denominator, and period before comparing percentages.
Study instruction:
- Calculate a sales margin and a return on investment from the same hypothetical profit figure and explain why the two percentages are not interchangeable.
Key point:
- Different ratios describe different relationships within the business.
- A percentage has no clear meaning until its base is identified.
Source links:
- SEC: Beginners’ Guide to Financial Statements
- W. W. Norton: How to Lie with Statistics
32. Q: What is the difference between profit on sales and return on investment?
A: Show answer
Profit on sales divides a profit measure by revenue and describes the portion of sales represented by profit. Return on investment divides a return by the capital or resources committed to generate it. These denominators can differ greatly, so the resulting percentages are not directly interchangeable. The chapter uses a retailer that buys an item for 99 cents and sells it for one dollar: the margin on the sale is about one percent, but if the same small amount of capital is reused many times over a year, the annual return on that capital can be much larger. The comparison depends on turnover, time period, and the definition of investment.
Study instruction:
- Work through a simple buy-and-sell example and calculate the profit margin per sale separately from the return on the capital used across repeated transactions.
Key point:
- Margin uses sales as its base; return uses invested resources as its base.
- Always check the time period and turnover assumptions.
Source links:
- SEC: Beginners’ Guide to Financial Statements
- W. W. Norton: How to Lie with Statistics
33. Q: How can the same profit be described in several different ways to influence an audience?
A: Show answer
The same financial result can be expressed as a dollar amount, a percentage of sales, a return on investment, a change from an earlier period, or a comparison with an industry benchmark. Each expression emphasizes a different reference point. A small percentage of a very large sales total may sound modest, while the same profit divided by a smaller capital base may sound substantial. A percentage increase over an unusual historical period may also make an ordinary result appear dramatic. Readers should translate the statement back into the underlying numerator and denominator and examine the full financial statements rather than relying on the one figure selected for a press release or argument.
Study instruction:
- Take a hypothetical profit amount and express it as a sales margin, investment return, and year-over-year change, then explain the impression each measure creates.
Key point:
- Framing changes when the denominator or reference period changes.
- Reconstruct the underlying calculation before judging the claim.
Source links:
- SEC: Beginners’ Guide to Financial Statements
34. Q: Why should readers inspect the numerator and denominator of a financial ratio separately?
A: Show answer
The numerator defines what is being treated as profit or return, while the denominator defines the base against which that amount is evaluated. “Profit” may refer to gross profit, operating income, pretax income, or net income, and “investment” may refer to assets, equity, or another capital measure. A ratio can therefore change when either component changes, even if the business activity being discussed has not changed in the way the headline suggests. The SEC explains that financial ratios are calculated from different statement items and that desirable values vary by industry. A reader should confirm the formula, accounting period, and statement definitions before comparing companies or years.
Study instruction:
- Select a published ratio and identify its numerator, denominator, source statements, reporting period, and the business question it is intended to answer.
Key point:
- A ratio is a relationship between two defined quantities.
- Different formulas should not be compared as though they measure the same thing.
Source links:
- SEC: Beginners’ Guide to Financial Statements
35. Q: How can depreciation and reserves affect the way a company's reported profits are presented?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes how financial statements can use categories such as depreciation and contingency reserves that influence the profit figure shown for a period. Readers should not assume that every such expense is improper or that an accounting treatment automatically conceals wrongdoing; depreciation and reserves can be legitimate parts of financial reporting. The lesson is to understand the accounting policies, estimates, notes, and changes in classification that affect the reported result. A headline about profit should be read alongside the income statement, balance sheet, cash-flow statement, and explanatory notes rather than treated as a complete account of the company's financial position.
Study instruction:
- Review a company's financial statements and identify how depreciation, provisions, and other estimates affect reported earnings and cash flow.
Key point:
- Accounting categories and estimates influence reported profit.
- Examine the notes and cash flows before drawing conclusions about hidden or sustainable earnings.
Source links:
- SEC: Beginners’ Guide to Financial Statements
- W. W. Norton: How to Lie with Statistics
36. Q: Why should return on sales, return on assets, and return on equity not be treated as interchangeable?
A: Show answer
Each ratio uses a different denominator and answers a different question. Return on sales or operating margin relates profit to revenue and indicates how much of each sales dollar is retained at a specified profit level.
