Return on assets relates earnings to the assets used to generate them. Return on equity relates earnings to shareholders' capital. Leverage, asset intensity, business model, and industry conditions can cause these ratios to differ substantially. A company with a low sales margin may still generate a strong return on assets if it turns assets over efficiently, while leverage can affect the return reported for shareholders. The ratio must be selected according to the question being asked.
Study instruction:
- Compare a company's sales margin, return on assets, and return on equity and explain what each reveals about a different part of its financial performance.
Key point:
- Sales, assets, and equity are different bases for evaluating profitability.
- The ratio should match the analytical question.
Source links:
- SEC: Beginners’ Guide to Financial Statements
37. Q: Why can a low profit margin be presented as reassuring even when the business earns a substantial return on its capital?
A: Show answer
A low margin can sound modest because it is expressed as a small share of sales. If the business sells a large volume or turns its capital over many times, the return on the resources invested may be much higher than the margin suggests. The chapter uses this contrast to show how a company can choose the ratio that best supports its argument. A fair analysis should show both the margin and the return measure, define the profit concept used, and examine the period and capital base. Neither figure should be treated as the complete measure of performance in isolation.
Study instruction:
- Construct a business example with a low margin but rapid turnover and calculate why its return on capital can differ from its margin.
Key point:
- A low percentage on sales does not necessarily mean a low return on invested resources.
- Volume and turnover affect the relationship between the ratios.
Source links:
- SEC: Beginners’ Guide to Financial Statements
- W. W. Norton: How to Lie with Statistics
38. Q: Why is a financial ratio difficult to interpret without knowing the industry and accounting context?
A: Show answer
Different industries use assets, inventory, labor, financing, and pricing in different ways. A ratio that is typical for a grocery retailer may be unusual for a utility, software company, or manufacturer. Accounting policies and business models can also affect the numerator and denominator. The SEC notes that desirable financial ratios vary by industry, so a single percentage should not be treated as a universal standard. Comparisons are stronger when they use similar businesses, consistent accounting definitions, the same reporting periods, and relevant benchmarks. Readers should avoid declaring a company unusually profitable or weak solely because one ratio appears large or small.
Study instruction:
- Choose two businesses from different industries and identify why comparing one profitability ratio without context could lead to an incorrect conclusion.
Key point:
- Financial ratios need industry, accounting, and period context.
- A single benchmark rarely applies to every business.
Source links:
- SEC: Beginners’ Guide to Financial Statements
39. Q: How can changing the time period make a financial result appear more favorable?
A: Show answer
A presenter may select a short period with unusually high sales, a low comparison year, or a period that excludes seasonal weakness and one-time costs. The percentage change can then appear much larger or smaller than it would over a full business cycle. The chapter's examples show that the same financial fact can be framed using different time comparisons. Readers should check whether periods have equal length, whether seasonal patterns are comparable, whether accounting definitions changed, and whether unusual items affected either period. A valid year-over-year comparison should not silently compare a partial period with a full year or a peak with an exceptional trough.
Study instruction:
- Recalculate a reported financial growth rate using an additional comparison period and explain how the choice of baseline changes the impression.
Key point:
- Time period is part of the denominator and context of a comparison.
- A selected baseline can magnify or suppress apparent change.
Source links:
- SEC: Beginners’ Guide to Financial Statements
- W. W. Norton: How to Lie with Statistics
40. Q: What is the danger of comparing financial percentages without checking whether the underlying definitions changed?
A: Show answer
A percentage trend may appear to improve or deteriorate because the company changed how it classifies income, expenses, reserves, assets, or reporting categories. If the numerator or denominator is defined differently across periods, the figures may not be comparable even when they are presented in the same format. Readers should inspect accounting policy notes, restatements, segment definitions, and any changes in the basis of calculation. Consistency of labels does not guarantee consistency of measurement. When definitions change, the report should explain the effect and, where possible, provide comparable restated figures.
Study instruction:
- Compare two reporting periods and check whether the accounting policy, classification, or ratio formula changed before interpreting the trend.
Key point:
- A trend requires consistent definitions across periods.
- Identical labels can hide different calculations.
Source links:
- SEC: Beginners’ Guide to Financial Statements
41. Q: How can inconsistent disease-reporting rules create a false impression that an illness is concentrated in certain places?
A: Show answer
Reported case counts depend partly on whether health conditions are recognized, diagnosed, and required to be reported. If some states require reporting while others do not, the places with stronger reporting rules may appear to have far more cases even when the underlying occurrence is not correspondingly higher. The chapter's influenza and pneumonia example describes a period when three states accounted for a large share of reports because their reporting requirements differed from those elsewhere. The reader should examine case definitions, reporting mandates, surveillance coverage, diagnostic access, and changes in data collection before treating reported counts as direct measures of disease occurrence.
Study instruction:
- Compare two jurisdictions' disease-reporting systems and identify differences in case definition, mandatory reporting, diagnostic practice, and surveillance completeness.
Key point:
- Reported cases reflect both disease occurrence and the system that records them.
- Unequal reporting rules can distort geographic comparisons.
Source links:
- CDC: Describing Epidemiologic Data
- U.S. Census Bureau: Nonsampling Error Standard
42. Q: Why did influenza and pneumonia appear to be concentrated in three states in the chapter's example?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that the three states continued to require reports of these conditions after other states had stopped doing so. Their larger share of recorded cases therefore reflected a difference in reporting practice, not necessarily a comparable difference in the underlying disease burden. A reader who compares the reported totals without checking the reporting rules may conclude that the illness is concentrated geographically. The correct interpretation requires knowing which conditions were reportable, how consistently cases were diagnosed, and whether surveillance coverage changed during the period. A reporting system is part of the data-generating process and must be considered when interpreting a trend.
Study instruction:
- Create a short comparison table showing how changes in mandatory reporting can alter recorded counts even if the true number of illnesses remains unchanged.
Key point:
- More recorded cases may reflect more complete reporting rather than more disease.
- Geographic comparisons require consistent surveillance definitions and coverage.
Source links:
- CDC: Describing Epidemiologic Data
43. Q: How can a change in the definition of a disease alter reported case counts without a matching change in actual illness?
A: Show answer
A case definition determines which symptoms, test results, or clinical judgments qualify an event for inclusion. If a definition becomes narrower, fewer cases may be counted; if it becomes broader, more cases may be recorded. The chapter's malaria example describes a change from using the term colloquially for a cold or chill to recording only cases confirmed as malaria. The apparent decline was therefore partly a change in classification rather than proof that the disease had almost disappeared. Comparisons over time should use consistent case definitions or clearly explain how changes affect the series.
Study instruction:
- Identify a hypothetical change in diagnostic criteria and predict how it could affect the number of cases recorded without changing the underlying health condition.
Key point:
- A change in definition can create an apparent trend in the data.
- Compare like with like or disclose the break in the series.
Source links:
- CDC: Describing Epidemiologic Data
- W. W. Norton: How to Lie with Statistics
44. Q: Why can improved diagnosis or greater public awareness increase reported cases even if the underlying disease rate is unchanged?
A: Show answer
When clinicians become more aware of a condition, testing becomes more available, or reporting systems become more active, cases that previously went undetected may enter the records. The observed count can rise even when the underlying occurrence has not increased by the same amount. The chapter's polio example notes that greater awareness may have led to more frequent diagnosis and recording of mild cases. This is a form of ascertainment change: the system becomes better at detecting and documenting events. Analysts should examine diagnostic criteria, testing intensity, reporting completeness, and the population at risk before interpreting an increase as a real rise in disease occurrence.
Study instruction:
- List the changes in diagnosis, testing, awareness, and reporting that could increase recorded cases independently of a change in incidence.
Key point:
- Better detection can increase reported counts without increasing underlying risk.
- Surveillance changes must be considered in trend analysis.
Source links:
- CDC: Describing Epidemiologic Data
45. Q: Why did the chapter question the claim that 1952 was the worst polio year in medical history?
A: Show answer
The claim relied on a record number of reported cases, but the chapter identifies several factors that could increase the count without proving that the disease rate had reached a record level. There were more children in age groups considered especially susceptible, public awareness may have increased diagnosis of mild cases, and financial incentives may have affected reporting. The chapter also points to deaths as a cross-check that did not support the strongest interpretation of the case total. The broader lesson is that a count must be interpreted with the size and composition of the population at risk, case detection, reporting practices, and a suitable measure of severity or outcome.
Study instruction:
- Analyze the polio example by separating population size, age composition, diagnosis, reporting incentives, and mortality evidence.
Key point:
- A record count does not automatically mean a record rate or risk.
- Changes in population and detection can alter the number of recorded cases.
Source links:
- CDC: Describing Epidemiologic Data
- CDC: Age Adjustment
46. Q: Why are age-specific or age-adjusted rates often more useful than crude death rates when comparing populations?
A: Show answer
The risk of death varies substantially by age, so populations with different age structures can have different crude death rates even when their age-specific risks are similar or reversed. A population with more older people may record more deaths per person simply because it contains more people in age groups with higher mortality. Age adjustment applies the age-specific rates to a common standard population, allowing a more meaningful comparison of relative rates across groups or periods. The adjusted figure is a comparative index, not a direct count of deaths that actually occurred. Readers should check the age groups and standard population used.
Study instruction:
- Explain how two populations with different age structures could have misleadingly different crude death rates and why age adjustment helps compare them.
Key point:
- Population composition can affect crude rates.
- Age-adjusted rates improve comparison but are not actual observed totals.
Source links:
- CDC: Age Adjustment
- CDC: Describing Epidemiologic Data
47. Q: Why did the comparison between Navy and civilian death rates fail to prove that naval service was safer?
A: Show answer
The two populations were not comparable. The Navy consisted mainly of young men who had passed health screening, while the civilian population included infants, older people, and people with illnesses. Those differences affect death rates regardless of military service. The lower Navy rate therefore could reflect the age and health composition of the group rather than a protective effect of naval life. A fair comparison would require similar populations, consistent observation periods, and appropriate adjustment for factors that influence mortality. The figures do not prove that service was safer or more dangerous for otherwise comparable individuals.
Study instruction:
- Identify the selection and demographic differences between the two groups and propose a comparison design that would better isolate the effect of service.
Key point:
- Comparisons are misleading when groups differ in important risk factors.
- Similar definitions and comparable populations are necessary for a fair inference.
Source links:
- CDC: Age Adjustment
- CDC: Describing Epidemiologic Data
48. Q: How can a before-and-after salary comparison exaggerate the effect of a political decision?
A: Show answer
The chapter's example compares a low minimum salary from rural districts across a state with a higher salary range in New York City. The two figures describe different geographic groups, so they do not form a like-for-like comparison of the same teachers before and after the policy. The apparent improvement may reflect the different locations and salary categories rather than the effect of the governor's action. A valid before-and-after comparison should use the same population, geographic scope, job categories, salary definition, and time basis, while accounting for other changes that occurred between the two periods.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite the salary comparison using the same districts and teacher categories in both periods, and identify other factors that may influence salary changes.
Key point:
- Before-and-after figures must describe comparable groups and measures.
- A change in the comparison group can masquerade as a policy effect.
Source links:
- W. W. Norton: How to Lie with Statistics
- Statistics Canada: Questions to Ask About Surveys
49. Q: How can before-and-after photographs create the same kind of misleading comparison as semiattached statistics?
A: Show answer
A before-and-after image may appear to show the effect of a product or intervention even though other elements changed between the two photographs. The chapter describes examples in which furniture was added, lighting and image size changed, or a person altered their pose and expression. The visual difference may therefore be caused partly or entirely by factors other than the advertised product. A fair comparison keeps camera angle, lighting, distance, background, pose, timing, and other conditions consistent, and discloses any material changes. The same logic applies to numerical comparisons: both sides must be measured under equivalent conditions.
Study instruction:
- Design a fair before-and-after photographic test and list every condition that must remain constant or be disclosed.
Key point:
- A visual comparison is only persuasive when the conditions are comparable.
- Other changes can be mistaken for the effect of the advertised intervention.
Source links:
- Federal Trade Commission: Health Products Compliance Guidance
- W. W. Norton: How to Lie with Statistics
50. Q: What practical checklist can help identify a semiattached figure before accepting its conclusion?
A: Show answer
Ask what was measured, whether the evidence directly supports the claim, and whether the study conditions match real-world use. Identify the comparator and baseline behind every percentage. For counts, find the population or exposure denominator and check whether the numerator includes the right people and events. For surveys, inspect the exact question and whether it measures opinion, behavior, or an external condition. For financial ratios, identify the numerator, denominator, period, and accounting definition. For trends, check whether reporting rules, case definitions, population composition, and measurement methods remained consistent. The chapter's central lesson is to reconnect every figure to the question it is being used to answer.
Study instruction:
- Apply the checklist to one advertisement, poll, financial statement, health statistic, or safety report and write a short assessment of what the figure does and does not prove.
Key point:
- A figure is useful only when it is properly attached to the conclusion.
- Check relevance, comparator, denominator, population, definitions, and reporting conditions.
- Do not allow a precise number to stand in for a missing argument.
Source links:
- W. W. Norton: How to Lie with Statistics
- Federal Trade Commission: Health Products Compliance Guidance
- CDC: Describing Epidemiologic Data
Rows: 50
Table 8
Post Hoc Reasoning, Correlation, and Causal Inference
1. Q: What is the post hoc fallacy, and why does it matter when interpreting statistical relationships?
A: Show answer
The post hoc fallacy is the error of concluding that because event B occurred after event A, A must have caused B. In statistical reasoning, it often appears when two variables are observed to vary together and the analyst turns that association into a causal explanation without adequate evidence. The sequence may be real, and the association may be statistically strong, but neither fact alone identifies the mechanism. Chapter 8 illustrates the error with a reported relationship between smoking and lower college grades: even if the study measured the association carefully, it did not by itself show that smoking caused lower grades. Reverse causation, common causes, selection effects, chance, and other explanations must be considered before a causal claim is accepted. The practical danger is that a plausible story can be mistaken for a demonstrated explanation, leading to ineffective or harmful decisions.
Study instruction:
- "Rephrase the difference between “B followed A” and “A caused B,” then identify at least three alternative explanations for an observed association."
Key point:
- Temporal order is not proof of causation.
- A convincing causal explanation requires more than a sequence or correlation.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Correlation and causation
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
2. Q: How does correlation differ from causation?
A: Show answer
Correlation describes the direction and degree to which variables vary together. Causation is a claim that a change in one factor produces a change in another, under specified conditions. A positive correlation means higher values of one variable tend to accompany higher values of the other; a negative correlation means higher values of one tend to accompany lower values of the other. Neither sign establishes the causal direction or mechanism. For example, ice-cream sales and sunscreen sales may rise together during hot weather, but buying sunscreen does not ordinarily cause people to buy ice cream. Seasonal temperature can influence both. Correlation is useful for describing patterns, prediction, and generating hypotheses; causal analysis asks what would happen to the outcome if the exposure or intervention were changed while relevant alternatives were addressed.
Study instruction:
- "Write one example of a positive correlation and one of a causal relationship, and explain why the two claims are not interchangeable."
Key point:
- Correlation describes co-movement; causation describes an effect.
- A correlation coefficient does not identify why variables move together.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Correlation and causation
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
3. Q: Why can a statistically significant correlation still fail to establish causation?
A: Show answer
Statistical significance addresses how compatible the observed data are with a specified statistical null model, subject to the study's assumptions. It does not automatically address confounding, selection bias, information bias, reverse causation, measurement error, or a flawed design. A very small p-value can indicate that random sampling variation alone is an unlikely explanation under the model, but it cannot repair a biased comparison or prove the proposed mechanism. The CDC distinguishes chance from other explanations for an observed association and advises investigators to evaluate selection bias, information bias, confounding, and investigator error before making causal judgments. A large sample can make a small association statistically significant while its practical importance remains limited. The correct conclusion should therefore separate evidence that an association exists from evidence that the exposure caused the outcome.
Study instruction:
- "Review a published result with a p-value and list the design-related explanations that statistical significance does not eliminate."
Key point:
- Statistical significance is not a certificate of causal validity.
- Assess design, bias, confounding, and practical magnitude separately.
Source links:
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
4. Q: What are the principal explanations for an observed association between two variables?
A: Show answer
An observed association can arise because A causes B, B causes A, both variables influence each other, a third factor causes both, one variable affects the other indirectly through a mediator, or the pattern occurs by chance. It may also be distorted by selection bias, information or measurement bias, data-processing errors, or changes in how the variables were defined. Some relationships combine several mechanisms: an initial causal effect may create feedback, while common causes also influence both variables. Chapter 8 emphasizes chance correlation, ambiguous direction, common causes, and trends over time. A responsible analysis does not select the explanation that best fits the analyst's preference; it develops competing hypotheses and asks what evidence would distinguish them.
Study instruction:
- "For a relationship you encounter at work, draw at least three competing causal explanations and identify evidence that could discriminate among them."
Key point:
- Multiple explanations can fit the same observed pattern.
- A causal conclusion should survive serious consideration of plausible alternatives.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
5. Q: What is reverse causation, and how can it distort interpretation?
A: Show answer
Reverse causation occurs when the outcome is partly or wholly responsible for the exposure that an analyst has treated as its cause. In the chapter's smoking-and-grades illustration, it is possible to ask whether lower academic performance could influence smoking behavior rather than smoking causing lower grades. That alternative is not proved merely because it is possible; it demonstrates why direction must be investigated. Reverse causation is especially plausible when exposure and outcome are measured at the same time, when behavior responds to an existing condition, or when early symptoms influence later exposure. Establishing that the proposed cause precedes the outcome is an essential first step, but temporal order alone is not sufficient to prove causation.
Study instruction:
- "Identify which variable occurs first in a proposed causal claim and explain how the outcome might influence the supposed exposure."
Key point:
- Check whether the outcome could influence the exposure.
- Cross-sectional association often leaves causal direction unresolved.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
6. Q: What is a confounding variable, and how can it create a misleading relationship?
A: Show answer
A confounder is a third factor associated with the exposure and causally relevant to the outcome, so that the observed exposure–outcome association may partly or wholly reflect that factor. For example, age can explain why schoolchildren's shoe size and vocabulary are associated: older children tend to have both larger feet and larger vocabularies, without either variable causing the other. In the chapter's examples, social characteristics or historical trends may influence both behaviors being compared. Confounding can exaggerate, reduce, conceal, or even reverse an association. Investigators should identify plausible confounders before analysis, measure them carefully, and use an appropriate design or analytical method to address them. Statistical adjustment is not automatic protection: an unmeasured or poorly measured confounder can remain.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a simple causal diagram showing an exposure, an outcome, and a common cause; state how the common cause affects interpretation."
Key point:
- A common cause can produce association without direct causation.
- Identify confounders using subject knowledge and study design, not only automated model selection.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
7. Q: How can a common time trend produce spurious correlation?
A: Show answer
Two unrelated series can move together because both are influenced by a broader trend, such as inflation, population growth, technological adoption, seasonal conditions, or a general rise in recorded activity. Chapter 8's illustration of ministers' salaries and rum prices is intended to show how a broad rise in prices can create a relationship between otherwise unrelated quantities. If two variables both increase over time, a correlation calculated from their raw levels may largely reflect the shared time trend rather than a direct relationship. Analysts should inspect time plots, consider the underlying process, compare changes rather than only levels when appropriate, and use a time-series model suited to the data. Detrending or differencing can help in some settings, but it does not by itself prove a causal mechanism.
Study instruction:
- "Plot two rising time series and explain how a shared trend could create an apparent relationship between them."
Key point:
- Shared movement over time can create spurious association.
- Investigate time, seasonality, and common trends before assigning a causal interpretation.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Correlation and causation
8. Q: What is a chance correlation, and why is repeated testing a concern?
A: Show answer
A chance correlation is an apparent relationship that arises from random variation rather than a stable underlying connection. When researchers examine many pairs of variables, subgroups, outcomes, time periods, or model specifications, some results may appear unusually strong even if no meaningful relationship exists. The risk increases when analysts selectively report the most striking result and omit unsuccessful analyses. Chapter 8 describes the danger of discarding unwanted results and publicizing only a favorable finding. A sound investigation pre-specifies important hypotheses where feasible, records how many analyses were conducted, reports all relevant results, uses appropriate multiplicity procedures when needed, and seeks independent replication. A single striking correlation discovered after extensive searching should be treated as exploratory until confirmed.
Study instruction:
- "Review a data-mining exercise and record how many variables, outcomes, and subgroup comparisons were tested before the reported relationship was selected."
Key point:
- A selected extreme result may be a product of chance and selective reporting.
- Disclose the search process and seek confirmation in independent data.
Source links:
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
9. Q: Why can a large sample and a highly significant result still be misleading?
A: Show answer
A large sample can reduce random sampling error and make estimates more precise, but it does not remove systematic bias. If the sample is selected in a way that differs from the target population, if important variables are measured inaccurately, or if a confounder is omitted, increasing the sample size may produce a more precise estimate of the wrong quantity. Statistical significance also depends on sample size: a very small effect can become statistically detectable when observations are numerous. Analysts should therefore assess sampling, measurement, missingness, study design, effect size, uncertainty, and relevance to the target population. Chapter 8's opening smoking-and-grades example makes the important distinction that a carefully conducted association study can still be insufficient to support a causal interpretation.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a large observational study with a smaller randomized study and identify which validity questions remain for each."
Key point:
- Precision does not eliminate bias.
- A large sample cannot compensate for an invalid comparison or unsupported causal model.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
10. Q: What is the difference between a direct cause, an indirect cause, and a common cause?
A: Show answer
A direct cause influences an outcome without requiring an intervening variable in the causal pathway being examined. An indirect cause operates through one or more mediators: for example, a training intervention might improve knowledge, which then improves task performance. A common cause influences both variables and can create an association even when neither variable causes the other. These structures imply different questions and interventions. If a mediator is part of the mechanism, adjusting for it may remove part of the total effect being estimated. If a common cause is a confounder, failing to address it can bias the estimated effect. The analyst must therefore define the causal question before selecting variables for adjustment.
Study instruction:
- "For a program evaluation, identify one possible mediator and one possible confounder and explain why they should not be treated identically."
Key point:
- Mediators transmit effects; confounders can distort the exposure–outcome comparison.
- Variable selection must follow the causal question and a defensible causal model.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
11. Q: What is bidirectional causation, and why can it complicate analysis?
A: Show answer
Bidirectional causation exists when A affects B and B also affects A, either simultaneously through feedback or across successive periods. Chapter 8 uses the relationship between income and stock ownership to illustrate reciprocal influence: higher income may enable investment, while investment returns may subsequently increase income. A simple one-way explanation would omit part of the process. Analysts should consider timing, lagged effects, feedback loops, and the possibility that both variables are jointly determined. Longitudinal data, explicit dynamic models, and a clearly specified time sequence can help investigate the relationship. Even then, identifying the direction and magnitude of each effect requires assumptions and evidence beyond a single correlation coefficient.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a two-way causal diagram for income and investment and label the sequence in which each effect may occur."
Key point:
- Feedback can make one-direction explanations incomplete.
- Specify the time sequence and distinguish simultaneous association from lagged effects.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
12. Q: How should an analyst distinguish a plausible explanation from a demonstrated cause?
A: Show answer
A plausible explanation is a story that could account for the data; a demonstrated cause is a conclusion supported by a study design and body of evidence that address serious alternatives. Plausibility is useful for generating hypotheses, but a familiar or persuasive mechanism is not proof. The analyst should ask whether the exposure precedes the outcome, whether the association is replicated, whether the magnitude and pattern are coherent, whether alternative explanations have been addressed, and whether the design can estimate the causal effect being claimed. The CDC describes causal assessment as a reasoned evaluation of strength, biological plausibility, consistency, temporal sequence, and dose-response, alongside checks for chance and bias. These considerations support judgment rather than a mechanical checklist.
Study instruction:
- "Take a persuasive causal story and list the observations that would be required before treating it as established."
Key point:
- A good story is a hypothesis, not proof.
- Evidence must distinguish the proposed cause from competing explanations.
Source links:
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
13. Q: Why is temporal precedence necessary but not sufficient for causation?
A: Show answer
A cause must precede its effect in the causal sequence, so temporal precedence is a necessary condition for ordinary causal claims. However, observing A before B does not establish that A produced B. A third factor may have caused both, the sequence may be coincidental, or the outcome may have been developing before it was measured. Retrospective studies can also misclassify the timing of exposure and outcome. Investigators should define when exposure begins, when the outcome begins, and the relevant risk or follow-up period; then assess whether the proposed sequence is credible and whether alternative explanations remain. The CDC notes that exposure preceding disease can be difficult to document in retrospective investigations.
Study instruction:
- "Create a timeline for a proposed exposure and outcome, marking measurement dates separately from the dates when the underlying events actually began."
Key point:
- Temporal order is required but cannot establish causation alone.
- Measure the timing of the underlying events, not merely the dates recorded in a dataset.
Source links:
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
14. Q: How can dose-response evidence strengthen a causal argument?
A: Show answer
A dose-response pattern exists when changes in the amount, intensity, or duration of an exposure are associated with systematic changes in the outcome. If greater exposure is followed by a greater effect in a theoretically coherent pattern, the relationship may become more credible. However, dose-response is not conclusive: confounding can also vary with exposure level, measurement error can distort the pattern, and some genuine causal relationships have thresholds or non-linear effects. The CDC treats dose-response as one consideration among several and cautions that its absence does not automatically disprove causation. Analysts should define the exposure scale, inspect possible thresholds and saturation, consider reverse causation, and avoid assuming a straight-line relationship without evidence.
Study instruction:
- "Construct a simple exposure-level table and explain what pattern would support, weaken, or complicate a dose-response hypothesis."
Key point:
- Dose-response can strengthen but cannot independently prove causation.
- Assess the exposure scale, thresholds, non-linearity, and competing explanations.
Source links:
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
15. Q: What role does replication play in evaluating causal claims?
A: Show answer
Replication asks whether a finding appears again when the question is tested in other samples, settings, periods, or research teams. A result repeated using different designs and populations is less likely to be a peculiarity of one dataset, one measurement method, or one investigator. Replication is especially important when an original result emerged from many exploratory comparisons or a narrow population. Consistency does not guarantee causation, because repeated studies can share the same bias or confounder; researchers should examine whether the studies are genuinely independent and whether their methods differ in useful ways. The CDC identifies consistency across studies and populations as an important consideration in causal assessment.
Study instruction:
- "Compare two studies reporting the same association and determine whether they provide independent replication or share the same underlying bias."
Key point:
- Independent replication increases confidence in the stability of a finding.
- Check whether repeated results use genuinely different data, settings, and methods.
Source links:
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
16. Q: What is biological or substantive plausibility, and what are its limitations?
A: Show answer
Biological plausibility asks whether a proposed causal mechanism is compatible with established knowledge about the relevant biological process. In non-medical settings, the equivalent question is whether a credible mechanism exists in the relevant social, economic, technical, or organizational system. Plausibility can help distinguish a serious hypothesis from an implausible story, but it should not be used to dismiss surprising evidence automatically. A mechanism may be unknown or incompletely understood, and a plausible mechanism can still be wrong. The CDC treats plausibility as one factor to weigh alongside temporality, consistency, strength, dose-response, and alternative explanations. Analysts should explain the mechanism and identify what evidence supports each link in it.
Study instruction:
- "Describe the proposed mechanism between an exposure and outcome, then identify evidence needed to test each link in the mechanism."
Key point:
- Mechanistic plausibility is supportive evidence, not a substitute for study design.
- Do not confuse a plausible mechanism with an empirically established effect.
Source links:
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
17. Q: How can selection bias create a misleading association?
A: Show answer
Selection bias occurs when inclusion in the study, continued participation, or availability of records is related to the exposure and outcome in a way that distorts the comparison. For example, if a survey disproportionately includes people who are unusually engaged with a program, the relationship between participation and reported outcomes may not represent the wider target population. Selection can also arise from restricting analysis to people who share a characteristic influenced by both exposure and outcome. The CDC lists selection bias among the explanations that must be considered before a causal conclusion. Investigators should define eligibility in advance, document non-response and exclusions, compare participants with the target population where possible, and conduct sensitivity analyses when selection may be consequential.
Study instruction:
- "Inspect a participant flow diagram and identify where inclusion, exclusion, or loss to follow-up could distort the exposure–outcome relationship."
Key point:
- A biased comparison group can produce a misleading association.
- Document how participants entered, remained in, or left the analysis.
Source links:
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
18. Q: How can information bias or measurement error distort a relationship?
A: Show answer
Information bias arises when exposure, outcome, or other variables are measured or recorded inaccurately in a systematic way. Recall bias may occur when people with an outcome remember past exposures differently from those without it. Interviewers may ask questions differently across groups, administrative records may use inconsistent definitions, and a proxy measure may not represent the underlying concept. Measurement error can weaken a real association, create an apparent one, or distort its direction depending on how the error occurs. The CDC includes information bias among the key explanations for spurious associations. Good practice requires clear operational definitions, validated instruments where available, consistent collection procedures, training, data checks, and transparent reporting of missing or uncertain values.
Study instruction:
- "Choose an exposure and outcome and specify how each would be measured consistently across comparison groups."
Key point:
- Measurement quality is part of causal validity.
- Use consistent definitions and assess whether errors differ between groups.
Source links:
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
19. Q: What is the difference between confounding and mediation?
A: Show answer
Confounding occurs when a third factor influences both the exposure and outcome and thereby distorts their observed relationship. Mediation occurs when a variable lies on the causal pathway through which the exposure affects the outcome. Consider a workplace training program: prior employee motivation might influence both enrollment and performance, making motivation a potential confounder; knowledge gained through training may transmit part of the program's effect, making knowledge a mediator. Adjusting for a confounder can help reduce bias, while adjusting for a mediator may remove part of the total effect the analyst intends to estimate. The distinction depends on the causal question and the timing and role of each variable, not simply on whether it correlates with the exposure and outcome.
Study instruction:
- "For a program evaluation, classify baseline motivation and post-training knowledge as confounder or mediator and justify the classification."
Key point:
- Confounders distort comparisons; mediators transmit effects.
- Do not adjust for every correlated variable without considering its causal role.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
20. Q: Can multiple regression prove that an observed relationship is causal?
A: Show answer
No. Multiple regression can estimate conditional associations and may reduce confounding from measured variables when the model is correctly specified and the relevant assumptions are defensible. It cannot guarantee that all confounders have been identified or measured, eliminate selection bias, correct poor temporal ordering, or establish the causal direction by itself. Results can also be sensitive to functional-form choices, omitted variables, measurement error, collinearity, and inappropriate adjustment for mediators or colliders. The National Academies' reference guide explains that regression is one method for addressing confounding but emphasizes that association is not causation, even in complex observational analyses. Regression should be part of a design-based causal argument, not a substitute for one.
Study instruction:
- "Review a regression model and list the causal assumptions that remain untested after the software produces its coefficients."
Key point:
- Statistical adjustment does not automatically create a valid causal comparison.
- Explain which confounders were measured and which assumptions remain uncertain.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
21. Q: What is the difference between an observational study and a controlled experiment?
A: Show answer
In an observational study, researchers observe exposures and outcomes as they occur; participants or external circumstances determine exposure rather than the investigator assigning it. In a controlled experiment, investigators assign participants or units to treatment and comparison conditions and measure subsequent outcomes. Observational studies are essential when intervention is impractical, unethical, or impossible, but exposed and unexposed groups may differ in important ways. A well-designed randomized experiment tends to make groups comparable at baseline and thereby reduces confounding. Neither design is automatically flawless: experiments can suffer from non-compliance, attrition, measurement problems, and limited generalizability; observational studies can provide strong evidence when carefully designed and triangulated.
Study instruction:
- "Classify a program evaluation as observational or experimental and explain who determined exposure to the intervention."
Key point:
- The allocation process is a central difference between study designs.
- Study who selected the exposure and how comparable the groups are.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Correlation and causation
22. Q: Why does random assignment strengthen causal inference?
A: Show answer
Random assignment uses a chance process to allocate study units to treatment and comparison groups. If implemented properly, it tends to balance both measured and unmeasured baseline characteristics across groups, reducing systematic differences that could confound the estimated treatment effect. Randomization does not guarantee perfect balance in every finite sample, and it does not solve problems that arise after assignment, such as differential attrition, non-compliance, contamination, or biased outcome measurement. Researchers must report the allocation process, preserve the assigned groups in the primary analysis where appropriate, and assess implementation. The National Academies describes randomized controlled experiments as generally providing more secure causal inferences than observational comparisons, within the limits of the study.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how random assignment differs from selecting a sample randomly from a population; give one purpose for each."
Key point:
- Random assignment improves comparability between treatment groups.
- Do not confuse random sampling with random allocation to treatment.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Correlation and causation
23. Q: When are observational studies necessary, and how can they still support causal conclusions?
A: Show answer
Observational studies are necessary when researchers cannot ethically or practically assign exposure, when the exposure is a naturally occurring condition, or when an intervention has already happened. They can contribute to causal conclusions when the research question is well defined, the comparison group is appropriate, exposure precedes outcome, important confounders are addressed, measurements are credible, and findings are consistent across different populations and designs. Natural experiments and quasi-experimental designs can strengthen inference when external events create a comparison resembling random assignment. Observational evidence should be assessed as a body of evidence rather than dismissed automatically or treated as equivalent to randomization. The National Academies notes that observational studies can be useful, including in circumstances where experiments are impossible.
Study instruction:
- "Identify an ethical or practical reason a randomized experiment cannot be used and propose an observational or quasi-experimental alternative."
Key point:
- Observational evidence can be valuable but requires explicit assumptions.
- Use design, comparison groups, confounder assessment, and replication to strengthen inference.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Designing and Conducting Analytic Studies
24. Q: What is a natural experiment, and what must be checked before relying on it?
A: Show answer
A natural experiment occurs when an external event or policy change creates treatment and comparison groups in a way that approximates random assignment, even though researchers did not allocate exposure. A classic example discussed in the National Academies reference guide is a historical water-supply change that created different exposure conditions across households during a cholera epidemic. The causal strength depends on whether the groups were otherwise comparable and whether the external change affected the outcome through the exposure of interest rather than through other pathways. Analysts should examine how assignment occurred, baseline differences, timing, spillovers, concurrent changes, and the plausibility of alternative explanations. Calling a study a natural experiment does not make its assumptions automatically true.
Study instruction:
- "Describe the external event that creates the comparison and list the conditions required for it to approximate random assignment."
Key point:
- A natural experiment is credible only when its assignment mechanism is defensible.
- Test comparability and alternative pathways before interpreting the estimate causally.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Designing and Conducting Analytic Studies
25. Q: How should an analyst investigate the possibility that two variables influence each other?
A: Show answer
Begin by specifying the expected time sequence and distinguishing immediate from delayed effects. Use repeated observations where possible to see whether earlier values of A predict later values of B, whether earlier B predicts later A, or whether both patterns occur. Consider whether a third factor drives both, and define the causal effect the analysis is intended to estimate. Cross-lagged or other longitudinal models may describe temporal patterns, but they do not by themselves remove confounding or prove causation. Where feasible, use interventions or credible external changes to isolate one direction of influence. Chapter 8's income-and-stock example illustrates why reciprocal relationships should not be forced into a single-direction story.
Study instruction:
- "Create a two-period diagram and identify what data would help distinguish A-to-B, B-to-A, and reciprocal effects."
Key point:
- Feedback requires explicit timing and direction.
- Use longitudinal evidence and a defensible identification strategy, not a single cross-sectional correlation.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
26. Q: Why can a correlation change or reverse when data are divided into subgroups?
A: Show answer
An overall association can differ from subgroup associations because groups have different compositions, baseline risks, or relationships among variables. This can produce aggregation effects and, in some cases, Simpson's paradox, where a pattern in combined data reverses after stratification. A third variable such as age, institution, location, or baseline severity may influence both group membership and outcome. Analysts should examine whether the groups are comparable, whether the aggregation is meaningful, and whether subgroup analysis was planned or selected after seeing the results. The National Academies reference guide discusses how combining heterogeneous groups can make a characteristic such as college selectivity act as a confounder. Subgroup analysis should explain the underlying structure rather than simply choose the most favorable result.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate or inspect an overall rate and subgroup rates; explain how differing group sizes can change the aggregate relationship."
Key point:
- Aggregated data can conceal important subgroup patterns.
- Check composition and stratification before interpreting an overall correlation.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
27. Q: What is the ecological fallacy, and how is it related to the chapter's warnings?
A: Show answer
The ecological fallacy occurs when a relationship observed across groups or geographic areas is assumed to apply to individuals within those groups. For example, a region with higher average education and higher income does not prove that every more-educated individual in that region earns more than every less-educated individual. Group-level patterns may reflect differences in age, industry, migration, urbanization, or other characteristics. Chapter 8's comparisons between regions and populations illustrate the danger of treating aggregate statistics as direct evidence about individual behavior or risk. Analysts should match the unit of analysis to the claim and avoid moving from group-level correlations to individual causal conclusions without individual-level evidence.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the unit of analysis in a published statistic and state whether its conclusion concerns individuals, organizations, or aggregate areas."
Key point:
- Group-level association does not automatically describe individual-level relationships.
- Keep the level of evidence aligned with the level of the claim.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
28. Q: Why can a relationship be real but have little predictive value for an individual case?
A: Show answer
A population-level correlation describes an average tendency, not a deterministic rule for every person or event. Even when a genuine relationship exists, individual outcomes may vary widely because of other influences, measurement uncertainty, and random variation. Chapter 8 uses the example of height and weight: taller boys tend to weigh more on average, but a shorter boy can weigh more than a taller boy. Similarly, a positive average return to education does not establish what a particular person's earnings would have been under a different educational choice. Individual prediction requires attention to the distribution of outcomes, prediction intervals, relevant personal characteristics, and the limits of extrapolation. A group-level relationship should not be converted into an individual guarantee.
Study instruction:
- "Find an average relationship and identify realistic exceptions; explain why the average cannot determine one person's outcome."
Key point:
- A population tendency is not an individual certainty.
- Distinguish average effects, prediction, and individual counterfactual outcomes.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Correlation and causation
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
29. Q: What is the difference between positive, negative, and zero correlation?
A: Show answer
A positive correlation means that higher values of one variable tend to accompany higher values of the other. A negative correlation means that higher values of one tend to accompany lower values of the other. A correlation of zero indicates no linear association as measured by the usual Pearson coefficient; it does not necessarily mean that no relationship of any kind exists. A strong curved or other non-linear relationship can have a Pearson correlation near zero. The coefficient ranges from -1 to +1 for the standard Pearson measure, with the sign indicating direction and the absolute magnitude indicating strength of linear association. Interpretation should be supported by a scatterplot and knowledge of how the variables were measured.
Study instruction:
- "Classify three scatterplots as positive, negative, or near-zero linear association and describe the visible pattern."
Key point:
- The sign describes direction; magnitude describes linear strength.
- Zero Pearson correlation does not rule out a non-linear relationship.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Correlation and causation
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
30. Q: Why should a scatterplot be inspected before interpreting a correlation coefficient?
A: Show answer
A scatterplot reveals the shape, spread, clusters, outliers, and possible subgroups in the data. A single coefficient compresses the relationship into one number and can conceal important structure. A plot may show a curved pattern, a cluster of separate populations, a restricted range, or one influential observation that dominates the result. It can also reveal data-entry errors or a relationship that changes across the range. The National Academies reference guide explains that Pearson correlation is designed to measure linear association and can be distorted by outliers. Analysts should therefore examine plots and descriptive summaries before relying on a coefficient or fitting a model.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a scatterplot for two numeric variables and annotate any curvature, clusters, outliers, or restricted range."
Key point:
- A coefficient cannot replace inspection of the underlying data pattern.
- Visualize the data before interpreting a summary statistic.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Correlation and causation
31. Q: How can outliers distort a correlation coefficient?
A: Show answer
An outlier is an observation far from the general pattern of the data. Because correlation is calculated from deviations around the variables' means, an extreme point can have disproportionate influence. It may weaken a strong relationship, create an apparent relationship where little exists, or reverse the sign. The National Academies reference guide illustrates that one outlier can move a correlation from nearly perfect to close to zero, or from near zero to nearly one. Analysts should investigate whether the point is a data error, a valid but unusual case, or evidence that the population contains different subgroups. Deleting an outlier solely because it changes the desired result is not defensible; any exclusion needs a documented substantive and methodological justification.
Study instruction:
- "Recalculate a correlation with and without an influential point and explain why both results should be examined."
Key point:
- Outliers can dominate correlation estimates.
- Investigate unusual observations and document any justified exclusion or robust alternative.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
32. Q: What happens when the true relationship is non-linear?
A: Show answer
A non-linear relationship changes at a rate or in a pattern that cannot be adequately represented by a straight line. Pearson correlation measures linear association, so it may be small even when variables have a strong curved relationship. For example, an outcome may increase with exposure up to a threshold and then decline, or it may rise rapidly at first and level off. Chapter 8's rainfall-and-crop illustration describes a relationship that can change from positive to negative when excessive rainfall damages the crop. Analysts should use scatterplots, subject-matter knowledge, and appropriate non-linear models or transformations when justified. They should not infer that a near-zero Pearson coefficient proves the absence of any meaningful relationship.
Study instruction:
- "Sketch a threshold or inverted-U relationship and explain why a single linear coefficient may misrepresent it."
Key point:
- A linear statistic can miss a meaningful curved pattern.
- Inspect functional form and avoid extrapolating a linear trend beyond its observed range.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Correlation and causation
33. Q: Why is extrapolating a correlation beyond the observed data dangerous?
A: Show answer
An association established within one range, population, period, or set of conditions may not continue outside that range. The mechanism may change, a threshold may be crossed, a resource may become limiting, or the population may differ. Chapter 8 uses rainfall and corn yields: additional rain may improve growth up to a point, while excessive rain can damage the crop and reverse the relationship. Extrapolating the positive segment would lead to a false prediction. Analysts should identify the range of observed data, test for non-linearity and thresholds, explain the conditions under which the relationship was estimated, and avoid making claims about populations or time periods not represented in the study.
Study instruction:
- "Mark the observed data range on a graph and explain what additional evidence is needed before predicting outside it."
Key point:
- A relationship need not remain stable beyond its observed range.
- State the scope of the evidence and test for thresholds before extrapolating.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Correlation and causation
34. Q: How can a secular trend be mistaken for a causal relationship?
A: Show answer
A secular trend is a long-term movement in a variable over time. If two unrelated variables both increase because of population growth, inflation, technology, or broader social change, they may show a strong correlation even without a direct causal link. Chapter 8's historical examples of unrelated quantities rising together demonstrate this risk. Analysts should plot each series over time, examine whether the relationship persists after accounting for the trend, consider seasonality and structural breaks, and assess whether the proposed mechanism is credible. Methods such as differencing, detrending, or time-series regression can help characterize the data, but model choice must match the underlying process. A shared trend should not be presented as proof that one variable caused the other.
Study instruction:
- "Select two variables that rise over time and identify a shared trend that could explain their correlation."
Key point:
- Common trends can make unrelated variables appear connected.
- Separate long-term movement from the relationship of substantive interest.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Correlation and causation
35. Q: How should historical examples in a statistics book be treated when evaluating modern evidence?
A: Show answer
Historical examples are valuable for illustrating reasoning errors, but their empirical details should not automatically be treated as current scientific conclusions. Chapter 8 uses smoking and grades, education and earnings, marriage patterns, milk consumption and cancer, and other period-specific comparisons to demonstrate how correlation can be misinterpreted. The reader should distinguish the author's methodological lesson from the historical validity, measurement quality, and contemporary relevance of each example. Modern evidence may use different populations, definitions, study designs, and accumulated research. When a real-world claim matters, consult current authoritative evidence and verify the underlying study rather than repeating a historical illustration as established fact.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one historical example from the chapter and identify which part illustrates the reasoning principle and which part would require fresh empirical verification."
Key point:
- Use historical examples to learn the method, not to substitute for current evidence.
- Separate illustrative anecdotes from current evidence about the real-world topic.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
36. Q: How should the college-education and earnings example be interpreted without committing the post hoc fallacy?
A: Show answer
If college graduates earn more on average than non-graduates, the observed difference is consistent with a positive association between education and earnings. It does not, by itself, show how much of the difference was caused by college. Prior ability, family resources, social networks, geography, motivation, field of study, and labor-market selection may influence both educational attainment and earnings. The relevant causal question is counterfactual: how would the same person's earnings have differed under a different education pathway, all else appropriately considered? Researchers can strengthen inference through credible comparison groups, longitudinal data, natural experiments, and careful adjustment, while recognizing that assumptions remain. The chapter warns against converting a group average into a guarantee for an individual.
Study instruction:
- "List possible pre-college differences between graduates and non-graduates and explain how each could influence later earnings."
Key point:
- An earnings gap is not automatically the causal return to education.
- Distinguish observed group differences from the effect of the educational decision.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Correlation and causation
37. Q: Why can a comparison of marriage rates among graduates and non-graduates be misleading?
A: Show answer
A difference in marriage rates between education groups is an association, not proof that attending college caused a person to marry or remain unmarried. Age, cohort, socioeconomic background, geography, cultural norms, career plans, and pre-existing preferences may influence both education and marital outcomes. The chapter criticizes a magazine's causal interpretation of a comparison involving middle-aged graduates because the statistics did not establish what would have happened to those same people without college. A valid interpretation must also check the denominator, population definition, age range, time period, and whether the comparison groups are comparable. Historical claims about gender and marriage should not be repeated as current general rules.
Study instruction:
- "Inspect a headline about education and marriage and identify the missing comparison, timing, and confounding information."
Key point:
- Group differences cannot reveal an individual's counterfactual outcome.
- Check the population, denominator, cohort, and alternative explanations before inferring cause.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
38. Q: How can age or cohort effects create a misleading comparison between groups?
A: Show answer
An age effect occurs when outcomes differ because people are at different stages of life. A cohort effect occurs when groups born or socialized in different periods have different experiences, norms, or opportunities. Chapter 8's walking-posture illustration describes older and younger women who learned different conventions in different eras; comparing the groups at one point in time could be mistaken for evidence that aging itself changed walking posture. To distinguish age from cohort, researchers need suitable longitudinal or repeated-cohort designs and must consider period effects that influence everyone at a particular time. Simple cross-sectional comparisons often cannot separate these explanations.
Study instruction:
- "Design a study that could distinguish aging effects from differences between generations and explain its limitations."
Key point:
- Age, cohort, and period can be entangled in cross-sectional data.
- Match the design to the time-related explanation being tested.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
39. Q: How can a correlation be used responsibly in decision-making?
A: Show answer
A correlation can be used to describe a pattern, identify a population or period for further investigation, support prediction when validated, and generate a hypothesis for intervention research. It should not be converted directly into a policy or operational intervention unless the causal basis and consequences are adequately assessed. Decision-makers should ask whether the relationship is stable, whether the variables are measured well, whether it applies to the target population, whether the effect is practically meaningful, and whether acting on it could create unintended consequences. Where causal certainty is limited but action is urgent, decisions can still be made using a transparent assessment of risks, uncertainty, reversibility, and the cost of delay.
Study instruction:
- "For a proposed intervention based on a correlation, specify what further evidence would be required and what precautionary action may be justified meanwhile."
Key point:
- Correlation can guide inquiry and prediction without proving an intervention effect.
- Match the strength of the decision to the strength and limits of the evidence.
Source links:
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Correlation and causation
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
40. Q: What questions should be asked before accepting a claim that A causes B?
A: Show answer
A careful review should ask: Was A measured before B occurred? Was the association observed in an appropriate population and comparison group? How large is the effect and how uncertain is it? Could chance, selection bias, information bias, confounding, reverse causation, or investigator error explain it? Is there a credible mechanism? Does the finding replicate across different studies? Is there a dose-response or threshold pattern where one would be expected? Does the analysis use an appropriate unit of analysis and account for the study design? What alternative explanations were tested, and what assumptions remain? The CDC's interpretation checklists organize many of these questions and emphasize that causal judgment is not a mechanical scoring exercise.
Study instruction:
- "Use this set of questions to conduct a structured review of a news report that makes a causal claim from observational data."
Key point:
- Causal review is a sequence of design, bias, mechanism, and replication checks.
- Demand transparent reasoning and disclose what the evidence cannot establish.
Source links:
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
41. Q: How should causal claims be worded when the evidence is only correlational?
A: Show answer
When the study is observational and the causal assumptions have not been established, use language such as “was associated with,” “was correlated with,” “participants with X tended to have Y,” or “the finding is consistent with several explanations.” Avoid words such as “caused,” “led to,” “resulted in,” or “prevented” unless the design and evidence justify causal interpretation. If causal evidence is suggestive but uncertain, qualify the claim and state the key limitations. Precise wording prevents readers, managers, or policymakers from treating an association as an intervention effect. The National Academies emphasizes the distinction between association and causation, while the CDC recommends evaluating alternative explanations before using causal language.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite three causal headlines as appropriately qualified statements based on observational evidence."
Key point:
- Use wording that reflects what the study design can support.
- Do not let confident language exceed the strength of the evidence.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
42. Q: How should a researcher plan an analysis to reduce post hoc interpretation?
A: Show answer
The researcher should define the causal or descriptive question before examining outcomes, specify the target population, exposure, outcome, comparison group, time frame, and plausible confounders, and prepare an analysis plan. The plan should identify data sources, variable definitions, missing-data procedures, subgroup analyses, statistical measures, and the sequence of analyses. Exploratory analyses can still be useful, but they should be labelled as exploratory and distinguished from pre-specified tests. The CDC recommends planning the analysis before data collection and documenting hypotheses, variables, potential confounders, study design, and analytical strategy. A transparent plan reduces selective reporting and makes it easier to assess whether the conclusion was driven by the data or by an after-the-fact choice.
Study instruction:
- "Draft a one-page analysis plan for a study, including hypotheses, variables, confounders, and planned sensitivity checks."
Key point:
- Pre-specification makes analytical decisions more transparent.
- Plan the analysis before seeing which result is most attractive.
Source links:
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Designing and Conducting Analytic Studies
43. Q: What documentation should accompany a reported correlation?
A: Show answer
A useful report should state the research question, population, sampling or selection process, study period, unit of analysis, variable definitions, data sources, missing-data treatment, statistical method, coefficient, uncertainty measure, and relevant visualizations. It should identify potential confounders and explain whether and how they were addressed. The report should disclose the number of comparisons or models considered, important exclusions, sensitivity analyses, and limitations. If a causal interpretation is proposed, the author should state the design and assumptions that support it and discuss plausible alternatives. Without this information, readers cannot assess whether the correlation is robust, meaningful, or generalizable.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a checklist for reviewing a correlation report and mark which items are missing from a sample article."
Key point:
- Transparent methods allow readers to judge the evidence.
- Report design, measurement, uncertainty, alternatives, and analytical choices.
Source links:
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
44. Q: How should missing data be considered when interpreting a relationship?
A: Show answer
Missing data can bias an association when the likelihood of missingness is related to the exposure, outcome, or factors that influence them. For example, people with poor outcomes may be less likely to complete follow-up, or participants with certain exposures may be less willing to disclose them. Simply deleting incomplete records can change the composition of the sample and distort the relationship. Analysts should describe the amount and pattern of missingness, compare included and excluded observations, justify the chosen handling method, and test how conclusions change under plausible assumptions. The CDC advises analysts to understand the data, including variables with many or all values missing, as part of planning and interpreting an analysis.
Study instruction:
- "Inspect a dataset's missingness by exposure and outcome group and explain whether complete-case analysis could bias the result."
Key point:
- Missingness can affect both the sample and the estimated relationship.
- Report missing-data patterns and test whether conclusions depend on assumptions about missingness.
Source links:
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
45. Q: How can data errors or investigator mistakes create an artificial association?
A: Show answer
An apparent relationship can result from errors in data entry, coding, record linkage, formula selection, variable recoding, denominator construction, or interpretation. A misplaced formula reference or inconsistent definition can produce systematic differences that look meaningful. The CDC explicitly includes investigator error in its checklist of possible explanations for a spurious association and recommends rigorous checking and colleague review. Quality controls should include data dictionaries, range and logic checks, duplicate detection, reconciliation to source records, reproducible code, independent review, and documented corrections. The analyst should verify the result from raw data through final tables before presenting it as evidence.
Study instruction:
- "Trace a reported statistic from raw records through a spreadsheet or script and identify checkpoints where errors could enter."
Key point:
- An analytical result is only as reliable as its data and calculations.
- Use reproducible workflows, validation, and independent review.
Source links:
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
46. Q: What is the difference between statistical significance and practical significance?
A: Show answer
Statistical significance concerns whether an observed result is sufficiently incompatible with a specified null model under the test's assumptions. Practical significance concerns whether the size of the relationship or effect matters in the real context. A tiny difference may be statistically significant in a very large dataset but too small to justify a costly intervention. Conversely, an important effect may be estimated imprecisely in a small study. Decision-makers should examine effect sizes, confidence intervals, baseline risks, costs, benefits, and consequences rather than relying on a p-value alone. Chapter 8 warns against being impressed by the appearance of precise figures when the causal interpretation is not justified.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a small but statistically significant association with a larger but uncertain estimate and explain what decision information is still needed."
Key point:
