- A p-value does not measure importance or prove causation.
- Interpret magnitude and uncertainty in the context of the decision.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
47. Q: Why is a confidence interval useful when evaluating an association?
A: Show answer
A confidence interval communicates the range of effect estimates reasonably compatible with the data and the statistical model at a stated confidence level. It shows precision and helps readers distinguish a narrowly estimated small association from a wide range of plausible effects. It does not correct confounding, selection bias, information bias, or poor measurement, and it does not convert an association into a causal effect. The interval must be interpreted in relation to the measure used, the study design, and the assumptions. Reporting only whether an interval excludes a null value can encourage a binary interpretation that ignores magnitude and practical importance.
Study instruction:
- "Read a confidence interval for a correlation or risk ratio and explain both its precision and its causal limitations."
Key point:
- Uncertainty intervals describe statistical precision, not freedom from bias.
- Interpret intervals alongside design quality and plausible alternative explanations.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
48. Q: How can a researcher avoid cherry-picking correlations?
A: Show answer
Cherry-picking occurs when analysts select only favorable results from a larger set of analyses and omit those that do not support the preferred story. It can arise through selective outcome reporting, subgroup selection, changing the time window, trying multiple variable definitions, or publishing only successful replications. Safeguards include pre-registering primary hypotheses where appropriate, retaining analysis logs, reporting all planned outcomes, disclosing exploratory analyses, describing multiplicity, sharing code and data when ethically and legally possible, and seeking independent replication. Chapter 8's discussion of discarded toothpaste results illustrates how selective publication can make chance patterns appear reliable. Transparency is essential even when the full analysis produces an untidy result.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the planned analyses with the published tables and identify outcomes or subgroups that were omitted."
Key point:
- Selective reporting can turn random variation into a persuasive but false narrative.
- Disclose the full analytical search and distinguish confirmatory from exploratory findings.
Source links:
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
49. Q: What is the danger of assuming that a real association must have a useful explanation?
A: Show answer
A statistically real association may still be difficult to explain, weakly predictive, non-causal, or irrelevant to the decision being considered. A pattern can arise from a common cause, a broad time trend, a selection process, or a mechanism not captured by the available data. Even when the association is causal at a population level, the effect may vary by subgroup or be too small to guide individual action. Chapter 8's examples of seemingly absurd relationships are intended to train skepticism toward confident stories built around numbers. The correct response is not to dismiss every unexpected finding, but to investigate its stability, mechanism, scope, and usefulness before assigning meaning.
Study instruction:
- "Select an unexpected correlation and prepare a plan to test whether it is robust, explainable, and useful."
Key point:
- A real pattern does not automatically have a meaningful causal interpretation.
- Investigate stability, mechanism, scope, and decision relevance before acting.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Correlation and causation
50. Q: How should managers use correlation analysis without turning it into a causal claim?
A: Show answer
Managers can use correlation to identify patterns worth investigating, monitor indicators, detect unusual co-movement, and generate hypotheses about operational performance. For example, a relationship between staff turnover and service delays may help identify where deeper review is needed. It does not prove that turnover alone caused the delays: workload, funding, leadership changes, seasonality, or service complexity may influence both. Managers should combine statistical analysis with process mapping, interviews, document review, comparison groups, and where feasible, a controlled or quasi-experimental evaluation. Decisions should distinguish monitoring signals from demonstrated intervention effects and should be revisited as new evidence emerges.
Study instruction:
- "Choose two organizational indicators and design a follow-up investigation that tests alternative explanations before recommending an intervention."
Key point:
- Correlation is a diagnostic signal, not an automatic management prescription.
- Connect quantitative patterns to process evidence and causal evaluation.
Source links:
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Designing and Conducting Analytic Studies
51. Q: What would a rigorous investigation of a suspected causal relationship look like?
A: Show answer
A rigorous investigation begins with a precise question defining the exposure, outcome, population, time frame, and causal contrast. Researchers develop a causal model, identify plausible confounders and competing explanations, select a design that can answer the question, and define how variables will be measured. They establish a comparison group, plan sample size and analysis, address missing data, and document quality controls. After analysis, they assess effect magnitude and uncertainty, examine bias and sensitivity, test consistency across studies or subgroups, and consider whether the proposed mechanism and timing are credible. The conclusion should state what is supported, what remains uncertain, and what further evidence is needed. The CDC's field investigation guidance emphasizes hypothesis-led design and an analysis plan that connects data collection to the research question.
Study instruction:
- "Develop a short protocol for a causal question and include the design, variables, comparison, confounders, and interpretation plan."
Key point:
- Causal inference is a complete research process, not a correlation calculation.
- Align question, design, data, analysis, and conclusion from the outset.
Source links:
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Designing and Conducting Analytic Studies
52. Q: What are the most important lessons from Chapter 8, “Post Hoc Rides Again”?
A: Show answer
The chapter's central lesson is that association must not be confused with cause and effect. When two variables move together, the explanation may involve direct causation, reverse causation, reciprocal influence, a common cause, chance, selection, a broad time trend, or another source of bias. A large sample, precise coefficient, or statistically significant result does not automatically resolve these alternatives. Correlations can be useful, but they describe tendencies rather than deterministic rules for individuals; they may be non-linear, sensitive to outliers, limited to the observed range, or different across subgroups. Responsible interpretation requires clear questions, sound comparisons, temporal reasoning, transparent analysis, replication, and careful language. The practical habit is to ask what else could explain the pattern before deciding what caused it.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the chapter by explaining one example of reverse causation, one common cause, one chance correlation, and one warning against extrapolation."
Key point:
- Do not infer causation from sequence or correlation alone.
- Question the direction, common causes, chance, trends, scope, and quality of the evidence.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reference Guide on Statistics and Research Methods
- Australian Bureau of Statistics: Correlation and causation
- CDC Field Epidemiology Manual: Analyzing and Interpreting Data
Rows: 52
Table 9
How to Recognize and Prevent Statistical Manipulation
1. Q: What is “statisticulation,” and how does it differ from an ordinary statistical error?
A: Show answer
Statisticulation is the distortion or misleading presentation of statistical material so that an audience is encouraged to reach an inaccurate conclusion. It may involve deliberate manipulation, but it can also arise from incompetence, careless selection, oversimplification, or an unexamined method. The important test is not whether the author admits an intention to deceive; it is whether the presentation gives a fair and interpretable account of the evidence. A numerical result can be arithmetically correct yet misleading because its denominator, comparison period, visual scale, or context has been concealed.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish an arithmetic error from a technically correct calculation presented in a misleading way."
Key point:
- Correct arithmetic does not guarantee an honest interpretation.
- Assess the method, context, selection, and likely impression—not just the calculation.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
2. Q: Who can distort statistical information, and why should responsibility not be assigned only to statisticians?
A: Show answer
Statistical distortion can occur at every stage between data production and public communication. A statistician may calculate a valid result, but a manager, marketer, advocate, journalist, designer, or spokesperson may select only favorable figures, remove qualifications, change the comparison, or create a graphic that exaggerates the message. Responsibility therefore follows the decisions made by everyone who collects, analyzes, interprets, edits, and communicates the figures. A sound review process should identify who selected the data, who approved the method, and who is accountable for the final claim.
Study instruction:
- "Map the people and decisions involved in a report from data extraction through publication."
Key point:
- Misleading statistics can be created downstream from an accurate analysis.
- Review the full communication chain, not only the analyst’s spreadsheet.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
3. Q: How can incompetence and deliberate manipulation be distinguished?
A: Show answer
Intent is often difficult to establish from a published chart or claim alone. A mistake may result from weak statistical knowledge, an inappropriate method, poor documentation, or an honest misunderstanding. Deliberate manipulation may be suggested by repeated one-sided choices, selective comparisons, concealed assumptions, or corrections that consistently favor the same party. However, an audience should not infer motive solely from an unfavorable result. The defensible approach is to test the data, reproduce the calculation, inspect the methodology, request the missing context, and describe the observable defect without making unsupported allegations about intent.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a disputed statistic by documenting the technical defect separately from any claim about the author’s motive."
Key point:
- A misleading result can be identified without proving deliberate deception.
- Describe evidence and method failures before drawing conclusions about intent.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
4. Q: Why are consistently one-sided errors a warning sign?
A: Show answer
An isolated mistake may be accidental, but a pattern of errors that repeatedly benefits the same organization or argument deserves closer scrutiny. For example, a report may repeatedly choose unusually favorable base years, omit unfavorable categories, use large denominators for one claim and small denominators for another, or emphasize absolute changes only when they support its position. Such a pattern does not by itself prove fraud, but it weakens confidence that the presentation is neutral. Reviewers should compare the full set of choices with reasonable alternatives and disclose whether the conclusion changes under those alternatives.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the direction and effect of methodological choices across several reports from the same source."
Key point:
- Repeated directional bias warrants investigation even when individual calculations appear plausible.
- Look for patterns of selection, not merely isolated mistakes.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
5. Q: Why can a map misrepresent a statistical relationship even when its underlying numbers are accurate?
A: Show answer
A map can emphasize geographic area while the subject being measured is population, income, expenditure, disease incidence, or another quantity that is not proportional to land area. Large, sparsely populated regions may dominate the visual field and create an impression that they dominate the measured phenomenon. A shaded map may also conceal differences in population density, exposure, or the size of the relevant denominator. The map should therefore be judged against the question being asked: if the claim concerns people or money, area alone may be a poor visual proxy.
Study instruction:
- "For a thematic map, identify the measured quantity, geographic unit, denominator, and visual encoding."
Key point:
- A large shaded area does not necessarily represent a large share of people or activity.
- Choose a map design that represents the statistic’s actual denominator.
Source links:
- UK Office for National Statistics: Choropleth Maps
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
6. Q: How should a map comparing government spending with regional income be evaluated?
A: Show answer
First establish whether the comparison is based on total dollars, per-capita amounts, a percentage of regional income, or another measure. Then inspect how regions were selected and shaded. Selecting large states with relatively small populations can make a phenomenon appear geographically extensive even if those states account for a modest share of national income or population. A fair presentation should show the underlying values, explain the selection rule, and test whether the visual conclusion survives alternative groupings. If the map’s purpose is to communicate a national share, a table or chart of comparable shares may be clearer than an area-based map.
Study instruction:
- "Recalculate a map-based claim using population, income, or per-capita denominators appropriate to its stated purpose."
Key point:
- Geographic size and statistical importance are different quantities.
- Require the map’s grouping and denominator to be explicit.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
- UK Office for National Statistics: Choropleth Maps
7. Q: What is the difference between a choropleth map and a proportional-symbol map, and why does the distinction matter?
A: Show answer
A choropleth map shades geographic areas according to a value, usually a rate, proportion, or standardized measure. A proportional-symbol map places symbols whose size represents a quantity, such as total expenditure or population. The distinction matters because shading an entire large region can visually overstate its contribution when the underlying measure is a count or total. Conversely, a rate may be better shown through shading after the denominator is correctly defined. The map type should match the data type; totals, rates, and densities should not be treated as interchangeable.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a map type for a count, rate, and density, and explain the denominator each requires."
Key point:
- The visual encoding must match whether the data are totals, rates, or densities.
- Do not map raw totals as though they were comparable rates.
Source links:
- UK Office for National Statistics: Choropleth Maps
- Esri: Data Visualization Types
8. Q: What is the difference between the mean and the median?
A: Show answer
The mean is calculated by adding all observations and dividing by the number of observations. The median is the middle value after observations are arranged in order, dividing the distribution into two halves. The mean is sensitive to unusually high or low values; the median is less affected by extreme observations. For example, a small number of very high incomes can raise the mean even when most households’ incomes have not changed. Neither measure is universally superior: the appropriate measure depends on the question, the distribution, and whether the reader needs a measure of the arithmetic total per unit or the typical midpoint.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate both measures for a skewed data set and explain why they differ."
Key point:
- Mean and median describe different features of a distribution.
- Always name the measure used and consider the distribution’s shape.
Source links:
- U.S. Census Bureau: Subject Definitions
9. Q: Why can an average income figure give a misleading impression of typical living standards?
A: Show answer
An average may conceal inequality and differences in household composition. A high-income minority can pull the mean upward, making it appear that the typical household has more income than most households actually receive. A median often gives a better indication of the midpoint household, but it still does not describe every household or account for differences in household size, regional prices, taxes, or needs. A responsible report should state whether it uses mean or median, define the population being measured, identify the period, and provide distributional context such as percentiles or income bands where relevant.
Study instruction:
- "Compare mean and median income and identify what each says—and does not say—about the population."
Key point:
- An unqualified “average” does not tell readers which measure was used.
- Name the statistic and provide distributional context.
Source links:
- U.S. Census Bureau: Subject Definitions
10. Q: Why is it invalid to estimate a typical family’s income by multiplying per-person mean income by four?
A: Show answer
Multiplying mean personal income by four creates a hypothetical amount for four people; it does not establish the income of an actual family of four. People’s incomes are not equal, children and adults may have different or no earnings, and household members do not necessarily contribute independently. Family size is not a reliable multiplier for income. The calculation also changes the statistical unit from persons to families without using family-level observations. To estimate family income, use a data set and definition that directly measure family income, and distinguish a per-capita measure from a family-level measure.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the unit of analysis in an income statistic and explain why person-level data cannot automatically be converted into family-level data."
Key point:
- Changing the unit of analysis requires suitable data, not a convenient multiplier.
- Do not infer family income by multiplying per-capita mean income by family size.
Source links:
- U.S. Census Bureau: Subject Definitions
11. Q: When is the mean more useful than the median?
A: Show answer
The mean is useful when the total amount matters or when mathematical analysis requires an arithmetic average. For example, total expenditure divided by the number of transactions gives mean expenditure per transaction, which can reconcile to the aggregate total. The mean is also used in many statistical models and calculations. However, if the distribution is highly skewed, the mean may not describe a typical observation. A sound report can show both mean and median, explain the purpose of each, and include measures of spread so that readers can understand the difference between the arithmetic average and the distribution’s midpoint.
Study instruction:
- "Select the mean or median for a total-cost analysis and for a typical-household description, explaining the different objectives."
Key point:
- The right average depends on the analytical question.
- Use the mean for arithmetic totals and the median when the midpoint is the focus.
Source links:
- U.S. Census Bureau: Subject Definitions
12. Q: What does false precision mean in statistical reporting?
A: Show answer
False precision occurs when a result is reported with more digits than the underlying data, measurement process, or assumptions can support. A survey respondent estimating sleep duration, for example, may not know the answer to the nearest minute. Averaging rough estimates and reporting the result to three decimal places does not make the original observations more accurate. Extra digits can create an unwarranted impression of certainty and authority. Precision should reflect the quality of measurement, the sampling design, rounding rules, and uncertainty—not merely the number of digits a calculator can display.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the measurement process behind a decimal result and decide how many digits are defensible."
Key point:
- More decimal places do not repair uncertain inputs.
- Report precision that the measurement process can justify.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
- NIST: Measurement Variability
- NIST: Standard and Expanded Uncertainties
13. Q: How are precision and accuracy different?
A: Show answer
Precision concerns the level of detail or repeatability in a measurement; accuracy concerns closeness to the true or accepted value. A value can be reported with many decimal places yet be inaccurate because the instrument is poorly calibrated or the underlying estimate is uncertain. Conversely, a rounded value may be accurate enough for the decision being made. Statistical reports should not confuse formatting precision with evidential strength. They should explain measurement limitations and, where relevant, sampling error, confidence intervals, rounding, and data-quality constraints.
Study instruction:
- "Give an example of a highly precise but inaccurate measurement and a rounded but sufficiently accurate report."
Key point:
- Precision is not the same as truth or reliability.
- Assess measurement quality before choosing displayed digits.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
- NIST: Measurement Variability
14. Q: Why can decimal places make an estimate look more authoritative than it is?
A: Show answer
Readers often interpret detailed decimals as evidence of careful measurement. Yet a calculation can preserve meaningless digits from rough assumptions, estimates, or small samples. The result may be mathematically reproducible while its inputs remain uncertain. For example, converting a rough estimate of 783 hours into an average of 7.831 hours per person creates a level of detail that the observations do not support. A report should round appropriately and disclose the method, sample, and uncertainty so that presentation does not imply a degree of knowledge that was never obtained.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the source data’s precision with the precision of the final reported result."
Key point:
- A calculator’s output precision can exceed the evidence’s precision.
- Round and qualify estimates according to their actual reliability.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
- NIST: Measurement Variability
- NIST: Standard and Expanded Uncertainties
15. Q: Why are percentages based on very small counts potentially misleading?
A: Show answer
A percentage can magnify the apparent importance of a small number of observations. If two of 41 cases fall into a category, the percentage is about 4.9%, but readers may overlook that the result represents only two cases. A small change in the count can materially change the percentage, and sampling or classification errors can have a large relative effect. Reports should show the numerator and denominator alongside the percentage, describe the sample and collection method, and avoid excessive decimal places. The percentage should not be interpreted as stable or broadly representative without supporting evidence.
Study instruction:
- "Translate a percentage back into its count and denominator, then test how one additional case changes the result."
Key point:
- Percentages can conceal the small number of observations behind them.
- Report counts and denominators with percentages, especially for small samples.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
- AAPOR: Disclosure Standards
16. Q: What information should accompany a percentage in a statistical report?
A: Show answer
At minimum, readers should be able to identify the numerator, denominator, population or sample, time period, and definition of the event being counted. Where relevant, the report should also describe the sampling method, exclusions, missing observations, weighting, uncertainty, and whether the percentage is rounded. A statement such as “4.9% of offers” is incomplete if readers cannot determine how many offers were examined or what qualified as an offer. Context allows the audience to judge whether the percentage is meaningful, comparable, and sufficiently reliable for the decision at hand.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite an isolated percentage claim so that its base, period, population, and count are visible."
Key point:
- A percentage without its base is an incomplete statistical statement.
- Show the denominator and define the population behind every percentage.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
- AAPOR: Disclosure Standards
17. Q: What is a percentage base, and why must it be stated clearly?
A: Show answer
The base is the reference amount against which a percentage is calculated. A percentage change is generally the difference divided by the starting or reference value, multiplied by 100. The same absolute difference can therefore represent very different percentages depending on the base. A rise of 10 from 20 is 50%, while a rise of 10 from 100 is 10%. Claims about savings, wage changes, profits, and price reductions become misleading when the speaker changes the base without explaining it. Always identify the reference amount and the direction of comparison.
Study instruction:
- "For each percentage claim, write the numerator, denominator, and reference period explicitly."
Key point:
- The base determines the meaning and size of a percentage.
- Never interpret a percentage until its denominator is clear.
Source links:
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Calculating Percent Changes
18. Q: Why does a 50% price reduction require a 100% increase to return to the original price?
A: Show answer
A 50% reduction is calculated from the original price. If an item falls from 100 to 50, the reduction is 50. To return from 50 to 100, the increase is 50 measured against the new base of 50, so the increase is 100%. The absolute change is the same, but the percentage differs because the base changed. This asymmetry applies to prices, wages, budgets, and other values. A report that compares percentage decreases and increases without identifying their bases may create a false impression of recovery or deterioration.
Study instruction:
- "Use a numerical example to calculate both the downward and upward percentage changes from their respective starting values."
Key point:
- Percentage decreases and increases are not symmetrical when the base changes.
- Calculate each percentage from the correct starting value.
Source links:
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Calculating Percent Changes
19. Q: How can a 5% wage increase fail to recover one-quarter of a previous 20% wage cut?
A: Show answer
Assume the original wage is 100. A 20% cut reduces it to 80. A 5% increase on 80 adds only 4, bringing the wage to 84. The original cut was 20, so the increase of 4 restores one-fifth of the cut, not one-quarter. The error comes from comparing the increase with the original wage rather than the reduced wage. When evaluating wage negotiations or price movements, calculate each change sequentially using the amount that actually applied immediately before that change.
Study instruction:
- "Reconstruct a sequence of wage changes using an initial value of 100 and calculate the remaining gap from the original level."
Key point:
- A percentage increase is applied to the post-reduction base.
- Use sequential calculations rather than adding percentage labels.
Source links:
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Calculating Percent Changes
20. Q: Why is a claim that something became “100% cheaper” usually mathematically impossible in ordinary pricing?
A: Show answer
A 100% reduction from a positive starting price means the entire price has been removed, leaving zero. A reduction greater than 100% would produce a negative price under the ordinary formula, meaning the seller would pay the buyer, which is not what most price claims intend. Speakers sometimes use “100% cheaper” to mean that the current price is half the former price or that a saving equals the new price, but those are different statements. The correct report should state the original and current prices and calculate the percentage change transparently.
Study instruction:
- "Test a “100% cheaper” claim by calculating the implied current price from the stated original price."
Key point:
- A 100% reduction means a zero price, not a half-price discount.
- Replace ambiguous advertising language with the actual prices and calculation.
Source links:
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Calculating Percent Changes
21. Q: How should successive discounts such as “50% and 20% off” be calculated?
A: Show answer
Successive discounts are applied to the remaining price, not both to the original price. If the list price is 100, a 50% discount reduces it to 50. A further 20% discount on 50 removes 10, leaving a final price of 40. The total reduction is therefore 60%, not 70%. Adding the two discount rates ignores the shifting base. The same principle applies to sequential price changes, fees, tax adjustments, and compound percentage movements.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate a two-stage discount using a list price of 100 and compare it with the incorrect sum of the rates."
Key point:
- Successive percentages apply to different bases.
- Calculate each stage in sequence and state the final effective discount.
Source links:
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Calculating Percent Changes
22. Q: What is the difference between a percentage and a percentage-point change?
A: Show answer
A percentage change compares a difference with a reference value; a percentage-point change is the arithmetic difference between two percentages. If a rate rises from 3% to 6%, it increases by 3 percentage points but doubles, which is a 100% relative increase. Saying only “up 3%” is ambiguous because it may mean three percentage points or a 3% relative change. Reports should use the correct term and, when the distinction matters, provide both the starting and ending rates.
Study instruction:
- "Describe a movement from 3% to 6% using percentage points and relative percentage change."
Key point:
- Percentage points measure absolute differences between rates; percentages measure relative change.
- State both measures when a rate change could be misunderstood.
Source links:
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Calculating Percent Changes
23. Q: How should percentage profit be calculated, and why must the denominator be disclosed?
A: Show answer
A profit margin commonly expresses profit as a share of sales revenue, while markup expresses profit as a share of cost. For cost of 1.75 and selling price of 40, the gross difference is 38.25. Dividing by cost produces a very different percentage from dividing by selling price. Neither calculation should be labeled simply “profit percentage” without defining the denominator and the type of profit being measured. The analyst must also distinguish gross profit from net profit and identify which costs are included.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate profit as a percentage of cost and as a percentage of selling price for the same transaction."
Key point:
- Margin and markup use different denominators.
- Name the profit measure and disclose its calculation base.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
24. Q: Why should an analyst avoid adding percentage increases across unrelated cost categories?
A: Show answer
Percentage changes can be added only under specific conditions. If several cost categories have different starting weights, their individual percentage increases do not contribute equally to the total. A 10% rise in a category representing 5% of total cost has a much smaller effect on total cost than a 10% rise in a category representing 70%. The aggregate percentage change must be calculated from the combined monetary totals or from appropriately weighted changes. Simply adding category percentages can greatly exaggerate the overall increase.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate the total cost before and after category-specific increases, then compare the aggregate rate with the sum of the individual rates."
Key point:
- Aggregate change depends on each category’s weight in the total.
- Use weighted calculations or recompute the total rather than adding rates blindly.
Source links:
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Calculating Percent Changes
25. Q: How do weights affect an overall percentage change?
A: Show answer
A weight represents the relative contribution of a component to the total being measured. If a component accounts for a large share of expenditure, its price or cost movement has more influence on the aggregate than the same percentage movement in a small component. A weighted aggregate therefore reflects both the rate of change and the component’s initial importance. Weights should be defined for the relevant population and period, and the analyst should explain whether they are fixed or updated. A simple unweighted average may be appropriate only when equal weighting is justified by the analytical objective.
Study instruction:
- "Build a two-category example with unequal starting costs and calculate the total percentage change."
Key point:
- The effect of a component’s rate depends on its share of the whole.
- Use defensible weights that match the population and period being analyzed.
Source links:
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Index Numbers
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Calculating Percent Changes
26. Q: Why is adding unrelated quantities a form of statistical misrepresentation?
A: Show answer
A sum is meaningful only when its components represent compatible quantities and do not overlap. Adding hypothetical lost production, supplier losses, transport fares, retail sales, and other estimated effects may count the same economic activity several times or combine quantities that cannot be aggregated. The resulting total may sound precise but lack a coherent definition. Before adding components, identify what each measures, whether the items overlap, whether they are actual or hypothetical, and whether they share the same period, currency, and unit. A defensible total must have a clear conceptual boundary.
Study instruction:
- "Audit a composite loss estimate and identify overlapping effects, incompatible units, and hypothetical amounts."
Key point:
- A total is meaningful only when its components are compatible and non-duplicative.
- Define the total and test every component before summing.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
27. Q: How can estimates of the economic cost of a strike become exaggerated?
A: Show answer
A strike-cost estimate may add the output that would have been produced, suppliers’ hypothetical sales, transport fares, retail transactions, and other downstream effects as though each were an independent loss. Some components may overlap, some may be postponed rather than permanently lost, and others may represent gross sales rather than net economic value. A credible analysis should define the counterfactual, identify direct and indirect effects, avoid double counting, distinguish gross activity from value added, and disclose assumptions. It should also present a range or sensitivity analysis when the estimate depends on uncertain behavior.
Study instruction:
- "Review a strike-impact calculation and separate actual direct losses, indirect effects, delayed activity, and duplicated estimates."
Key point:
- Large totals may reflect repeated counting rather than larger real losses.
- Use a transparent counterfactual and eliminate double counting.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
28. Q: Why is an unweighted average of different wage rates often meaningless?
A: Show answer
A simple average of a regular hourly rate, overtime rate, and double-time rate gives each rate equal influence even if employees worked very different numbers of hours at each rate. The result may not describe the average wage actually paid per hour. A meaningful average requires a defined weighting basis, such as total wages divided by total hours. If the objective is to compare contractual rates, show the rates separately. If the objective is realized average compensation, calculate from the relevant wage and hour totals and explain which employees and periods are included.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the simple average of three pay rates with total pay divided by total hours worked."
Key point:
- An average must reflect the units and weights relevant to the question.
- Use hours or another justified weight when calculating realized average rates.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
29. Q: What is double counting, and how can it distort an aggregate statistic?
A: Show answer
Double counting occurs when the same underlying economic event or value is included more than once in a total. It can arise when direct output losses are added to supplier losses that already incorporate the same production shortfall, or when gross sales and their component transactions are both counted. Double counting inflates the total without adding new information. Analysts should trace each component to its source, identify overlaps, use mutually exclusive categories where possible, and reconcile the components to an independently defined total.
Study instruction:
- "Trace each component of an aggregate estimate to determine whether it represents a distinct event or repeats another item."
Key point:
- A large sum is not credible if components overlap.
- Use mutually exclusive categories and a documented reconciliation.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
30. Q: How can a chart show the same data while encouraging different conclusions?
A: Show answer
The conclusion readers draw depends on the question, scale, unit, and comparison used. Absolute amounts emphasize the size of monetary differences; percentage changes emphasize relative growth from different starting levels. A chart showing wages and profits in currency units may make wages appear dominant because the wage total is larger. A chart showing percentage growth may make profits appear more dynamic because they started from a smaller base. Both views can be mathematically valid, but neither alone establishes which factor caused an outcome. A balanced report should show the relevant measures and explain what each can and cannot establish.
Study instruction:
- "Recreate a comparison in both absolute amounts and percentage changes and explain why the visual story differs."
Key point:
- Absolute change and relative change answer different questions.
- Show the scale and comparison that match the claim being made.
Source links:
- UK Office for National Statistics: Axes and Gridlines
- UK Office for National Statistics: Chart Text
31. Q: What are the risks of using dual-axis charts?
A: Show answer
A dual-axis chart places two series on separate vertical scales, allowing their lines to appear to move together or diverge depending on the chosen ranges. Because the scales may use different units and ranges, visual alignment can be created without a meaningful relationship. Readers may infer correlation, similar magnitude, or causation that the data do not establish. The UK Office for National Statistics advises avoiding dual-axis charts because they are often misleading and difficult to interpret. Separate charts, a common scale where appropriate, or clearly labeled annotations are generally safer.
Study instruction:
- "Inspect a dual-axis chart and test whether changing either axis range changes the apparent relationship."
Key point:
- Independent axes can manufacture visual similarity or difference.
- Prefer separate, clearly labeled charts over ambiguous dual-axis displays.
Source links:
- UK Office for National Statistics: Axes and Gridlines
32. Q: How can choosing absolute values instead of percentage changes bias a comparison?
A: Show answer
Absolute values emphasize the size of a change in the units measured, while percentage changes show that change relative to the starting level. A large organization may record the largest dollar increase simply because its starting base is much larger. A smaller organization may experience a much higher percentage increase from a low base. Neither measure is inherently deceptive, but selecting only one can favor a particular interpretation. Reports should choose the measure that matches the question and, where both scale and growth matter, display both with clear explanations.
Study instruction:
- "Compare two organizations with different starting values using both absolute and relative changes."
Key point:
- Absolute and relative comparisons can produce different rankings.
- Use the measure that answers the stated question and disclose the alternative view.
Source links:
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Calculating Percent Changes
33. Q: How should a reader interpret a chart that uses the same data to tell two apparently opposite stories?
A: Show answer
The reader should first identify the quantities being compared, the units, the denominators, the time period, and the scales. Then ask what each chart emphasizes and whether the conclusion follows from the data or merely from the chosen presentation. A rise in total dollars may be important even when the percentage rise is modest; a large percentage rise may be less consequential if the starting amount is small. A chart cannot by itself establish causation or allocate responsibility. The correct response is not to select the preferred graphic but to examine the full evidence and state the limits of inference.
Study instruction:
- "Write two neutral descriptions of a data series—one in absolute terms and one in relative terms—without claiming either proves causation."
Key point:
- A valid chart can still be incomplete as evidence for a causal claim.
- Separate what the data show from what the presenter wants readers to infer.
Source links:
- UK Office for National Statistics: Axes and Gridlines
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
34. Q: What is an index number, and what does a base period of 100 mean?
A: Show answer
An index number expresses the level of a series relative to a chosen reference period, which is conventionally set to 100. An index of 110 indicates a level 10% above the reference level; an index of 90 indicates a level 10% below it. The index is not a currency amount or a direct count. It is a standardized measure that makes movements over time easier to describe. Readers need the base period, population, basket or series definition, and methodology to interpret the index correctly.
Study instruction:
- "Explain what index values of 90, 100, and 110 mean when the reference period is 100."
Key point:
- An index expresses relative movement from a reference period.
- Always identify the index base and what the series measures.
Source links:
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Index Numbers
35. Q: Why can changing the base period change the impression created by an index?
A: Show answer
Rebasing changes the reference point and the displayed index levels, even though the underlying observations may be unchanged. A value of 150 on a base of 100 means the series is 50% above that base, but it does not reveal the path taken between periods. A different base can make the same movement look larger or smaller in index points. Percentage changes between two observations remain interpretable when calculated consistently, but comparisons require the same series definition and compatible base. Reports should disclose rebasing and avoid presenting index-point differences as if they were directly comparable across different bases.
Study instruction:
- "Rebase a short series to a different reference period and distinguish changed index levels from unchanged underlying observations."
Key point:
- The index scale depends on its reference period.
- Disclose rebasing and use consistent bases for comparisons.
Source links:
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Index Numbers
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Calculating Percent Changes
36. Q: How can different averaging methods produce different conclusions about price movements?
A: Show answer
The arithmetic mean and geometric mean summarize values differently. The arithmetic mean adds observations and divides by their number; the geometric mean multiplies positive values and takes the appropriate root. The geometric mean is often useful for multiplicative changes and relative movements, while the arithmetic mean is appropriate for additive quantities. In the chapter’s milk-and-bread illustration, one price halves while another doubles. The arithmetic mean of the relative price indexes is 125, whereas the geometric mean is 100. This does not mean either method can be selected arbitrarily; the appropriate measure depends on the economic question and index construction.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate arithmetic and geometric means for two relative price indexes of 50 and 200, then explain what each summarizes."
Key point:
- Different averaging methods answer different questions.
- Choose the averaging method from the structure of the data and purpose of the index.
Source links:
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Index Numbers
37. Q: What does the milk-and-bread example reveal about the choice of index formula?
A: Show answer
If milk falls from 20 to 10 and bread rises from 5 to 10, the relative prices are 50 and 200 when the earlier period is set to 100. Their arithmetic average is 125, suggesting a 25% rise under equal weighting. The geometric average is the square root of 50 multiplied by 200, which equals 100, indicating no change under that particular formula and equal-item treatment. The example demonstrates that index results depend on the formula and weights, not that any result can be chosen without justification. Real cost-of-living measures require representative baskets and defensible expenditure weights.
Study instruction:
- "Reproduce the two index calculations and list the assumptions—especially equal weighting—that make the results possible."
Key point:
- An index formula and its weights materially affect the reported movement.
- Disclose the formula, weights, and interpretation of an index.
Source links:
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Index Numbers
38. Q: Why is it misleading to choose an index formula solely because it produces a preferred conclusion?
A: Show answer
An index formula should be selected because it fits the objective, economic theory, data structure, and established methodology—not because it yields a favorable result. Choosing an arithmetic or geometric average after seeing which one supports a preferred claim creates outcome-driven analysis. A defensible analyst specifies the method in advance where practicable, documents the rationale, tests sensitivity to reasonable alternatives, and explains how conclusions change. If more than one method is defensible, the report should present the alternatives rather than conceal the one that produces an inconvenient result.
Study instruction:
- "Compare plausible index methods and document the methodological reason for choosing one before interpreting the result."
Key point:
- Method selection is part of the analysis and must not be driven by the desired answer.
- Predefine and disclose the index formula and sensitivity checks.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Index Numbers
39. Q: What is a percentile, and how should percentile rank be interpreted?
A: Show answer
A percentile rank indicates the relative position of an observation within a defined distribution. A person at the 90th percentile is at or above approximately 90% of the reference group under the stated convention; the exact interpretation depends on the percentile method and ties. It is not the same as scoring 90% of possible marks. Percentiles describe relative standing, not the size of the underlying difference. The comparison group, assessment, date, and calculation method should therefore be stated.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the difference between a 90th percentile rank and a score of 90 out of 100."
Key point:
- Percentile rank is relative position, not percentage correct.
- Identify the reference population before interpreting a percentile.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
- NIST: Percentiles
40. Q: Why are percentile differences not necessarily equal intervals of achievement?
A: Show answer
Percentile ranks describe positions in a distribution, not equal units of performance. In a distribution clustered around the middle, a small difference in raw score may move a person across several percentile ranks, while a much larger raw-score difference near an extreme may correspond to a smaller percentile movement. The chapter illustrates that a student at the 99th percentile may be substantially ahead of one at the 90th, while students at the 40th and 60th percentiles may have similar underlying achievement. The distribution and assessment scale must be examined before interpreting gaps.
Study instruction:
- "Use a hypothetical clustered score distribution to show why percentile intervals do not equal score intervals."
Key point:
- A ten-percentile-point gap does not represent a fixed amount of ability.
- Interpret percentiles with the score distribution and assessment context.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
- NIST: Percentiles
- NIST: Measures of Location
41. Q: How does a normal distribution affect the interpretation of percentiles?
A: Show answer
In a normal or bell-shaped distribution, observations cluster around the mean and become less frequent toward the extremes. Consequently, percentile positions near the center may correspond to relatively small raw-score differences, while positions in the tails can behave differently. This helps explain why percentile gaps should not be treated as equal units of achievement. Not every real-world variable follows a normal distribution, however, and a percentile report should not assume normality without evidence. Analysts should inspect the actual distribution and explain how the percentile was derived.
Study instruction:
- "Plot or inspect a score distribution and compare raw-score gaps around the center and tails."
Key point:
- Percentile spacing depends on the shape of the underlying distribution.
- Do not assume a bell curve unless the data support it.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
- NIST: Percentiles
- NIST: Measures of Location
42. Q: Why should a comparison use a defensible base year or reference period?
A: Show answer
A comparison can be distorted when the selected base year is unusually weak, unusually strong, or otherwise unrepresentative. Comparing current productivity with a year of exceptionally low output may exaggerate growth; comparing wages in total dollars when earlier workers were employed part-time may confuse earnings with hourly rates. A defensible comparison should explain why the base period is appropriate, examine structural changes, use consistent definitions, and test alternative periods. The objective is to compare like with like rather than select the year that makes the trend look most favorable.
Study instruction:
- "Recalculate a trend using an unusual base year and a more representative comparison period."
Key point:
- The base period can shape the apparent size and direction of a trend.
- Justify the comparison period and test whether the conclusion is robust.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Index Numbers
43. Q: How can changes in workforce composition distort comparisons of total earnings?
A: Show answer
Total earnings can rise because hourly wage rates increase, because employees work more hours, because more people are employed, or because the workforce composition changes. A comparison of aggregate payroll across two years does not isolate the change in pay rates. If many workers were part-time in the earlier year, comparing total earnings with a later full-time workforce may exaggerate the change in hourly compensation. Analysts should distinguish total earnings, average hourly rates, hours worked, employment levels, and workforce composition, using consistent populations and definitions.
Study instruction:
- "Separate the effect of wage rates, hours, headcount, and employee mix in a payroll trend analysis."
Key point:
- Aggregate earnings do not reveal the change in an individual’s pay rate.
- Choose a measure that controls for workforce and hours differences.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
44. Q: What is cherry-picking, and how does it distort statistical comparisons?
A: Show answer
Cherry-picking is the selective inclusion of observations, periods, groups, outcomes, or methods that support a preferred conclusion while excluding relevant contrary evidence. It can occur through a favorable starting year, a restricted geographic area, selected survey responses, or a single outcome among many measured. A selected comparison may be factually accurate but unrepresentative of the complete evidence. Good practice requires a transparent selection rule, disclosure of exclusions, and a clear account of alternative findings that could change the conclusion.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a selected time window with the full available series and explain whether the selection rule was defined before analysis."
Key point:
- Selective evidence can produce a persuasive but incomplete story.
- Disclose selection criteria and relevant results that point in other directions.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
45. Q: How should conflicting statistical claims from opposing parties be evaluated?
A: Show answer
When opposing parties present competing statistics, do not assume that the midpoint between their claims is correct or that one side must be wholly dishonest. Identify each party’s data source, definitions, time period, denominator, base year, inclusion criteria, and calculation method. Reproduce both calculations where possible and determine whether they answer the same question. If one party uses total income and another uses hourly earnings, the results may differ without directly contradicting each other. Explain which comparison is appropriate for the decision and what remains uncertain.
Study instruction:
- "Create a comparison matrix showing the source, measure, base, population, period, and method behind each claim."
Key point:
- Conflicting figures may arise from different definitions as well as bias.
- Reconcile methods before choosing which claim is more relevant.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
46. Q: What is the difference between a valid statistical method and a fair statistical presentation?
A: Show answer
A method may be mathematically valid but still be presented unfairly if the author hides assumptions, omits context, selects only favorable results, or uses a graphic that encourages an unsupported interpretation. Conversely, a clear presentation cannot rescue an invalid method. Statistical integrity requires both sound analysis and honest communication. The American Statistical Association’s guidance emphasizes appropriate methods, transparency about assumptions and limitations, disclosure of data sources and transformations, and resistance to selective interpretation. Reviewers should therefore assess technical validity and communication fairness separately.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a report twice: first for calculation validity, then for completeness and neutrality of presentation."
Key point:
- Technical validity and communicative fairness are separate requirements.
- Audit both the method and the impression created for the audience.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
47. Q: How should uncertainty and limitations be disclosed when reporting statistical findings?
A: Show answer
A report should identify limitations that could materially affect interpretation, including sampling error, nonresponse, missing data, measurement error, small sample size, changing definitions, and model assumptions. Where appropriate, provide confidence intervals, standard errors, ranges, or sensitivity analysis rather than presenting a single estimate as exact. Explain whether the data are descriptive, estimated, or modeled and avoid implying certainty that the design cannot support. Limitations should be specific enough to help readers judge whether the result is fit for the decision being considered.
Study instruction:
- "Review a statistical claim and list the uncertainty sources that could change its interpretation."
Key point:
- A point estimate alone may conceal substantial uncertainty.
- Disclose material limitations and quantify uncertainty where possible.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
48. Q: What controls can organizations use to prevent statisticulation in reports and dashboards?
A: Show answer
Useful controls include documented metric definitions, approved data sources, reproducible calculations, version-controlled code or workbooks, independent review, denominator checks, reconciliation to source totals, chart-scale review, and sign-off by an accountable owner. Reports should retain assumptions, exclusions, transformations, and changes to methodology. High-impact claims may require a second analyst to reproduce the result and challenge the interpretation. A control should test both the number and the narrative: does the visual or headline imply more than the evidence supports?
Study instruction:
- "Design a review checklist for a management dashboard covering source, formula, denominator, scale, exclusions, and approval."
Key point:
- Reliable reporting requires repeatable analysis and independent challenge.
- Build controls around data lineage, calculation, interpretation, and publication.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
- UK Office for National Statistics: Axes and Gridlines
49. Q: How can an independent reviewer detect a misleading percentage claim?
A: Show answer
The reviewer should reconstruct the calculation from the raw numerator and denominator, confirm the population and period, and identify the comparison base. Then test whether the percentage is based on a small count, whether the denominator changed, whether the figure is weighted, and whether rounding materially affects the result. For sequential changes, calculate each stage using its actual starting value. For aggregate rates, recompute from totals rather than adding component percentages. Finally, compare the headline with the underlying table to determine whether it accurately describes the result.
Study instruction:
- "Take a percentage statement from a report and reproduce it independently from source counts."
Key point:
- Independent recalculation exposes hidden denominators and shifting bases.
- Rebuild the calculation rather than accepting the reported percentage.
Source links:
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Calculating Percent Changes
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
50. Q: How should a statistical claim be corrected after a material error is discovered?
A: Show answer
The organization should establish the affected data, calculation, report versions, and decisions that relied on the claim. Correct the error promptly, explain what changed and why, and communicate the correction to audiences who received the original result. If the error affects a decision, assess the consequences and determine whether the decision must be reconsidered. Preserve an audit trail of the original figure, corrected figure, reason, reviewer, and publication date. The correction should be proportionate and transparent rather than silently replacing the number.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a correction note that identifies the original claim, revised result, cause, impact, and recipients."
Key point:
- A correction is part of responsible statistical practice, not an optional courtesy.
- Correct visibly, assess downstream impact, and retain the audit trail.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
51. Q: What questions should a reader ask before accepting a statistical claim in advertising or public relations?
A: Show answer
Ask who produced the statistic, what decision or message it is intended to support, what population and period it covers, how the numerator and denominator are defined, and whether the source data are available. Check whether the comparison uses a fair base, whether percentages are based on enough observations, whether the claim confuses margin with markup or percent with percentage points, and whether the graphic uses an appropriate scale. Also ask what relevant information has been omitted and whether an independent source reaches a compatible conclusion.
Study instruction:
- "Apply a structured source-and-method check to a promotional claim that uses a percentage or chart."
Key point:
- Persuasive wording is not a substitute for transparent evidence.
- Interrogate the source, denominator, base, selection, and visual design.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
- UK Office for National Statistics: Chart Text
52. Q: How can a manager explain statistical uncertainty without making a report unusable?
A: Show answer
A manager can state the central estimate, explain the most important uncertainty in plain language, and identify what decisions the estimate can reasonably support. For example, a report may say that an estimated rate is approximately 12%, while noting that the sample is small and the plausible range is wider than usual. The goal is not to overwhelm readers with technical detail but to prevent false certainty. Put essential qualifications near the headline, provide technical detail in the methodology notes, and distinguish what is known from what remains uncertain.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a precise-sounding estimate as a clear management statement that includes its key limitation."
Key point:
- Clarity and qualification can coexist.
- Put the material caveat beside the claim it qualifies.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
- UK Office for National Statistics: Chart Text
53. Q: What is the relationship between statistical presentation and ethical responsibility?
A: Show answer
People who present statistics influence decisions, resource allocation, public understanding, and the treatment of affected groups. Ethical responsibility therefore extends beyond avoiding fabricated numbers. It includes using valid methods, explaining data sources and transformations, disclosing limitations and conflicts, resisting pressure to report only favorable results, and correcting substantive errors. These obligations apply to anyone engaged in statistical practice, regardless of job title. A technically correct number can still cause harm when its framing encourages an unfair or unsupported decision.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the ethical responsibilities of the analyst, reviewer, manager, and communicator in a published report."
Key point:
- Statistical ethics includes the way results are selected and communicated.
- Treat transparency, fairness, and correction as core reporting controls.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
54. Q: How can statistical methods be both a science and an art without becoming arbitrary?
A: Show answer
Statistics is a science because it uses formal concepts, mathematical methods, and systematic procedures. It also involves judgment in defining the question, selecting the population, choosing measures, setting assumptions, handling missing data, and presenting results. Judgment does not mean that any method is acceptable. Choices must be justified by the purpose, supported by the data, documented, and open to scrutiny. When multiple reasonable approaches exist, analysts should explain why one was selected and show whether alternative methods materially affect the conclusion.
Study instruction:
- "Document the judgment calls in a small analysis and justify each choice using the question and data characteristics."
Key point:
- Statistical judgment is necessary but must be disciplined and transparent.
- Make methodological choices explainable, reproducible, and reviewable.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
55. Q: How can a report distinguish descriptive statistics from causal conclusions?
A: Show answer
Descriptive statistics summarize what was observed, such as a change in wages, profits, prices, or output. A causal conclusion asserts that one factor produced a change in another. The fact that two series move together, or that one rises faster in percentage terms, does not establish causation. Causal analysis requires an appropriate design and consideration of confounding factors, timing, alternative explanations, and uncertainty. A report should use language that matches the evidence: “increased alongside” is not equivalent to “caused.”
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a causal headline as a descriptive statement and identify what additional evidence would be needed to support causation."
Key point:
- A chart of trends alone does not establish cause and effect.
- Match the strength of the conclusion to the strength of the research design.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
56. Q: What is a practical framework for checking whether a statistical presentation is misleading?
A: Show answer
Use a structured sequence: identify the claim; define the population and period; inspect the source and data lineage; reproduce the calculation; verify numerator, denominator, and base; assess sample size and uncertainty; check weighting and aggregation; inspect the chart scale and geographic or category selection; compare alternative reasonable methods; and determine whether the narrative overstates what the data establish. Record each issue and its materiality. This framework catches both numerical defects and presentation choices that can create a false impression.
Study instruction:
- "Apply the framework to one chart and one percentage claim, recording evidence for each check."
Key point:
- A systematic review is more reliable than relying on intuition about a graphic.
- Test data, calculation, comparison, visual design, and interpretation in sequence.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
- UK Office for National Statistics: Axes and Gridlines
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Calculating Percent Changes
57. Q: How can an analyst preserve neutrality when a client or manager wants a particular statistical conclusion?
A: Show answer
The analyst should clarify the decision question, agree on defensible measures before reviewing the outcome where possible, document assumptions, and explain which conclusions the evidence supports. If a requested method is inappropriate, explain why and propose a valid alternative. If several methods are defensible, disclose them and show the sensitivity of the result. Analysts should not suppress unfavorable findings or selectively interpret data to satisfy a sponsor. Organizational governance should protect practitioners who raise methodological or ethical concerns.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a response to a manager who asks you to select only the comparison period that produces the strongest improvement."
Key point:
- Professional independence requires resisting pressure to predetermine results.
- Use documented criteria and disclose valid alternatives, even when they are inconvenient.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
58. Q: What should be checked before using an index to compare prices or living costs?
A: Show answer
Confirm the index population, basket or set of items, expenditure weights, reference period, collection method, and treatment of quality changes or missing prices. Check whether the index is intended to measure prices for a particular group or a broad population, and whether the comparison uses a consistent series and base. A price index is not a direct measure of every household’s personal experience because spending patterns differ. Explain the index’s scope and avoid interpreting its movement as a universal change for all consumers.
Study instruction:
- "Review an index methodology and identify the population, basket, weights, base, and limitations."
Key point:
- An index summarizes a defined basket or series, not every individual experience.
- Check the scope and weighting before applying an index to a population.
Source links:
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Index Numbers
59. Q: Why should readers be cautious when a report gives only a single “average” without describing the distribution?
A: Show answer
A single average hides variation among observations. Two groups can have the same mean but very different distributions: one may be tightly clustered while another includes many low values and a few extreme highs. The median, percentiles, range, standard deviation, or distribution chart may reveal important differences that the mean conceals. The appropriate additional measures depend on the question and data quality. A report should not overload readers with every statistic, but it should include enough distributional information to prevent a misleading impression of uniformity.
Study instruction:
- "Construct two data sets with the same mean but different distributions and explain why their typical observations differ."
Key point:
- The same average can describe very different populations.
- Show distributional context when variation matters to the decision.
Source links:
- U.S. Census Bureau: Subject Definitions
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
60. Q: How should a statistical chart label axes, units, and context?
A: Show answer
Axes should clearly identify the quantity and unit being displayed, including currency, counts, rates, or percentages. The scale should be consistent across comparable charts, and any non-linear transformation should be explained. Titles and annotations should identify the relevant population, period, and comparison without duplicating or obscuring essential information. A truncated axis may be appropriate for some purposes but can exaggerate visual differences if not clearly signaled. The reader should be able to understand what is measured and how large a visual change is in the underlying units.
Study instruction:
- "Audit a chart for axis labels, units, scale, time period, and explanatory annotations."
Key point:
- Chart design is part of the statistical argument.
- Make units, scale, and context visible at the point of interpretation.
Source links:
- UK Office for National Statistics: Axes and Gridlines
- UK Office for National Statistics: Chart Text
61. Q: What is the central lesson of the chapter “How to Statisticulate”?
A: Show answer
The chapter shows that statistical information can mislead through selective maps, inappropriate averages, false precision, small-sample percentages, shifting bases, invalid addition, misleading comparisons, percentile misunderstandings, and flexible index formulas. Many distortions do not require inventing data; they arise from choices about what to measure, how to calculate it, and how to present it. The reader should neither accept every statistic uncritically nor reject statistical methods indiscriminately. The sound response is disciplined skepticism: inspect definitions, calculations, assumptions, denominators, comparisons, and visual presentation before accepting the conclusion.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize one example from each major technique and explain the specific reasoning error involved."
Key point:
- A statistic must be examined in context before it can be trusted.
- Be skeptical of presentation choices while remaining open to sound evidence.
Source links:
- American Statistical Association: Ethical Guidelines for Statistical Practice
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Calculating Percent Changes
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Index Numbers
- UK Office for National Statistics: Axes and Gridlines
- UK Office for National Statistics: Choropleth Maps
- NIST: Percentiles
- AAPOR: Disclosure Standards
