Table 10
Critical Evaluation of Statistical Claims and Data
1. Q: How can you recognize a phony statistic and distinguish it from sound, usable data?
A: Show answer
Evaluate a statistical claim by asking five connected questions: who produced or sponsored it; how the information was obtained; what relevant information is missing; whether the reported evidence and the conclusion refer to the same thing; and whether the result makes sense in context. These questions test provenance, method, completeness, interpretation, and plausibility. A statistic is not reliable merely because it is expressed numerically, attributed to a prestigious institution, or presented in a precise chart. Equally, an inconvenient result should not be rejected simply because it conflicts with expectations. Look for transparent definitions, a suitable population and sample, disclosed methods, relevant denominators and comparison periods, appropriate measures, and conclusions proportionate to the evidence. A sound statistic is fit for a defined purpose and its limitations are visible. The chapter's central lesson is to investigate the process behind a number before accepting the story attached to it.
Study instruction:
- Apply the five questions to a recent chart, survey, business claim, or public statistic and write down what evidence would increase or reduce your confidence.
Key point:
- A number is only as trustworthy as its source, method, context, and interpretation.
- Test the claim and the evidence separately before accepting the conclusion.
Source links:
- AAPOR: Best Practices for Survey Research
- Office for National Statistics: Quality Defined
2. Q: Why should the identity and interests of the person or organization presenting a statistic be examined first?
A: Show answer
The producer or sponsor may have an incentive to emphasize a favorable result, defend a policy, sell a product, protect a reputation, influence negotiations, or attract public attention. Such incentives do not prove that the statistic is false, but they create a reason to examine the method and presentation carefully. A company reporting customer satisfaction, a campaign reporting voter support, and an industry association reporting economic effects may each possess useful information while also having interests in the outcome. Identify who funded the work, who designed it, who collected the data, who analyzed it, and who approved the published conclusion. These roles may belong to different parties. Also distinguish the data provider from the person interpreting the data: an institution may have supplied observations without endorsing the conclusions later drawn from them. A transparent source explains its role, funding, methods, and limitations rather than relying on its reputation alone.
Study instruction:
- For a published statistic, identify the sponsor, researcher, data collector, analyst, and publisher; note any interests that could influence design or interpretation.
Key point:
- Interested sources can provide useful evidence, but their incentives deserve scrutiny.
- Do not transfer an institution's reputation to a conclusion it has not endorsed.
Source links:
- AAPOR: Best Practices for Survey Research
- Office for National Statistics: Quality Defined
3. Q: What is the difference between conscious bias and unconscious bias in statistical reporting?
A: Show answer
Conscious bias involves deliberate choices intended to influence the impression created by the data. Examples include omitting unfavorable observations, selecting a convenient comparison year, changing units, reporting only a favorable subgroup, or describing a measure ambiguously as an average. Unconscious bias arises when researchers or analysts unintentionally favor assumptions, expectations, familiar explanations, or methods that confirm what they already believe. It can influence which questions are asked, which variables are measured, how unusual observations are treated, and how results are explained. Both forms can distort conclusions, although evidence of a biased result does not by itself establish deliberate misconduct. A sound review therefore examines the design and decisions rather than speculating about motives. Documented methods, preregistered analysis plans where appropriate, independent review, reproducible calculations, and disclosure of limitations reduce the opportunity for either form of bias to go unnoticed.
Study instruction:
- Classify examples of selective reporting, confirmation bias, and ambiguous measurement, explaining what evidence would be needed to establish the source of the problem.
Key point:
- Bias can be deliberate or inadvertent and may enter at many stages of analysis.
- Assess observable methods and decisions rather than assuming intent.
Source links:
- AAPOR: Best Practices for Survey Research
- Office for National Statistics: Uncertainty and How We Measure It
4. Q: How can an authoritative or prestigious name be used to make a weak statistical claim appear credible?
A: Show answer
A report may mention a respected university, laboratory, ministry, professional body, or research institution even though that organization did not design the analysis, approve the conclusion, or endorse the interpretation. The institution may merely have supplied a dataset, hosted a survey, employed an author, or been mentioned in a footnote. Readers can mistakenly remember the institution as the speaker and treat the conclusion as an official finding. Verify the original report, authorship, institutional affiliation at the time, funding, publication status, and any disclaimer about responsibility for interpretation. Check whether the institution itself issued the statement or whether an individual author drew a conclusion independently. An institutional name is relevant provenance information, not a substitute for methodological evidence. Reputable institutions also publish work with uncertainty, limitations, and results that should not be generalized beyond the stated scope.
Study instruction:
- Trace one claim attributed to a university or public agency back to the original publication and determine precisely what the institution endorsed.
Key point:
- Distinguish the source of data from the author of the interpretation.
- Reputation cannot repair an unsuitable sample or unsupported conclusion.
Source links:
- Office for National Statistics: Quality Defined
- AAPOR: Best Practices for Survey Research
5. Q: What should a reader ask when a report says that “a survey shows” or “research proves” a conclusion?
A: Show answer
Ask who commissioned the survey, who designed and administered it, the population it intended to represent, how respondents were selected, how many were contacted, how many responded, when data were collected, what questions were asked, and how responses were analyzed. The phrase “a survey shows” can conceal a weak response process, leading wording, incomplete coverage, or a selective interpretation. “Research proves” is often too strong because statistical studies usually provide evidence with assumptions and uncertainty rather than absolute proof. Obtain the original report or technical documentation instead of relying only on a headline or press summary. Compare the conclusion with the actual questions and reported results. If the method is not disclosed, state that the claim cannot be independently evaluated; do not fill the gaps with assumptions.
Study instruction:
- Build a short evidence checklist for a survey claim and mark each item as disclosed, unclear, or unavailable.
Key point:
- General phrases about surveys and research are not methodological disclosures.
- The original study and its methods matter more than the headline.
Source links:
- AAPOR: Best Practices for Survey Research
6. Q: How can a self-selected or voluntary-response sample distort a survey result?
A: Show answer
In a voluntary-response survey, people decide for themselves whether to participate. Respondents may be unusually interested, angry, satisfied, dissatisfied, available, or motivated by the topic. Their views can therefore differ systematically from those of people who do not respond. A large number of voluntary responses does not automatically make the sample representative: increasing the number of people with the same selection bias can produce a more precise estimate of the wrong population quantity. For example, an online poll promoted to customers who recently complained may reveal the experiences of those participants but cannot automatically estimate the views of all customers. The analyst should disclose how people entered the sample, compare respondents with known population characteristics where possible, and avoid population-wide claims unless the design and adjustments justify them. A voluntary poll can still be useful for gathering ideas or identifying issues if its purpose is described honestly.
Study instruction:
- Compare a voluntary online poll with a probability sample and explain why their results support different types of conclusions.
Key point:
- A large sample can remain unrepresentative when participation is self-selected.
- Describe voluntary-response findings as the views of respondents unless broader inference is justified.
Source links:
- AAPOR: Best Practices for Survey Research
- Office for National Statistics: Uncertainty and How We Measure It
7. Q: Why is a low survey response rate a warning sign, and does it automatically prove that the results are biased?
A: Show answer
A low response rate raises concern because people who respond may differ from those who do not, particularly on characteristics related to the question being measured. If dissatisfied customers are more likely to respond to a service survey, for example, the resulting estimate may overstate dissatisfaction. However, response rate alone does not measure the amount or direction of nonresponse bias. A low response rate can sometimes produce limited bias when respondents and nonrespondents are similar on the estimates of interest; a higher response rate can still be biased if particular groups are systematically missing. Evaluate nonresponse by comparing respondent characteristics with reliable external information, examining response patterns across contact attempts, and testing the sensitivity of estimates to reasonable adjustments. Disclose the response rate and the steps taken to assess nonresponse rather than treating it as either proof of failure or proof of quality.
Study instruction:
- Review a survey's response rate and list at least two additional analyses that could help assess whether nonrespondents differ materially from respondents.
Key point:
- Low response is a risk indicator, not a direct measurement of bias.
- Investigate who is missing and whether their absence affects the estimate.
Source links:
- AAPOR: Best Practices for Survey Research
- Office for National Statistics: Uncertainty and How We Measure It
8. Q: How should sample size be evaluated when judging whether a survey or reported relationship is credible?
A: Show answer
Sample size matters, but it must be considered together with sampling design, population variability, response patterns, measurement quality, and the intended analysis. A small, well-designed sample can sometimes estimate a narrowly defined quantity more credibly than a large convenience sample. A larger probability sample generally reduces sampling variability, all else being equal, but it cannot automatically remove coverage error, nonresponse bias, misleading questions, or inaccurate answers. For comparisons between subgroups, the effective sample size within each subgroup may be much smaller than the total. For rare outcomes, the number of relevant cases may be too small for stable estimates even when the overall survey is large. Look for the sample size, selection method, subgroup counts, weighting, uncertainty measures, and design effects. If these are unavailable, avoid treating the sample as sufficient merely because the report describes it as large.
Study instruction:
- For a survey with several subgroups, calculate how many observations actually support each reported subgroup estimate and identify where the evidence becomes thin.
Key point:
- Sample size is only one component of statistical quality.
- Representativeness and measurement can matter as much as the number of observations.
Source links:
- Office for National Statistics: Uncertainty and How We Measure It
- AAPOR: Best Practices for Survey Research
9. Q: What is the difference between sampling error and non-sampling error?
A: Show answer
Sampling error arises because a sample rather than the entire target population is observed; another valid sample could produce a different estimate. It can often be described using standard errors or confidence intervals when the sampling design supports those calculations. Non-sampling errors arise from other sources, including incomplete coverage, nonresponse, inaccurate answers, poorly worded questions, interviewer effects, data-entry mistakes, coding errors, processing defects, and unsuitable analysis. These errors can occur even in a census that attempts to collect information from every member of a population. Increasing sample size may reduce some sampling variability but does not necessarily reduce non-sampling error. A credible evaluation should therefore examine both the sampling design and the full data-production process. A narrow confidence interval does not guarantee that the estimate is free from measurement or selection problems.
Study instruction:
- Classify at least five possible survey failures as sampling or non-sampling errors and explain whether increasing the sample size would address each one.
Key point:
- Sampling error and non-sampling error have different causes and remedies.
- A large sample does not eliminate systematic measurement or coverage defects.
Source links:
- Office for National Statistics: Uncertainty and How We Measure It
10. Q: Why should a statistic be accompanied by information about its uncertainty or reliability?
A: Show answer
A sample estimate is not necessarily the exact value for the population. Measures such as standard error, confidence interval, and coefficient of variation help readers understand the sampling uncertainty associated with an estimate. They also help distinguish a meaningful change from a difference that could plausibly arise from random variation between samples. These measures do not capture every source of error: a confidence interval calculated under a sampling model does not automatically account for biased coverage, inaccurate responses, or faulty processing. Readers should therefore interpret uncertainty measures alongside the survey design and quality notes. When a report publishes only a point estimate, ask whether uncertainty information exists and whether the estimate is stable enough for the intended decision. Avoid interpreting a small difference as decisive when the uncertainty around the estimates is substantial.
Study instruction:
- Read a published survey table with confidence intervals and explain what the interval says, what it does not say, and which non-sampling errors remain possible.
Key point:
- Point estimates should be interpreted in light of uncertainty.
- Statistical uncertainty measures do not certify the entire data process as error-free.
Source links:
- Office for National Statistics: Uncertainty and How We Measure It
11. Q: What information is missing when a report gives an average without specifying the type of average?
A: Show answer
The word “average” may refer to the arithmetic mean, median, or mode, which describe data differently. The mean adds all values and divides by the number of observations, so extreme values can pull it upward or downward. The median is the middle value after sorting and is often more informative for skewed distributions such as income or property prices. The mode is the most frequently occurring value and can be useful for identifying a common category or value. Without knowing which measure is used, the reader cannot assess whether the statistic represents a typical observation. A report should define the measure, describe the distribution where relevant, and explain how missing values and outliers were handled. A headline that says “average salary” may give a materially different impression depending on whether it reports the mean or median.
Study instruction:
- Calculate the mean, median, and mode for a small skewed dataset and explain which measure best answers a stated question about a typical value.
Key point:
- “Average” is ambiguous unless the statistic is identified.
- Choose the measure that fits the distribution and the decision being made.
Source links:
- Office for National Statistics: Uncertainty and How We Measure It
12. Q: Why can a percentage be misleading when the underlying raw numbers are not disclosed?
A: Show answer
A percentage expresses a relationship to a denominator, and a small denominator can produce a dramatic percentage from very few cases. If one person in a group of three experiences an event, the percentage is 33.3%; that figure may sound substantial but represents one person. A change from two cases to four cases is a 100% increase, although the absolute increase is only two. Conversely, a large absolute increase can represent a small percentage when the starting population is large. Always request the numerator, denominator, period, population definition, and whether the percentage is weighted. Check whether the denominator includes all eligible cases or only respondents, completed transactions, or selected categories. Percentages are useful for comparison, but they should not conceal the scale of the underlying counts.
Study instruction:
- For three percentage claims, reconstruct the raw counts and explain how the impression changes when the denominator is visible.
Key point:
- Percentages need numerators and denominators for meaningful interpretation.
- Report absolute counts alongside percentages when they affect the decision.
Source links:
- Office for National Statistics: Quality Defined
13. Q: How can a missing comparison make an otherwise accurate statistic misleading?
A: Show answer
A number has limited meaning without a suitable reference point. A reported increase in deaths, complaints, sales, or incidents may sound alarming, but the reader needs to know the usual level, relevant population, comparison period, seasonal pattern, and whether the measure has changed. A weekly increase may be ordinary seasonal variation; a monthly increase may reflect an extra trading day or a different holiday date. A comparison with a single unusual year can exaggerate or conceal a longer-term pattern. Ask whether the comparison uses the same geographic area, population, definitions, collection method, and time interval. Where possible, examine several periods and a relevant benchmark rather than relying on a selected pair of observations. The absence of context does not prove the reported number is false; it means the conclusion may be stronger than the evidence allows.
Study instruction:
- Take a headline reporting an increase and identify the baseline, denominator, time period, seasonal context, and comparison group needed to interpret it.
Key point:
- A figure without a relevant comparison may create an exaggerated impression.
- Compare like with like and disclose the reference period.
Source links:
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Calculating Percent Changes
- Office for National Statistics: Quality Defined
14. Q: Why must the base period be examined when interpreting an index or percentage increase?
A: Show answer
An index expresses a value relative to a selected reference period, often set to 100. The choice of base affects the index level and the number of index points between observations, but a correctly calculated percentage change between the same two periods is independent of the index reference base. A low starting value can also make a subsequent percentage increase look especially large even when the absolute change is modest. When comparing indexed series, confirm that the base definitions are compatible or rebase them consistently before comparing levels. Do not compare index-point movements from series with different bases as though they represented the same absolute change. For a percentage change, use the earlier value as the denominator and state the exact start and end periods. The base should be visible so readers can understand what the index represents.
Study instruction:
- Recalculate a percentage change from two index values and distinguish the change in index points from the percentage change.
Key point:
- Index levels depend on their reference base; percentage changes require consistent periods and calculations.
- A low base can magnify the appearance of a percentage increase.
Source links:
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Index Numbers
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Calculating Percent Changes
15. Q: How can a change in the definition of a category make two years of data appear comparable when they are not?
A: Show answer
A statistical category is meaningful only if its operational definition remains sufficiently consistent. If the definition of a farm, unemployed person, disease case, customer, or eligible transaction changes, a later count may include cases that would not have qualified under the earlier definition. The apparent trend may therefore reflect a classification change rather than a real change in the underlying phenomenon. Before comparing periods, check the technical notes, classification rules, coverage, collection procedures, and any revisions to the series. If a consistent historical series has been recalculated using the new definition, use that series where appropriate. If no comparable series exists, disclose the break and avoid presenting the two values as a continuous trend. Definitions should be treated as part of the data, not as minor explanatory details.
Study instruction:
- Find a time series with a documented definition change and explain how the change affects year-to-year comparability.
Key point:
- A change in classification can create an apparent change in the phenomenon being counted.
- Confirm that definitions and coverage are comparable before interpreting a trend.
Source links:
- Office for National Statistics: Quality Defined
16. Q: Why should the original measurement be distinguished from the conclusion drawn from it?
A: Show answer
The data may measure something narrower or different from the concept described in the headline. A survey can measure what respondents say they do, not necessarily what they actually do. A poll may measure stated preference, not actual purchasing behavior or voting. A count of reported cases may reflect detection and reporting activity rather than the true incidence of a condition. The analyst must identify the operational measure, then decide whether it is a valid proxy for the broader concept. A conclusion becomes misleading when the proxy is silently treated as the phenomenon itself. State precisely what was observed, who reported it, how it was measured, and what further evidence is required to support a broader inference. The distinction is especially important for self-reported behavior, attitudes, intentions, administrative records, and indirect indicators.
Study instruction:
- For a survey about behavior, separate the exact question and recorded response from the broader claim made in the headline.
Key point:
- A statistic describes what was measured, not automatically the wider concept readers may infer.
- Validate whether the measure is an appropriate proxy for the conclusion.
Source links:
- AAPOR: Best Practices for Survey Research
- Office for National Statistics: Quality Defined
17. Q: How can self-reported behavior differ from actual behavior, and why does that matter?
A: Show answer
Self-reported data depend on memory, interpretation, willingness to disclose, and the social context in which a question is asked. Respondents may forget events, round quantities, misunderstand a time period, overstate socially approved behavior, or underreport sensitive conduct. A survey asking how often people exercise, bathe, donate, or comply with a rule measures their reported answers under the survey conditions. It does not directly observe each behavior. Self-report can still be useful, particularly for experiences that cannot easily be observed, but its limitations should be acknowledged. Where the distinction matters, researchers can compare responses with records, direct observation, diaries, device measurements, or other independent evidence, while considering privacy and measurement effects. Avoid converting a claim about what people reported into a claim about what they demonstrably did.
Study instruction:
- Identify a self-reported measure in a public survey and propose one independent validation method, noting its own limitations.
Key point:
- Self-reported information is evidence about reported behavior, not necessarily verified behavior.
- Match the conclusion to the method of observation.
Source links:
- AAPOR: Best Practices for Survey Research
18. Q: How can changes in reporting, diagnosis, or detection create an apparent increase in the number of cases?
A: Show answer
Recorded cases depend not only on how often an event occurs but also on the probability that it is noticed, defined, diagnosed, reported, and entered into a dataset. More complete surveillance, new diagnostic tools, broader eligibility rules, improved access to services, or increased public awareness can raise recorded counts even if the underlying incidence changes little. Conversely, reduced reporting or limited access can make a problem appear to decline. Separate the event rate from the reporting process by examining case definitions, testing volume, detection rates, reporting completeness, and independent indicators. When possible, compare rates using a consistent population denominator rather than raw counts alone. A rise in reports may represent a real increase, improved detection, or both; the data must support the distinction before a causal explanation is asserted.
Study instruction:
- Choose an example involving disease, safety incidents, or complaints and list the stages between an event occurring and its appearance in published statistics.
Key point:
- Recorded counts reflect both the underlying phenomenon and the system that records it.
- Investigate changes in detection and reporting before claiming that incidence changed.
Source links:
- Office for National Statistics: Quality Defined
- Office for National Statistics: Uncertainty and How We Measure It
19. Q: What is the difference between correlation and causation, and why is confusing them a change of subject?
A: Show answer
Correlation describes an observed association between variables; causation means that a change in one factor produces a change in another under a defensible causal explanation. Two variables may move together because one causes the other, because the direction is reversed, because a third factor influences both, because of coincidence, or because of selection and measurement effects. A chart showing that electricity use and wages both increased over time does not establish that one caused the other. To support a causal claim, examine the study design, temporal order, alternative explanations, confounding variables, mechanisms, and whether the evidence comes from a credible experiment or suitable causal method. The conclusion should use language proportionate to the design: “associated with” may be justified when “caused” is not. Correlation is informative, but it is not a causal verdict.
Study instruction:
- Find a headline that uses causal language and identify at least two alternative explanations for the reported association.
Key point:
- Association alone does not establish a causal relationship.
- Causal conclusions require a design and analysis that address alternative explanations.
Source links:
- Office for National Statistics: Uncertainty and How We Measure It
20. Q: What is a confounding variable, and how can it distort an apparent relationship?
A: Show answer
A confounding variable is an additional factor associated with both the proposed explanatory variable and the outcome, creating or distorting an observed association. For example, a seasonal factor may influence both retail activity and the outcome being studied, making a simple comparison misleading. A confounder can make a relationship appear stronger, weaker, or even reverse its direction. Analysts should identify plausible common causes before interpreting a correlation, use an appropriate design or adjustment method, and explain which variables were measured and controlled. Statistical adjustment does not automatically solve every problem: an unmeasured or poorly measured confounder may remain, and inappropriate adjustment can introduce further bias. A causal interpretation should therefore describe the assumptions behind the analysis and avoid presenting adjusted association as unquestionable proof.
Study instruction:
- Draw a simple causal diagram for a relationship you encounter and identify plausible common causes that should be investigated.
Key point:
- A third factor can create or distort an apparent relationship between two variables.
- Explain the assumptions and limitations of any adjustment used.
Source links:
- Office for National Statistics: Uncertainty and How We Measure It
21. Q: Why should an increase in the number of reported events be distinguished from an increase in the underlying rate?
A: Show answer
Counts depend on the size of the population or exposure base. If the population grows, the number of incidents may increase even when the individual risk remains unchanged or declines. A company with twice as many employees may record more workplace incidents simply because more people are exposed. Compare rates using a relevant denominator, such as incidents per employee-hour, cases per population, or failures per transaction, while ensuring the denominator is measured consistently. A rate can also conceal differences in severity or exposure, so the numerator and denominator should both be examined. For time comparisons, consider changes in the population composition, reporting coverage, and exposure duration. A raw count answers “how many”; a rate addresses “how many relative to the relevant amount of exposure.” They are different questions and should not be interchanged.
Study instruction:
- Convert a pair of event counts into rates using the relevant population or exposure data and explain whether the interpretation changes.
Key point:
- More events do not necessarily mean a higher individual or exposure-adjusted risk.
- Choose a denominator that matches the phenomenon being measured.
Source links:
- Office for National Statistics: Quality Defined
22. Q: How can a change in the timing of a holiday, season, or reporting period distort a year-to-year comparison?
A: Show answer
Activity may be concentrated around events whose dates vary between years, such as holidays, school terms, harvest periods, or billing cycles. Comparing a month containing a major holiday with a month that did not contain it may attribute a calendar effect to a change in demand or performance. Similar distortions arise when periods contain different numbers of working days, when a reporting cut-off moves, or when a campaign begins at a different point in the year. Before explaining a change, check the calendar, seasonal pattern, working-day composition, and exact start and end dates. Seasonally adjusted data may help for some series, but users must understand the adjustment method and whether the series is revised. A fair comparison uses equivalent periods or explicitly adjusts for the relevant timing difference.
Study instruction:
- Select a monthly business or economic series and identify calendar effects that could make a simple year-on-year comparison misleading.
Key point:
- Calendar differences can create apparent changes unrelated to the underlying trend.
- Compare equivalent periods and disclose any seasonal adjustment.
Source links:
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Calculating Percent Changes
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: CPI Presentation
23. Q: How can an index or percentage comparison conceal the effect of a different starting point?
A: Show answer
Percentage growth is calculated relative to the starting value, so the same absolute increase produces different percentage changes when the starting values differ. An increase from 10 to 20 is 100%, while an increase from 100 to 110 is 10%, although both rise by 10 units. This does not make either percentage incorrect; it means the percentage answers a relative-change question rather than an absolute-change question. A report can mislead when it emphasizes the largest percentage without showing the initial and final values or the scale of the underlying activity. Compare both absolute and relative changes and consider whether the base period is unusually low or high. When using index numbers, verify the reference base and use consistent calculations across periods. Avoid ranking performance solely by percentage growth when starting conditions differ substantially.
Study instruction:
- Create two examples with equal absolute increases but different starting values and explain which comparison is appropriate for a stated decision.
Key point:
- Percentage change depends on the starting value.
- Show starting values, ending values, and absolute changes alongside percentages when material.
Source links:
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Calculating Percent Changes
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Index Numbers
24. Q: How can a misleading comparison arise when total cost is compared with a price for only one component?
A: Show answer
The compared quantities must cover the same components and scope. A total cost that includes accommodation, food, staffing, utilities, administration, and security cannot be fairly compared with a hotel-room price alone. The comparison may be numerically accurate but conceptually invalid because it changes the subject. Before comparing amounts, list what is included and excluded, the time period, service level, quality, location, and relevant accounting basis. If a total must be compared with a component, explain the difference and avoid implying equivalence. This principle applies to public spending, product prices, project costs, service fees, and unit economics. A fair comparison may require separating direct and indirect costs or constructing a common cost basis. The headline should describe the actual measure, not a more attention-grabbing substitute.
Study instruction:
- Review a cost comparison and prepare a like-for-like scope checklist identifying all included and excluded components.
Key point:
- Comparisons are invalid when the measures cover different things.
- Match scope, period, quality, and cost components before drawing a conclusion.
Source links:
- Office for National Statistics: Quality Defined
25. Q: How can definitions of “first,” “largest,” “best,” or “record-breaking” be manipulated in statistical claims?
A: Show answer
Superlatives depend on the category and comparison rule selected. A business may be “first” by one measure, such as full-page advertising, while another is first by total advertising volume. A product may be the “best-selling” in a narrow category, region, channel, or time window while not leading the broader market. A claim may be literally true under a carefully chosen definition but misleading when the qualification is omitted. Ask what universe was considered, which competitors were included, what period was measured, which unit or metric was used, and whether the ranking was independently verified. Check whether the category was created for the claim or is a recognized market definition. A credible superlative states its scope and method prominently enough that readers can understand what is being ranked.
Study instruction:
- Find a marketing claim using a superlative and rewrite it to disclose the category, geography, metric, and comparison period.
Key point:
- A ranking is only meaningful within its stated universe and measurement rule.
- Hidden qualifications can turn a narrow truth into a broad misimpression.
Source links:
- Office for National Statistics: Quality Defined
26. Q: Why can an interest rate quoted as a percentage fail to describe the true cost of borrowing?
A: Show answer
A quoted percentage may use a different calculation basis, repayment pattern, time period, or fee treatment from another loan carrying the same nominal rate. If a borrower repays principal in installments, the amount outstanding declines over time. Charging a flat fee calculated on the original principal can therefore cost more than charging interest on the declining balance. A short-term charge may also look modest when stated as a percentage of principal but become very expensive when annualized. To compare loans, examine the amount received, payment schedule, outstanding balance, fees, term, compounding, and the applicable annual percentage rate or equivalent disclosure. The exact legal calculation depends on jurisdiction and product type. Never assume that two quoted rates are comparable until their definitions and cash-flow assumptions are aligned.
Study instruction:
- Compare two illustrative loans with identical headline rates but different fee and repayment methods; calculate total payments and effective cost using the applicable method.
Key point:
- A quoted rate is not a complete description of borrowing cost.
- Compare cash flows, fees, repayment timing, and standardized disclosures.
Source links:
- Consumer Financial Protection Bureau: Interest Rate vs. APR
- Consumer Financial Protection Bureau: APR and Short-Term Loan Costs
27. Q: What is the difference between a nominal interest rate, a flat-rate charge, and an annual percentage rate (APR)?
A: Show answer
A nominal interest rate is a stated rate applied according to the contract's calculation method. A flat-rate charge may calculate interest or a finance charge on the original principal throughout the term, even while the borrower repays part of that principal. APR is a standardized annualized measure intended to incorporate specified borrowing costs and timing under the applicable disclosure rules; its precise inclusions and calculation are jurisdiction-specific. These measures are not interchangeable. For a meaningful comparison, obtain the contractual cash flows, fees, compounding assumptions, repayment dates, and APR disclosure. A lower headline rate may still produce a higher overall cost if fees or repayment terms differ. APR improves comparability but should still be read with the contract, because not every cost or product feature is necessarily captured in the same way in every jurisdiction.
Study instruction:
- Read a real loan disclosure and identify the stated interest rate, APR, fees, term, and payment schedule; note the legal jurisdiction governing the calculation.
Key point:
- Nominal rates, flat charges, and APR measure different aspects of borrowing cost.
- Use standardized disclosures and the contract rather than comparing headline percentages alone.
Source links:
- Consumer Financial Protection Bureau: Interest Rate vs. APR
28. Q: How should a reader evaluate a claim based on an assumption that is not demonstrated by the data?
A: Show answer
Identify the assumptions required for the conclusion and ask whether each one has been tested or merely taken for granted. A formula can be calculated correctly while relying on an invalid premise. For example, a readability score may use word and sentence length as proxies for difficulty, but the calculation alone does not establish that those variables capture comprehension for every reader and text. A model may assume stable behavior, complete reporting, independent observations, or a constant relationship over time. These assumptions should be stated, justified, and tested through validation, sensitivity analysis, or comparison with independent evidence. If an assumption is implausible or unsupported, the resulting number should not be treated as an objective measurement of the broader concept. Distinguish the arithmetic accuracy of a calculation from the validity of the model behind it.
Study instruction:
- Choose a metric or score and list its underlying assumptions, the evidence supporting them, and the conditions under which they may fail.
Key point:
- Correct arithmetic cannot rescue an invalid assumption.
- Evaluate construct validity and model assumptions before interpreting a score.
Source links:
- Office for National Statistics: Quality Defined
29. Q: Why should a statistic be tested against common sense without allowing intuition to replace evidence?
A: Show answer
A plausibility check can reveal impossible totals, inconsistent units, implausible rates, contradictory statements, or estimates that conflict with basic population constraints. For example, an estimated number of cases cannot exceed the relevant population if each person can contribute only one case under the stated definition. However, intuition can also be wrong, particularly when a result is unfamiliar or counterintuitive. Treat common sense as a diagnostic trigger, not as the final test. Recheck the source data, definitions, denominator, arithmetic, assumptions, and independent evidence. If the result remains surprising but the method is sound, report it with the appropriate explanation and uncertainty rather than rejecting it. The purpose is to prevent numerical presentation from suspending critical judgment while preserving openness to genuine findings that challenge prior beliefs.
Study instruction:
- Perform a plausibility review of a numerical claim and distinguish an arithmetic impossibility from a result that is merely surprising.
Key point:
- Plausibility checks can reveal errors but do not replace systematic evidence.
- Investigate surprising results rather than automatically accepting or rejecting them.
Source links:
- Office for National Statistics: Quality Defined
30. Q: Why can an extremely precise figure create a false impression of accuracy?
A: Show answer
Decimal places communicate numerical precision, not necessarily measurement accuracy. A model may calculate 40.13 from assumptions and estimates that are themselves uncertain, but the two decimal places do not prove that the true value is known to the nearest cent. Accuracy concerns closeness to the quantity being measured; precision concerns the level of detail or repeatability in the reported result. A result can be precise but inaccurate when the data or model is biased. Reporting excessive digits can distract from uncertainty, weak assumptions, or an approximate underlying concept. Choose rounding that reflects the quality of the data, the measurement process, and the decision. Preserve sufficient precision for calculations internally, but present results at a defensible level and explain material uncertainty. Do not confuse a calculator's output format with the certainty of the evidence.
Study instruction:
- Review a forecast or cost estimate and decide how many digits are justified by the underlying inputs and uncertainty.
Key point:
- More decimal places do not mean more reliable knowledge.
- Match reported precision to measurement quality and practical use.
Source links:
- Office for National Statistics: Uncertainty and How We Measure It
31. Q: How can extrapolation turn a valid historical trend into an unreliable forecast?
A: Show answer
Extrapolation extends an observed pattern beyond the period in which it was measured. It can be useful when conditions remain sufficiently stable, but it becomes unreliable when technology, policy, prices, behavior, competition, demographics, or other drivers change. A percentage growth rate applied repeatedly can produce explosive projections; a linear trend can also become impossible when it ignores physical, economic, or institutional limits. The historical trend may be accurately calculated while the future projection remains a conditional scenario rather than a fact. A responsible forecast states the model, assumptions, horizon, uncertainty, and factors that could change the path. Use alternative scenarios and sensitivity analysis rather than presenting a single extrapolated line as inevitable. Monitor new observations and revise the forecast when the assumptions no longer hold.
Study instruction:
- Extend a historical series using two different models, then identify the assumptions and real-world constraints that could invalidate each forecast.
Key point:
- A past trend does not guarantee continuation into the future.
- Forecasts are conditional on assumptions and should communicate uncertainty.
Source links:
- Office for National Statistics: Uncertainty and How We Measure It
32. Q: What is the danger of projecting a percentage growth rate indefinitely from a very small starting base?
A: Show answer
A small starting value can make an early percentage increase look spectacular. Repeating that growth rate mechanically can produce a projection that rapidly exceeds realistic demand, capacity, population, or resource limits. The mathematical calculation may be correct, but the model may ignore saturation, competition, changing adoption rates, supply constraints, regulation, and other structural factors. Early-stage technologies or services often grow quickly from a small base before growth slows. Forecasts should therefore distinguish a short-term growth observation from a long-term structural assumption. Use scenario ranges, compare with relevant market or physical constraints, and explain how the growth rate is expected to change. The smaller the initial base and the longer the projection horizon, the more important it is to test the model rather than extend the percentage mechanically.
Study instruction:
- Model a small-base growth series and identify the year in which the projection becomes inconsistent with plausible capacity or market size.
Key point:
- High percentage growth from a small base can be misleading when extended indefinitely.
- Long-range forecasts need constraints, scenarios, and explicit assumptions.
Source links:
- Office for National Statistics: Uncertainty and How We Measure It
33. Q: How should a reader distinguish a forecast from an observed statistical fact?
A: Show answer
An observed statistic describes data collected for a defined period and population, subject to measurement and sampling limitations. A forecast estimates a future value using a model, assumptions, and available information. A projection may simply extend a trend under specified assumptions, while a forecast may incorporate additional explanatory variables and expert judgment; terminology varies by field, so the method should be checked. Reports should label estimates, projections, and actual observations clearly, state the date on which the forecast was prepared, and disclose assumptions and uncertainty. A forecast should not be presented in a chart in a way that makes it visually indistinguishable from historical observations. When actual results become available, compare them with the forecast and investigate the reasons for material differences. Forecast accuracy should be evaluated over time, not inferred from the confidence of the presentation.
Study instruction:
- Examine a chart that combines historical data and projections; mark the boundary between observed and forecast values and list the assumptions disclosed.
Key point:
- Historical observations and future estimates are different types of evidence.
- Label forecast periods and assumptions clearly.
Source links:
- Office for National Statistics: Uncertainty and How We Measure It
34. Q: How can a survey question or questionnaire design introduce bias before any statistical calculation occurs?
A: Show answer
Question wording, order, response options, and context can influence how respondents understand and answer a question. Leading language can signal a preferred response; a double-barrelled question can ask about two issues at once; vague terms can be interpreted differently; and an incomplete set of response options can force an inaccurate answer. Earlier questions can prime respondents to think about particular facts or emotions when answering later questions. Social desirability and interviewer behavior may also influence responses. Good survey design uses clear, neutral, single-concept questions, appropriate response options, and logical ordering. Cognitive interviews and pilot tests help reveal how respondents interpret questions before the survey is launched. A technically correct analysis cannot repair a questionnaire that systematically measures the wrong thing.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite a leading or double-barrelled question into neutral, single-concept wording and explain how the response options should be tested.
Key point:
- Measurement bias can be introduced before data collection begins.
- Pretest wording, ordering, and response options with the intended population.
Source links:
- AAPOR: Best Practices for Survey Research
35. Q: What is the purpose of pretesting and piloting a survey or statistical data-collection process?
A: Show answer
Pretesting checks whether respondents understand questions as intended and can provide the information requested. Cognitive interviews or other qualitative methods can reveal confusing wording, unexpected interpretations, recall problems, and response difficulties. A pilot test examines whether the entire operational process works, including recruitment, data collection, programming, interviewer instructions, data cleaning, coding, and analysis. These tests can identify defects before they affect a full dataset. A pilot does not guarantee that the final survey is unbiased, but it provides an opportunity to correct foreseeable problems and document remaining limitations. The survey team should record changes made after testing and ensure that the final questionnaire and analysis correspond to the version actually used in the field.
Study instruction:
- Design a short pilot plan listing the target testers, procedures to test, defects to record, and criteria for approving the final instrument.
Key point:
- Pretesting evaluates understanding; piloting evaluates the wider survey process.
- Correct defects before full deployment and retain a record of changes.
Source links:
- AAPOR: Best Practices for Survey Research
36. Q: How can missing data or omitted variables change the interpretation of a statistical result?
A: Show answer
Missing information can affect both the size and meaning of an estimate. If cases with missing values are excluded, the remaining observations may no longer represent the intended population, especially when missingness is related to the outcome or a key characteristic. An omitted variable may also be important to the interpretation, such as a population change, a policy change, a price adjustment, or a change in reporting coverage. Review the amount and pattern of missing data, the reasons for missingness, the treatment used in analysis, and the effect of reasonable alternative assumptions. Do not silently treat missing values as zero or assume that excluded cases resemble included cases. When a relevant factor cannot be measured, disclose the limitation and qualify causal or comparative conclusions accordingly.
Study instruction:
- Inspect a dataset with missing observations and describe how complete-case analysis could alter the result and what sensitivity checks might be appropriate.
Key point:
- Missing information can introduce bias, not merely reduce the number of observations.
- Explain exclusions, imputation, and unmeasured factors transparently.
Source links:
- Office for National Statistics: Uncertainty and How We Measure It
37. Q: What should be checked when two statistical reports appear to contradict one another?
A: Show answer
First determine whether the reports measure the same population, concept, geography, period, unit, and outcome definition. Then compare the source data, sampling frame, collection method, response process, weighting, exclusions, seasonal adjustment, revisions, and uncertainty. One report may count total cases while another reports a rate; one may use self-reported data while another uses administrative records; one may cover a calendar year while another covers a fiscal year. Differences can also arise from publication dates and revised datasets. Do not assume that one report must be fraudulent simply because the values differ. Identify the exact methodological difference and decide whether the results can be reconciled or whether they answer different questions. If the contradiction remains unresolved, present both results with their limitations rather than selecting the preferred figure without explanation.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a comparison matrix for two conflicting reports covering population, definition, period, source, method, denominator, and uncertainty.
Key point:
- Apparent contradictions may result from different definitions or methods.
- Reconcile the scope and methodology before deciding which result is more relevant.
Source links:
- Office for National Statistics: Quality Defined
- Office for National Statistics: Uncertainty and How We Measure It
38. Q: How can a reader evaluate whether a statistical comparison uses equivalent groups and periods?
A: Show answer
Check whether the groups are defined in the same way and whether they are comparable in relevant characteristics. A comparison between two regions may be distorted by different age structures, industry composition, income levels, exposure, or access to reporting. A comparison across time may be distorted by changed boundaries, definitions, eligibility rules, or data-collection procedures. Standardization or stratification may help when group composition differs, but the method and assumptions must be explained. Ensure that the numerator and denominator refer to the same population and period. Consider whether one group has more complete records or a different probability of being observed. A fair comparison does not require groups to be identical in every respect, but it does require the analyst to identify differences that materially affect interpretation.
Study instruction:
- Select a comparison between two locations or time periods and list the composition differences that could affect the result.
Key point:
- Comparable labels do not guarantee comparable populations or measurement processes.
- Adjust or qualify comparisons when material differences remain.
Source links:
- Office for National Statistics: Quality Defined
39. Q: What should a responsible analyst disclose when publishing a statistical conclusion?
A: Show answer
A responsible statistical report should identify the source, purpose, target population, definitions, data-collection period, sampling frame, selection method, sample size, response process, weighting, exclusions, measures, and analytical approach as relevant. It should disclose uncertainty, limitations, revisions, conflicts of interest, and important changes in methodology. Charts should label units, axes, denominators, periods, and breaks in the series. The narrative should distinguish observation, association, causal inference, and forecast. Where the data are incomplete or the method cannot support a broad claim, the report should say so plainly. Transparency allows readers to reproduce calculations where possible, assess fitness for purpose, and understand what the evidence does not establish. Disclosure is not merely a technical appendix; it is part of the integrity of the conclusion.
Study instruction:
- Draft a one-page statistical disclosure checklist suitable for a report that will be used by decision-makers.
Key point:
- Transparent methods and limitations allow independent evaluation.
- The strength of the conclusion must not exceed the strength of the evidence.
Source links:
- AAPOR: Best Practices for Survey Research
- Office for National Statistics: Quality Defined
40. Q: How can statistical claims be challenged constructively when the evidence appears weak or incomplete?
A: Show answer
Challenge the claim by asking precise, neutral questions about provenance, definitions, sample selection, raw counts, denominators, comparison periods, uncertainty, missing information, and the connection between evidence and conclusion. Request the original report or data documentation rather than accusing the author of dishonesty without evidence. Identify which part of the claim is supported, which part is uncertain, and what additional information would resolve the issue. If the statistic is being used for a decision, explain the practical risk of relying on it and propose a better measure or additional analysis. Record the response and any revised assumptions. Constructive scrutiny improves the quality of decisions while preserving the possibility that the original result is valid within a narrower scope than first presented.
Study instruction:
- Prepare five evidence-based questions to ask the author of a questionable statistic and explain what answer would resolve each concern.
Key point:
- Challenge methods and conclusions with evidence, not unsupported accusations.
- Specify what additional data or analysis would make the claim usable.
Source links:
- AAPOR: Best Practices for Survey Research
41. Q: What would you do if a management presentation reports a dramatic increase but provides only a percentage and no underlying counts?
A: Show answer
Ask for the numerator and denominator for both periods, the exact dates, the population or exposure base, and the definition of the event. Recalculate the percentage change and check whether the starting value is unusually small. Confirm whether the same locations, departments, customers, or transactions are included in both periods. Determine whether reporting completeness or data definitions changed. If the percentage is based on a small number of observations, examine the stability and uncertainty of the estimate before recommending action. Present both absolute and relative changes in the revised report. If the missing information cannot be obtained, label the claim as insufficiently documented and avoid using it as the sole basis for a material decision.
Study instruction:
- Work through an example in which the reported percentage increase is large but the absolute change is small; prepare a corrected management explanation.
Key point:
- Reconstruct the underlying counts before interpreting a dramatic percentage.
- Do not make a material decision from an unexplained rate or denominator.
Source links:
- U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics: Calculating Percent Changes
- Office for National Statistics: Uncertainty and How We Measure It
42. Q: What would you do if a vendor or lender advertises a low percentage cost that seems inconsistent with the payment schedule?
A: Show answer
Request the full repayment schedule, amount financed, amount actually disbursed, fees, timing of each payment, method used to calculate charges, and the applicable standardized disclosure. Reconstruct the cash flows and compare the total amount paid with the amount received. Determine whether the quoted rate is applied to a declining balance or the original principal and whether the term is shorter than one year. Compare products using the same APR methodology and legal jurisdiction, while checking any costs excluded from that measure. Do not rely on a verbal description such as “six percent” without the contractual basis. If the disclosure and payment schedule do not reconcile, ask the provider for a written explanation before signing or approving the transaction.
Study instruction:
- Reconcile a sample loan quotation to its cash-flow schedule and identify any difference between the headline rate and the disclosed APR.
Key point:
- Evaluate the actual cash flows and contractual disclosures, not only the advertised rate.
- Resolve inconsistencies before committing to the borrowing arrangement.
Source links:
- Consumer Financial Protection Bureau: Interest Rate vs. APR
43. Q: How can statistical quality be assessed across the full data lifecycle rather than only at the analysis stage?
A: Show answer
Statistical quality depends on a chain of activities: defining the question, identifying the target population, selecting a suitable source and sample, designing the instrument, collecting information, processing and validating records, analyzing the data, interpreting the results, and publishing the findings. A defect early in the chain can flow through every later calculation. For example, an incomplete sampling frame cannot be repaired merely by applying a sophisticated statistical model to the responses received. Quality checks should therefore occur during planning, collection, coding, cleaning, analysis, and publication. Monitor unusual response patterns, missingness, duplicate records, interviewer effects, and processing exceptions while corrections are still possible. Retain documentation of methods, data versions, transformations, and revisions so that results can be reviewed and reproduced.
Study instruction:
- Map the lifecycle of a statistical report and assign at least one preventive and one detective quality check to each major stage.
Key point:
- Quality is created and protected throughout the data lifecycle.
- Sophisticated analysis cannot automatically repair poor source data or collection methods.
Source links:
- AAPOR: Best Practices for Survey Research
- Office for National Statistics: Quality Defined
44. Q: How can data quality controls and audit trails help prevent misleading statistical reporting in an organization?
A: Show answer
Organizations should maintain clear definitions, controlled data sources, documented extraction dates, versioned datasets, reproducible transformations, validation rules, review evidence, and approval of material changes. Automated checks can identify duplicates, missing fields, inconsistent units, invalid dates, unexpected totals, and breaks in a series. Independent review should confirm that charts and narratives agree with the underlying data and that exclusions or adjustments are justified. An audit trail should show who changed the data, what changed, when it changed, and why. These controls do not guarantee that a statistic is conceptually valid; a perfectly reconciled dataset can still measure the wrong thing. Governance should therefore combine technical checks with review of definitions, sampling, assumptions, and interpretation. Statistical integrity requires both reliable processing and sound judgment.
Study instruction:
- Design a control checklist for a recurring KPI report, including source ownership, data validation, version control, independent review, and sign-off.
Key point:
- Reproducibility and traceability help detect processing and reporting errors.
- Technical accuracy must be combined with valid definitions and interpretation.
Source links:
- AAPOR: Best Practices for Survey Research
- Office for National Statistics: Quality Defined
45. Q: What is the most important practical lesson from the five questions used to evaluate statistical claims?
A: Show answer
The five questions work together as a disciplined review process. “Who says so?” examines provenance, incentives, and responsibility. “How does the source know?” examines sampling, measurement, and evidence. “What's missing?” tests whether counts, denominators, comparisons, definitions, and relevant context have been omitted. “Did somebody change the subject?” checks whether the measure actually supports the conclusion and whether comparisons are like-for-like. “Does it make sense?” tests assumptions, arithmetic, plausibility, and the limits of extrapolation. No single question is sufficient in every case, and the review should not become an automatic rejection of unfamiliar findings. The aim is to determine what can reasonably be concluded, what remains uncertain, and what further evidence is needed. Statistical literacy is an active habit of asking how a number was produced and whether its interpretation is justified.
Study instruction:
- Apply all five questions to one statistic used in a real decision and prepare a short conclusion separating verified facts, reasonable interpretations, and unresolved uncertainties.
Key point:
- Investigate the source, method, omissions, meaning, and plausibility of every important statistical claim.
- Accept only conclusions that are proportionate to the evidence and its limitations.
Source links:
- AAPOR: Best Practices for Survey Research
- Office for National Statistics: Uncertainty and How We Measure It
- Office for National Statistics: Quality Defined
